\title{Atomic Endpoints of Averaged and Guided\\
Empirical Conditional Flows}

% Authors must not appear in the submitted version; tmlr.sty ignores \author
% unless the [accepted] or [preprint] option is given. Fill in names only then.
\author{Anonymous authors}
\newcommand{\openreview}{\url{https://openreview.net/forum?id=XXXX}}

\begin{document}
\maketitle
\raggedbottom

\begin{abstract}
We study whether averaging and guidance preserve the atomic endpoints of exact
empirical conditional flows. For any positive label bandwidth, averaging the exact
fields against an arbitrary probability measure preserves almost-sure atomicity
under every absolutely continuous source. Fixed finite nonnegative classifier-free
guidance has the same property. We prove convergence of every trajectory and show
that the basins of non-atom limits have Lebesgue measure zero; at an identifying
hard label, guidance reaches the designated atom from every initial point.
These results address the intervened original flows, beyond ordinary empirical
memorisation. Numerical stress tests cover positive averaging, signed guidance,
near-duplicate atoms, and exceptional symmetric trajectories. Five existing trained
models show that the exact-field results do not automatically transfer to learned
label averages. As supporting results, a fixed-target affine example illustrates
the known obstruction to certifying endpoints from small training loss, and an
explicit risk-transfer condition yields finite-time Wasserstein and moment bounds.
Earlier moment fits and independent distributional evaluations further delimit the
use of an empirical kernel law as a reference for learned conditional samplers.
\end{abstract}

% ==========================================================================
\section{Introduction}
\label{sec:intro}

Flow matching and stochastic-interpolant models
\citep{lipman2023flow,albergo2023building,liu2023rectified,albergo2023stochastic}
are usually trained by regressing a velocity field on a linear path between a noise
sample and a data sample \citep{tong2024improving}. On a finite dataset, minimising
against the \emph{empirical} data law is known to induce memorisation
\citep{carlini2023extracting,somepalli2023diffusion,sesmat2026leaks}, and conditional
flow-matching models trained past the point of good posterior calibration give
increasingly deterministic samples that coincide with training examples
\citep{physicscfm2026}. \citet{physicscfm2026} derive point collapse for an interpolatory separable
architecture and mixture dynamics for cell-conditioned architectures. Here we study
which interventions preserve the finite support of exact empirical fields, and why
small regression loss need not certify the endpoints of a learned field. The
label-noised minimiser supplies an explicit reference for these questions; its moment
fits are subsequently tested against trained samplers.
With the training set $\D=\{(x^i,y^i)\}_{i=1}^N$ fixed and only the interpolation random,
we characterise the population minimiser of the flow-matching objective over all
square-integrable vector fields, and the endpoint law of its flow.

Two lines of work are close to this. That the empirical minimiser memorises is well
established
\citep{pidstrigach2022manifolds,gu2023memorization,kadkhodaie2024generalization,biroli2023diffusion,kamb2024creativity,buchanan2025edge,gao2024flow,bertrand2025closed,li2026memory},
and reading a kernel out of a finite-support flow is not new either
\citep{scarvelis2025closedform,chen2026scoresmoothing}.
For external labels, \citet[Theorem~6.4]{baptista2025memorization} already identify
the empirical conditional law on the subset sharing the supplied label; unique
labels select one example. \citet[Section~3.3]{physicscfm2026} also analyze
cell-conditioned flows that select training subsets. Our endpoint results for label
averaging and signed guidance remove convergence and tie-avoidance premises for the
specified exact fields, rather than claiming conditional memorisation itself is new.
\citet{smola2026support} read the
\emph{spatial} factor $\pi_0(\tfrac{x-tx^i}{1-t})$ of the index posterior as a
Nadaraya--Watson smoother on fixed atoms, with query $x/t$ and bandwidth $(1-t)/t$ for $t>0$. In our original coordinates the same spatial kernel has moving centers $tx^i$ and scale $1-t$, as in \eqref{eq:kernel-field}. We work with the other factor, $K_h(y-y^i)$. Its bandwidth is a
modelling choice and not a consequence of the clock, and it survives to $t=1$, where it
fixes the conditional endpoint law. We do not claim a new generic kernel identity.
Gaussian noise on a conditioning signal is standard practice: it is the conditioning
augmentation of \citet{ho2022cascaded}, which \citet{sadat2024cads} anneal at inference
to restore conditional diversity. \citet[Theorems~2--3]{pfrommer2025conditional} derive label-smoothness
interpolants and a Fourier convolution for a Hessian penalty. Those combinations need
not be positive averages, so our positive label-averaging result does not cover all
such regularisers. Our signed result concerns the specified classifier-free guidance
field with fixed finite nonnegative strength. Ordinary empirical recovery
\citep[Theorem~1]{li2026memory} does not by itself identify the endpoint probabilities
of these intervened fields; our atomicity statements do not identify those
probabilities either.
\citet{jain2025attraction} empirically link guidance timing to memorisation in
text-to-image diffusion. Our fixed-strength exact-field statement addresses a
different question and does not rule out their mitigation for learned fields or
time-dependent guidance schedules.

\paragraph{The closest convergence results.}
\citet[Theorem~3.7, version~3]{liu2025pde} prove almost-everywhere recovery of
compact supports for ordinary score flows. For mixed scores,
\citet[Theorem~3.18 and Remark~3.19]{liu2026geometric} give an original-flow
convergence criterion assuming that the $\omega$-limit set contains no
nonminimising critical point, and leave almost-everywhere avoidance open in that setting.
Our results concern positive label weights on a common finite support: they prove
convergence for every trajectory and null basins for non-atom limits after the
specified averaging or guidance intervention. They do not resolve mixtures with
different supports or arbitrary guidance schedules.

\paragraph{Loss and endpoint stability.}
Small regression error without sufficient stability control is already known to
give poor endpoint guarantees. \citet[Theorem~1 and Section~3]{benton2024error}
bound Wasserstein error using learned-field Lipschitz control;
\citet[Appendix~D]{beltran2026reward} construct small-teacher-error examples with
poor generated-sample rewards. Our affine example fixes a singular target and
computes the surviving covariance explicitly. We use it as an illustration,
and give a sufficient risk-transfer certificate by coupling and variation of
constants, rather than claiming a new general obstruction or stability principle.

The conditional endpoint law is a Nadaraya--Watson
mixture over the training atoms (Theorem~\ref{thm:endpoint}). It gives a reference curve
computable from the training set alone (Proposition~\ref{prop:moments}), and it shows
what moment-level evaluation misses: the law stays supported on the training atoms at
every bandwidth, so it can match selected moments without recovering the posterior
(Proposition~\ref{prop:atomicity}). Closest in spirit is \citet{buchanan2025edge}, whose
capacity-driven transition in \emph{unconditional} diffusion is the capacity analogue of
our $h\colon0\to\infty$ interpolation; neither subsumes the other.
Table~\ref{tab:relatedshort} gives the scope boundary and Table~\ref{tab:related} adds
four further lines. Our contributions are:

\begin{table}[t]
\centering\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.25\textwidth}p{0.32\textwidth}p{0.36\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
Work & Setting & Relevant conclusion or premise\\
\midrule
\citet{baptista2025memorization} & Empirical conditional law & Recovery of the subset sharing a label\\
\citet{liu2025pde}, v3 & Ordinary score flow, compact support & Almost-everywhere convergence into the support\\
\citet{liu2026geometric} & Mixed scores, potentially different supports & Original-flow convergence assumes no nonminimising critical limit points\\
This paper & Positive label averages and fixed CFG on a common finite support & Every trajectory converges; non-atom basins are null\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\caption{Convergence scope of the closest results. Our intervention results require
positive label bandwidth, except for the identifying hard-label guidance case.
They do not identify endpoint probabilities. Table~\ref{tab:related} gives further context.}
\label{tab:relatedshort}
\end{table}

\begin{enumerate}\itemsep1pt
\item \textbf{Atomicity after field interventions.} Positive label averaging and
  fixed finite nonnegative classifier-free guidance preserve atomic endpoints
  almost surely (Propositions~\ref{prop:filtered} and~\ref{prop:cfgatom}). The proofs
  establish trajectory convergence and exclude non-atom basins in Lebesgue measure;
  hard-label guidance reaches the identified atom from every initial point.
\item \textbf{A reference and its certification limits.} The empirical conditional
  kernel law (Theorem~\ref{thm:endpoint}) makes moments and an atomic approximation
  floor explicit. A fixed-target example illustrates the known limitation of small
  teacher loss (Proposition~\ref{prop:loss-no-certificate}); a sufficient
  risk-transfer condition yields a finite-time certificate
  (Theorem~\ref{thm:risktransfer}).
\item \textbf{Tests of the exact-to-learned connection.} Paired exact-field stress
  tests and tie controls check the intervention predictions, while five trained
  models test learned label averages (Section~\ref{sec:intervention-tests}).
  Earlier moment fits agree at $48/48$ synthetic and $31/36$ positive-bandwidth
  CIFAR-10 checkpoints, but independent distributional evaluation does not certify
  equality with the reference (Section~\ref{sec:independent}). The bandwidth audit
  therefore remains a conditional-moment diagnostic (Section~\ref{sec:heff}).
\end{enumerate}

\begin{figure}[t]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.88\textwidth]{fig_flow_portrait.png}
\caption{The mechanism in trajectory space, on one condition of the linear-Gaussian instance
($d{=}2$, $N{=}200$): $160$ draws of $x_0$, their paths and endpoints. (a,~b) One
\emph{trained} network at two checkpoints. At $1000$ iterations its samples are
dispersed; at $200000$, $96\%$ of its paths reach the atom identified by the label
(Proposition~\ref{prop:collapse}). (c) The population optimum at the $h^\star=0.70$ with
$\tr\Covh=\tr\Sigmapost$ at this condition (averaged over conditions the match is near

do not help.}
\label{fig:mechanism}
\end{figure}

At an identifying observed label, hard conditioning selects one training atom.
Positive label noise gives a kernel-weighted empirical reference without leaving
those atoms (Figure~\ref{fig:mechanism}). Fitted bandwidths summarize selected
conditional moments of trained networks; fresh-condition distributional tests
determine the limits of that description.

\paragraph{What to verify.} The intervention statements are
Propositions~\ref{prop:filtered} and~\ref{prop:cfgatom}, with full proofs in
Appendices~\ref{apx:pf-filtered} and~\ref{apx:pf-cfgatom}. Their finite-time tests
and learned-field comparison are in Section~\ref{sec:intervention-tests}, with
protocol and failed solver attempts in Appendix~\ref{app:intervention-protocol}.
The reference endpoint law is Theorem~\ref{thm:endpoint}; the moment audit and
its independent distributional check are in Sections~\ref{sec:heff}
and~\ref{sec:independent}.

% ==========================================================================
\section{Setup and notation}
\label{sec:setup}

We first define the empirical flow-matching objective, then use the endpoint law of its population minimiser as a reference for trained models. For vectors, $|\cdot|$ and $\|\cdot\|$ denote the Euclidean norm. For a random vector, $\Var$ denotes the scalar total variance, $\tr\Cov$.

\paragraph{Sampling law.} Fix a training set~$\D=\{(x^i,y^i)\}_{i=1}^N$ with $x^i\in\R^d$ and $y^i\in\R^k$. Its empirical joint law is $\rhohat_{XY}=N^{-1}\sum_i\delta_{(x^i,y^i)}$, with marginal $\rhohat_X=N^{-1}\sum_i\delta_{x^i}$. Draw $I\sim\mathrm{Unif}\{1,\dots,N\}$, $X_0\sim\pi_0=\N(0,I_d)$ and $t\sim\mathrm{Unif}(0,1)$ independently. The selected endpoint is $X_1=x^I$, the label is $Y=y^I$, and the linear interpolant is $X_t=(1-t)X_0+tX_1$, with velocity target $U:=\dot X_t=X_1-X_0$.

\paragraph{Conditioning and objective.} At bandwidth~$h>0$, draw $\varepsilon\sim\N(0,I_k)$ independently of $(I,X_0,t)$ and replace the label by $\widetilde Y=Y+h\varepsilon$. The corresponding label kernel is $K_h(u)=(2\pi h^2)^{-k/2}\exp(-|u|^2/2h^2)$. We minimise
\begin{equation}
L_h(v)=\E\big[\,|U-v(X_t,t,\widetilde Y)|^2\,\big]\,,
\label{eq:objective}
\end{equation}
over square-integrable vector fields. Hard conditioning is the case $h=0$, with $\widetilde Y=Y$ and objective $L_0$. The unconditional objective omits the label argument of~$v$. We write $p_1^{(h)}(\cdot\mid y)$ for the endpoint law of the minimiser's flow from~$\pi_0$ at a fixed condition~$y$, and $p_1^{\mathrm{unc}}$ for the unconditional endpoint law. Endpoint laws are understood as weak limits as $t\uparrow1$, as specified in Section~\ref{sec:theory}.
At $h=0$, the empirical objective constrains the field only at observed labels.
Its conditional law at a new label is not specified by that objective
\citep[Theorem~6.4]{baptista2025memorization}. Evaluation at new labels therefore
tests the learned extension, whereas hard-conditioning endpoint theorems concern
identifying training labels. At $h>0$, the Gaussian label density is positive and the
explicit reference is defined at every query label.

\begin{assumption}[Standing]
\label{as:standing}
\emph{(i)} $\D$ is fixed: the expectation in \eqref{eq:objective} is over
$(I,X_0,t,\varepsilon)$ only. \emph{(ii)} The interpolant is the deterministic linear one
above, unless a noisy one is introduced explicitly. \emph{(iii)} Labels identify:
$y^i\neq y^j$ for $i\neq j$, so $Y=y^i$ determines $I=i$ at $h=0$.
\end{assumption}

\paragraph{Reference moments.} The empirical marginal has mean $\bar x=N^{-1}\sum_i x^i$ and covariance $\Xhat=N^{-1}\sum_i(x^i-\bar x)(x^i-\bar x)^\top$. These dataset moments are distinct from the moments of generated samples, estimated in Section~\ref{sec:heff}. In the linear-Gaussian experiment, $y=Ax+\eta$ with $\eta\sim\N(0,\sigma_{\mathrm{obs}}^2I_k)$; the analytic Gaussian posterior has covariance~$\Sigmapost$.

% ==========================================================================
\section{Theory}
\label{sec:theory}

Everything in this section holds under Assumption~\ref{as:standing} and concerns the
population minimiser over $L^2$-measurable vector fields for the empirical data law, with
$\D$ fixed. Propositions~\ref{prop:expansion} and~\ref{prop:nwrate} are the exception:
they relax (i) to a random design under Assumption~\ref{as:design} and let $N\to\infty$.
Proofs are in Appendix~\ref{app:proofs}, three conventions they rely on in
Appendix~\ref{app:deferred}. The
projection identity below is the standard $L^2$ decomposition behind flow-matching
objectives \citep{lipman2023flow,albergo2023building,liu2023rectified}.

\begin{proposition}[$L^2$ projection]
\label{prop:projection}
For every square-integrable $v$,
$L_h(v)=\E[\Var(U\mid X_t,t,\widetilde Y)]+\E\,|v-\E[U\mid X_t,t,\widetilde Y]|^2$,
so the a.e.-unique minimiser is $\vstar(x,t,y)=\E[U\mid X_t=x,t,\widetilde Y=y]$ with
$\inf_v L_h=\E[\Var(U\mid X_t,t,\widetilde Y)]$. \proofin{apx:pf-projection}
\end{proposition}

Lemmas~\ref{lem:cov} and~\ref{lem:wellposed} (appendix) justify the conditional
expectations and show the flows are well posed with no further assumption: for $t<1$ the
map $X_0\mapsto X_t$ is an invertible affine change of variables with positive conditional
density, and the explicit population fields below are locally Lipschitz on
$\R^d\times[0,1)$, so their flows are well
posed there. Each endpoint law $p_1$ is the weak limit of the marginals at fixed $y$,
shown to exist case by case (appendix, ``Three conventions'').

\subsection{Hard conditioning: exact collapse}
\label{sec:theory-hard}

If the conditioning event pins down a single index, the conditional expectation of
Proposition~\ref{prop:projection} collapses:

\begin{proposition}[Exact conditional collapse; $h=0$]
\label{prop:collapse}
Let $h=0$. Then
(a) $v_0^\star(x,t,y^i)=(x^i-x)/(1-t)$ for $t<1$ $(\star)$;
(b) $\inf_v L_0(v)=0$;
(c) the ODE $\dot x_t=(x^i-x_t)/(1-t)$ has the explicit solution
$x_t=(1-t)x_0+tx^i$, so $x_t\to x^i$ for every $x_0$; and
(d) $p_1^{(0)}(\cdot\mid y^i)=\delta_{x^i}$, hence
$\Cov[p_1^{(0)}(\cdot\mid y^i)]=0$. \proofin{apx:pf-collapse}
\end{proposition}
If labels are not distinct, collapse is onto the label's empirical conditional
support $|\mathcal I_y|^{-1}\sum_{i\in\mathcal I_y}\delta_{x^i}$ (Corollary~\ref{cor:repeated},
appendix), a single atom when the label is an identifier ($|\mathcal I_y|=1$). The unconditional
minimiser, by contrast, mixes all $N$ atoms and reaches $\rhohat_X$
(Proposition~\ref{prop:uncond}, appendix).

\begin{corollary}[The correct conditional/unconditional distinction]
\label{cor:distinction}
Both exact population flows are supported on the training set:
$p_1^{(0)}(\cdot\mid y^i)=\delta_{x^i}$ and
$p_1^{\mathrm{unc}}=\frac1N\sum_i\delta_{x^i}$. The difference is between
single-example and full-empirical-measure memorisation, not between memorising and not,
and it shows in the conditional second moment:
\begin{equation}
\Cov[p_1^{(0)}(\cdot\mid y^i)]=0
\qquad\text{vs.}\qquad
\Cov[p_1^{\mathrm{unc}}]=\Xhat.
\label{eq:distinction}
\end{equation} \proofin{apx:pf-distinction}
\end{corollary}

The often-cited observation $\tr\Cov\approx\tr\Xhat$ for the unconditional baseline is
\eqref{eq:distinction}: it is evidence of full-empirical-measure memorisation, not of its
absence. Two empirical findings read the same way. Verbatim replication is common with
text conditioning, rare without it, and reduced by randomising captions
\citep{somepalli2023copying}; and uninformative random labels sharply increase
memorisation \citep{gu2023memorization}. Proposition~\ref{prop:collapse} accounts for the
label's part in both: informativeness is irrelevant, and what matters is that the label
identifies the example (Appendix~\ref{app:adv} tests this directly).

\subsection{Label smoothing is kernel regression at the optimum}
\label{sec:theory-kernel}

The natural fix for a label that identifies its example too precisely is to blur it,
replacing $y^i$ by $\widetilde Y=y^i+h\varepsilon$. The population minimiser then becomes
the velocity field of a Nadaraya--Watson regression over the atoms~\citep{nadaraya1964estimating,watson1964smooth}.

\begin{proposition}[Kernel-weighted population minimiser]
\label{prop:kernel-field}
For $h>0$, $t<1$,
\begin{equation}
\vstar(x,t,y)=\sum_i w_i^{(h)}(x,t,y)\,\frac{x^i-x}{1-t},\quad
w_i^{(h)}=\frac{\pi_0\!\big(\tfrac{x-tx^i}{1-t}\big)K_h(y-y^i)}
{\sum_j\pi_0\!\big(\tfrac{x-tx^j}{1-t}\big)K_h(y-y^j)}.
\label{eq:kernel-field}
\end{equation} \proofin{apx:pf-kernel-field}
\end{proposition}
This factorises the index posterior into a spatial source-consistency term and a label
kernel. With $p_i^{(h)}(y):=K_h(y-y^i)/\sum_j K_h(y-y^j)$ the normalised label weights,
Lemma~\ref{lem:mixture-coupling} identifies $\vstar$ with the conditional velocity of the
coupling $I\sim p^{(h)}(y)$, $X_1=x^I$, which gives the endpoint law.

\begin{theorem}[Endpoint law under label smoothing]
\label{thm:endpoint}
For every $h>0$ and query $y$,
\begin{equation}
p_1^{(h)}(\cdot\mid y)=\sum_{i=1}^N p_i^{(h)}(y)\,\delta_{x^i}.
\label{eq:endpoint}
\end{equation} \proofin{apx:pf-endpoint}
\end{theorem}
\emph{Proof sketch.} By Lemma~\ref{lem:mixture-coupling}, $\vstar$ is the conditional velocity of the
coupling $I\sim p^{(h)}(y)$, $X_1=x^I$. Its marginals solve the flow's continuity equation, so they
coincide with the flow's (Lemma~\ref{lem:wellposed}), and they tend to $\mathrm{Law}(x^I)$ since
$X_t\to x^I$ almost surely.
\begin{corollary}[Zero bandwidth at fixed $N$]
\label{cor:zerobw}
If $y$ has a unique nearest label $i^\star=\arg\min_i|y-y^i|$, then
$p_i^{(h)}(y)\to\mathbf 1\{i=i^\star\}$ as $h\to0$, so
$p_1^{(h)}(\cdot\mid y)\Rightarrow\delta_{x^{i^\star}}$; at $y=y^i$ this
recovers Proposition~\ref{prop:collapse}(d). \proofin{apx:pf-zerobw}
\end{corollary}
\begin{corollary}[Infinite bandwidth at fixed $N$]
\label{cor:infbw}
$p_i^{(h)}(y)\to1/N$ for every $i$ as $h\to\infty$, so
$p_1^{(h)}(\cdot\mid y)\Rightarrow p_1^{\mathrm{unc}}$. \proofin{apx:pf-infbw}
\end{corollary}
So $h$ interpolates continuously between the two hard regimes of
Section~\ref{sec:theory-hard}.

\begin{proposition}[Exact finite-$N$ moments]
\label{prop:moments}
$\bar x_h(y)=\sum_i p_i^{(h)}(y)\,x^i$ and
$\Covh(y)=\sum_i p_i^{(h)}(y)\,(x^i-\bar x_h(y))(x^i-\bar x_h(y))^\top$, both computable
from the training set for any $h>0$; they are the reference for generated moments.
$\tr\Covh(y)$ need not be monotone in $h$. \proofin{apx:pf-moments}
\end{proposition}

These are the moments of the law in Theorem~\ref{thm:endpoint}, and every experiment below
is measured against them. For labels that are close but not equal,
Proposition~\ref{prop:nearduplicate} shows that a usable bandwidth exists when
clusters are tight relative to their separation, and that inside that window
$n_{\mathrm{eff}}=1/\sum_i(p_i^{(h)})^2$ counts the cluster and not the dataset: near-duplicate labels buy
effective sample size, not continuity.

\begin{proposition}[Label smoothing never leaves the training set]
\label{prop:atomicity}
For every $h>0$, and for $h=0$ at $y=y^i$, $\operatorname{supp}(p_1^{(h)}(\cdot\mid y))
\subseteq\{x^1,\dots,x^N\}$. Consequently, if the true posterior $p(\cdot\mid y)$ puts no
mass on $\{x^i\}$,
\begin{equation}
W_2^2\big(p_1^{(h)}(\cdot\mid y),\,p(\cdot\mid y)\big)\ \ge\
\int\operatorname{dist}\big(x,\{x^i\}\big)^2\,p(x\mid y)\,dx\ >\ 0,
\label{eq:w2floor}
\end{equation}
and this lower bound does not depend on $h$. \proofin{apx:pf-atomicity}
\end{proposition}
\emph{Proof sketch.} Any coupling of an atomic law with $p(\cdot\mid y)$ moves each posterior draw at
least to its nearest atom, and that bound involves the atoms alone, not $h$.
This is the central caveat on any ``variance is restored at $h\approx0.1$'' claim:
label smoothing redistributes weight over training atoms and never generates new
samples, so matching $\tr\Covh$ matches a second moment and not the posterior. A remedy
needs $h\to0$ and $N\to\infty$ together (Propositions~\ref{prop:expansion}
and~\ref{prop:nwrate}, for the first two moments), not $h$ alone.
Whether the floor matters depends on its size. For a target on $\R^d$ with a nonzero absolutely continuous part and a finite $(2+\eta)$ moment for some $\eta>0$, Zador's theorem~\citep[Ch.~6]{graf2000quantization} gives the fixed-$d$ asymptotic rate $N^{-1/d}$ for optimal $N$-point $W_2$ approximation, with a distribution-dependent constant. A fixed training codebook cannot improve on the optimal one. This rate alone gives neither a finite-$N$ error nor its ratio to the one-atom error. Appendix~\ref{app:deferred} measures random-codebook errors in the tested Gaussian settings. For CIFAR-10 we make no quantitative extrapolation from ambient or intrinsic dimension; the floor constrains the exact reference rather than arbitrary learned outputs.

\subsection{Atomic endpoints after field interventions}
\label{sec:intervention-atomicity}

The kernel endpoint law describes a single exact field. Averaging such fields
does not generally commute with averaging their priors or endpoint laws.
Nevertheless, positive label averaging preserves atomic endpoints.

\begin{proposition}[Label resolution reweights the atoms and never moves them]
\label{prop:filtered}
Let $h>0$, $\varphi$ a probability measure on $\R^k$ and
$v_\varphi(x,t,y)=\int\vstar(x,t,y-u)\,\varphi(du)$. (a) $v_\varphi=\sum_i\tilde
w_i(x,t,y)(x^i-x)/(1-t)$ with $\tilde w_i\ge0$, $\sum_i\tilde w_i=1$, and $\tilde
w_i(x,0,y)=q_i(y):=\int p_i^{(h)}(y-u)\,\varphi(du)$. (b) Every trajectory converges. If
$N(z)=\{x^j:|z-x^j|=\min_\ell|z-x^\ell|\}$, its limit satisfies
$z\in\operatorname{conv}N(z)$. For any absolutely continuous source, including
$\pi_0$, the limit is a training atom almost surely. Thus the endpoint law is
$\sum_i\tilde q_i(y)\delta_{x^i}$ for some probability weights $\tilde q(y)$.
\proofin{apx:pf-filtered}
\end{proposition}

No moment or bounded-support condition is imposed on the averaging measure
$\varphi$. The endpoint weights $\tilde q$ are not identified by this statement.
The positive-bandwidth assumption ensures that every component prior gives
positive weight to the same finite set of atoms.

For guidance, let $v_{\mathrm{unc}}^\star$ be the exact unconditional field for
the same training atoms and define
$v_w=(1+w)\vstar-wv_{\mathrm{unc}}^\star$.
Its coefficients can be negative, so the convex-average argument does not apply.

\begin{proposition}[Guidance lands on a training atom]
\label{prop:cfgatom}
Assume $x^1,\dots,x^N$ are distinct and fix a finite $w\ge0$.
The exact guided field has a unique trajectory on $[0,1)$ from every finite
initial point. Then:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*),leftmargin=2em]
\item if $h>0$, every trajectory converges to a point
  $z\in\operatorname{aff}N(z)$, where $N(z)$ is its set of nearest atoms.
  For an absolutely continuous source, the endpoint is a training atom almost surely;
\item if $h=0$ at an identifying label $y^i$, every trajectory converges to $x^i$,
  without an exceptional set of initial points.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}
\proofin{apx:pf-cfgatom}

For positive bandwidth, both proofs first use a time-dependent log-partition energy to establish
convergence to a finite set of candidate limits. For positive averages, covariance
positivity gives volume expansion near non-atom candidates. For guidance, a signed
covariance identity gives the required expansion integrated over late unit-time
intervals. Change of variables and Fatou's lemma then force the corresponding
basins to have zero Lebesgue measure. These are statements about the original
non-autonomous trajectories, including their exceptional initial points.
Neither proposition extends to arbitrary signed combinations or time-dependent
guidance strengths. Section~\ref{sec:intervention-tests} tests their finite-time
predictions and illustrates a non-atom symmetric exceptional trajectory.

\subsection{Where noise helps, and what the theory does not cover}
\label{sec:interventions}

Proposition~\ref{prop:atomicity} rules out any remedy that leaves the endpoint on the
training atoms. Appendix~\ref{app:interventions} classifies the places to inject
randomness by what they do to the endpoint law: interpolant noise is inert at every noise
level (Proposition~\ref{prop:prop17}), classifier-free guidance \citep{ho2022classifier} keeps the atoms
(Propositions~\ref{prop:cfg} and~\ref{prop:cfgatom}), label noise reweights them
(Theorem~\ref{thm:endpoint}), and among the three noise interventions considered,
only endpoint noise moves the support, at
$+\rho^2I_d$ of variance (Proposition~\ref{prop:tgtnoise}). The same appendix gives the
$N\to\infty$ expansion of $\Covh$ (Proposition~\ref{prop:expansion}) and how a velocity
error survives the collapsing flow (Proposition~\ref{prop:survival}). All of this
describes the empirical minimiser, not how fast training reaches it;
Section~\ref{sec:heff} measures how far a trained model has gone.

% ==========================================================================
\section{Experiments}
\label{sec:experiments}

\paragraph{Setup.} Unless noted, the linear-Gaussian experiment (EXP-1 in the appendix) uses the problem
$d{=}2,\,k{=}1,\,N{=}200,\,\sigma_{\mathrm{obs}}{=}0.1$ with a 4-layer width-128 MLP,
Adam, RK4 integration stopping at $t{=}1{-}10^{-3}$, and $M{=}1000$ samples at 20
training and 8 held-out conditions; full settings are in Appendix~\ref{app:exp}. We do
not early-stop, since overtraining is what we study. Sweeps use five seeds, and the seed
controls both the problem instance and the training randomness, so every error bar (a
population standard deviation) mixes the two; Appendix~\ref{sec:seedsplit}
compares two one-factor slices, whose descriptive quadrature share for instance variation is $78\%$; it does not estimate their interactions.

\subsection{Synthetic instances: the exact-theory quantities}
\label{sec:exp1}

\paragraph{Collapse under hard conditioning.} Table~\ref{tab:exp1} and Figure~\ref{fig:p1p4} (appendix)
report the four exact-theory observables. Past the good-calibration phase the conditional
generated variance collapses from $\approx\Sigmapost$ toward $0$, the velocity field
approaches $(x^i-x)/(1-t)$, the generated mean moves from $\mu_{\mathrm{post}}$
toward $x^i$, and the unconditional baseline keeps data-scale variance. The
variance tracks the true posterior up to $\sim\!10^4$ iterations, a moment-level
agreement consistent with the benefit of early stopping \citep{physicscfm2026}.
This alone does not establish a correct Bayesian sampler. The variance then collapses,
stalling near $0.40$ at $2\times10^5$. That residual is not a failure of
Proposition~\ref{prop:collapse}: all four observables keep descending with the loss,
and Section~\ref{sec:heff} reads it as an effective bandwidth still above zero. Three measurements sharpen this (Appendix~\ref{app:exp4}). The
memorisation ratio of \citet{yoon2023memorization} reaches $0.650\pm0.033$ conditionally
against $0.389\pm0.032$ unconditionally from a common $\approx0.11$ baseline, so both
flows memorise, as Corollary~\ref{cor:distinction} says, and the conditional one more
strongly. At held-out conditions the variance collapses as at training labels while the
distance to $\mu_{\mathrm{post}}(y)$ grows from $0.12$ to $0.87$: concentration with
increasing posterior-mean error. Nearest-label atomicity is the exact-reference
prediction (Corollary~\ref{cor:zerobw}); these diagnostics do not identify the learned limit's support. And permuting $Y$ against $X$ leaves the
collapse intact while that distance grows, so the law follows the arbitrary
label-to-example assignment and not any relation between $x$ and $y$.

\label{sec:p7}%
\paragraph{On synthetic MLPs, label smoothing tracks the kernel reference.} The comparison
depends on using the right reference,
$\Covh$ and not $\Sigmapost$. Over 20 conditions ($M{=}1000$, 5 seeds) the generated
variance tracks $\Covh$: ratio-to-kernel $=1.002\pm0.050$, $0.994\pm0.039$ and
$1.011\pm0.022$ at $h=0.05,0.1,0.5$ (Figure~\ref{fig:p7summary},
Table~\ref{tab:p7kernel}), with the field agreeing too. At $h{=}0.01$
($n_{\mathrm{eff}}\approx3.5$) the reference is near-degenerate and the ratio unstable,
$1.308\pm0.376$. That $h\approx0.1$ ``perfectly
restores'' the posterior variance is a misreading: $\tr\Cov$ crosses $\Sigmapost$ there
only because $\Sigmapost\approx\Covh|_{h=0.1}$ for this problem.

\paragraph{What matching the variance does not show.} Three consequences follow
(Appendices~\ref{sec:aux} and~\ref{app:tgt}). The law stays atomic even at the
best-matching $h$ ($n_{\mathrm{eff}}\approx26/200$), so matching variance is not
recovering the posterior. The floor \eqref{eq:w2floor} is small here, $F=0.0353$ or
$3.5\%$ of $\tr\Sigmapost$, while the population-optimal atomic law already scores below
the trained model in MMD, so the MMD curves do not measure atomicity. And endpoint
smoothing removes the obstruction without improving the fit: the best cell of the
$(h,\rho)$ sweep is $h{=}0.5,\rho{=}0$ in both metrics. It helps only where the atoms are
sparse enough for the floor to dominate, and the gain grows with the floor at fixed $d$
but not across $d$ in these configurations (Table~\ref{tab:floor}), illustrating the variance cost of isotropic smoothing.

\paragraph{Interpolant noise.} With the corrected target the measured variance is $0.367\pm0.146$, similar to the old-target runs (Table~\ref{tab:p7i}). Training uses the unweighted squared loss with times clamped to $[10^{-4},1-10^{-4}]$, whereas \eqref{eq:invariance} concerns the exact field of the time-weighted theoretical objective.

\label{sec:exp23}%
\paragraph{Beyond the Gaussian case.} \emph{Gaussian mixture (EXP-2):} with a bimodal
analytic posterior, mode coverage is $1.0$ up to $\sim\!10^4$ iterations, then falls to
$0.72$ while the MMD grows to $\sim0.24$ and the share of samples nearest the
$x^i$-containing mode rises from $\approx0.5$ to $1.000$
(Figs.~\ref{fig:exp2curves},~\ref{fig:exp23qual}). \emph{MNIST inpainting (EXP-3,
$N{=}500$, three seeds):} the inpainted-region variance falls from $0.137\pm0.021$ to
$(1.17\pm0.12)\times10^{-3}$ by 15000 iterations and the nearest-training-image distance
to $(0.77\pm0.26)\times10^{-3}$ (Fig.~\ref{fig:exp3prog}); one small-U-Net CIFAR-10 run
falls $0.142\to0.0087$.

\FloatBarrier

\subsection{Averaging, guidance, and exceptional ties}
\label{sec:intervention-tests}

\paragraph{Exact fields.} Five independent geometry seeds, $d\in\{2,8\}$,
$N=12$, random and near-duplicate atoms, and $h\in\{0.3,1\}$ give 200 paired
cells across single-field, CFG ($w=3,12$), label-average, and prior-average arms.
With $M=128$ common sources and terminal gap $10^{-6}$, the largest cellwise
95th percentile of nearest-atom distance is $6.11937\times10^{-6}$.
No measured sample exceeds the prespecified threshold of $1\%$ of minimum atom
separation. Doubling log-time RK4 resolution leaves atom assignments unchanged;
the largest cellwise mean paired displacement is $7.54797\times10^{-13}$.
These are finite-time consistency checks, not a numerical proof of atomicity.
Field and prior averages differ in paired assignments by up to $31.25\%$ across
the 40 geometry--bandwidth comparisons, so we keep these interventions distinct.

\paragraph{Exceptional sources and assumption boundary.}
For atoms $\{-1,1\}$ and equally averaged fields at labels $\{-1,1\}$, symmetry
keeps the source $x_0=0$ at the non-atom midpoint. Nearby nonzero sources can
remain near this tie until very small terminal gaps. Averaging the two identifying
hard-label fields also gives a midpoint endpoint, illustrating why $h>0$ matters
for Proposition~\ref{prop:filtered}. A separate refinement reaches gap $10^{-18}$,
but four of six adaptive near-tie integrations fail at small bandwidths. We retain
these failures and do not claim certified numerical convergence in those cases
(Appendix~\ref{app:intervention-protocol}).

\paragraph{Learned label averages.}
We reuse five $h=0.1$, 200k-step MLP checkpoints, averaging predictions at
$y-r,y,y+r$, with $r$ scaled by training-label standard deviation.
Each seed has two training and two fresh queries and 128 paired sources.
The comparison is against the exact average of the same three fields at
$T=0.999$. Table~\ref{tab:learned-averages} reports means of seed means.
Wider averaging increases the mean discrepancy, although not on every seed.
This pilot does not transfer exact atomicity to a network or measure posterior
quality. Its biased finite-sample energy statistic has no null subtraction or
equivalence threshold. Figure~\ref{fig:intervention-validation} summarizes all
three diagnostics; the independent replication unit is the seed.

\begin{table}[t]
\centering\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}rrrr@{}}
\toprule
$r/\operatorname{sd}(y)$ & Training energy & Fresh energy & Fresh seed range\\
\midrule
0 & 0.005627 & 0.007227 & 0.001876--0.009327\\
0.5 & 0.011200 & 0.011054 & 0.001887--0.018859\\
1.5 & 0.289771 & 0.143176 & 0.001758--0.544995\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\caption{Learned versus exact label averages across five existing problem seeds.
Energy is normalized by the square root of analytic posterior trace. Conditions
and samples are nested within seeds; these are descriptive values, not hypothesis tests.}
\label{tab:learned-averages}
\end{table}

\begin{figure}[t]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.98\textwidth]{fig_intervention_validation.pdf}

for $d=2$, random atoms, $h=1$, averaged across five geometry seeds.
Middle: sources at offset $10^{-8}$ from a symmetric tie, under field averaging;
delayed departure at finite cutoff does not imply a non-atom limiting endpoint.
Right: learned versus exact field averages from five existing checkpoints. The

Appendix~\ref{app:intervention-protocol} records solver limitations.}

\FloatBarrier

\subsection{Independent evaluation of the fitted reference}
\label{sec:independent}

Moment agreement is weaker than agreement of probability laws. We train ten further
MLPs on five new problem seeds (1001--1005), paired across $h\in\{0,0.1\}$, using the
same synthetic setup and the common final checkpoint at $200000$ iterations.
For each model, 20 training conditions fit a single reference scale, and 64 fresh
conditions evaluate it with $M=512$ samples. The independent replication unit is the
problem seed: the 64 conditions are nested within a seed. Appendix~\ref{app:newvalidation}
specifies the finite-time reference, discrepancy and solver screen.

\begin{table}[t]
\centering\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}lrr@{}}
\toprule
Problem seed & $h=0$ & $h=0.1$\\
\midrule
1001 & 0.430079 & 0.021337\\
1002 & 0.126044 & 0.008118\\
1003 & 0.282378 & 0.020624\\
1004 & 4.084575 & 0.004253\\
1005 & 0.446561 & 0.002055\\
\midrule
Mean over seeds & 1.073927 & 0.011277\\
Median over seeds & 0.430079 & 0.008118\\
Gaussian best among four fitted families & $0/5$ & $5/5$\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\caption{Fresh-condition discrepancy from the fitted Gaussian-label reference.
Each entry is a seed mean of normalized energy distance minus its matched
reference--reference mean; smaller means closer to that reference, rather than
necessarily closer to the true posterior. The hard-conditioned mean retains the
extreme extrapolation condition. The four families share atoms and are fitted using
training-condition variances, without optimizing held-out energy distance.}
\label{tab:independent}
\end{table}

Table~\ref{tab:independent} shows smaller discrepancy with smoothing on every paired
seed. The Gaussian reference is best among the four protocol-fitted families on all
five smoothed seeds and none of the hard-conditioned seeds. This supports its use as
a more accurate reference under explicit label noise, but does not establish equality
of laws. Of 320 fresh conditions per bandwidth, 317 at $h=0$ and 164 at $h=0.1$
exceed a diagnostic reference--reference 95th percentile. Each percentile uses only
16 reference pairs, so these counts are descriptive rather than calibrated test
rejections. At $h=0$, this is a test of the learned extension to new labels, not a
contradiction of hard-label collapse at training labels.

\paragraph{An extrapolation outlier.} One condition of seed 1004 lies beyond the
training-label range and has two extreme endpoints among 512 draws. It fails the
initial 200-to-400-step practical solver screen; all other 639 model--condition pairs
pass. Retaining it gives the reported hard-conditioned mean. Subsequent RK4 refinement
and independent adaptive integration reproduce the extreme trajectories
(Appendix~\ref{app:newvalidation}). This numerical diagnosis supports a learned-field
extrapolation failure, while the original screen correctly warns against interpreting
the coarse-step value as solver-independent.

\subsection{Realistic capacity: CIFAR-10 with a 35.75M-parameter DDPM U-Net}
\label{sec:cifarddpm}

Everything above is $d\le10$ synthetic or a $0.5$M U-Net, so one objection is that the
mechanism is an artefact of small capacity. We train a standard DDPM U-Net
($35.75$M parameters) on CIFAR-10 bottom-half inpainting, $N{=}2000$, $d{=}3072$,
$k{=}1536$, for $60000$ iterations at batch $128$, evaluating $48$ conditions at
$M{=}256$ samples each. Each bandwidth is repeated on three independently redrawn
instances, which vary the training subset and the training randomness together; the
mask is the bottom half throughout (Appendix~\ref{app:exp4}).

\paragraph{Hard conditioning ($h{=}0$).} On the displayed instance, the training-time
estimate of $\tr\Cov$ (8 conditions) falls from $179.1$ at iteration $500$ to $0.431$ at
$60000$, a factor of $415$ ($0.458$ re-measured); the memorisation ratio is $1.000$ and the generated mean identifies
the correct training image at $48/48$ conditions. Retrained and re-measured at $48$
conditions and $M{=}256$, the three redrawn instances end at $0.411$--$0.503$, each with
the correct image at $48/48$. Hard-conditioning collapse is therefore robust at this scale, although the experiment
does not separate instance variation from optimisation variation.

\paragraph{Label smoothing ($h>0$) against the reference at $h$.} The OLS slope $\beta$ of
$\log\tr\Cov$ on $\log\tr\Covh$ across conditions, which the optimum puts at $1$, is
$0.481\pm0.037$ at $h{=}4$, where the reference spans $4.57$ decades, and $-0.156$ at
$h{=}6$, where it spans $0.74$ and a median ratio of $1.15$ certifies nothing
(Table~\ref{tab:cifarddpm}, Figure~\ref{fig:cifartrack}, appendix). $38\%$ of numerical samples are off-atom at $h{=}6$ under the stated nearest-neighbour criterion. This finite-cutoff measurement combines learned-field, truncation and discretisation effects; it does not identify the limiting support. The next
section fits the conditional moments with a reference at a larger bandwidth; this
description does not identify the network's law.

\paragraph{Paired solver and coverage pilot.} We additionally evaluate the six
final checkpoints ($h=0,4$, three seeds each) on the same eight held-out images, with
32 paired source draws and Euler resolutions 50, 100, 200 and 400. At 400 steps,
hidden-pixel central 90\% coverage is $0.0728$--$0.0830$ at $h=0$ and
$0.7709$--$0.7983$ at $h=4$, against the finite-sample reference $29/33$.
For the 32-coordinate PCA joint score, upper-rank 90\% coverage is zero at $h=0$
and $0.375$--$0.875$ at $h=4$, against $30/33$. Smoothing increases spread but
does not establish joint calibration. The joint score also depends on discretization:
at $h=4$, seed 1, coverage changes from $0.75$ at 50 steps to $0.375$ at 400.
These are eight shared evaluation images, with coverage resolution $1/8$, rather than
independent pixel or seed--image replicates. Appendix~\ref{app:newvalidation} gives
the selected-condition refinement; the 400-step pilot alone is not a convergence
certificate.

\subsection{Effective bandwidth as a moment diagnostic}
\label{sec:heff}

A trained model's conditional moments need not match the reference at its training bandwidth~$h$. We therefore fit a bandwidth shared across evaluation conditions, using the exact reference moments of Proposition~\ref{prop:moments}.

\paragraph{Moment estimates.} At a fixed checkpoint, draw $M>1$ samples $\hat x_c^{(1)},\dots,\hat x_c^{(M)}$ at each condition $y_c$, $c=1,\dots,C$. The generated mean and covariance trace are estimated by
\begin{align*}
m_c &= \frac1M\sum_{r=1}^M\hat x_c^{(r)}\,,\\
T_c &= \frac1{M-1}\sum_{r=1}^M\big\|\hat x_c^{(r)}-m_c\big\|^2\,.
\end{align*}
The factor $M-1$ is the sample-covariance normalisation; it is distinct from the factor~$N$ in the empirical reference moments. Sampling uses the integration settings of each experiment.

\paragraph{Bandwidth fits.} Let $\mathcal H\subset(0,\infty)$ be a finite log-spaced grid. Fit the trace in log space and the mean in Euclidean distance:
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
h_{\mathrm{eff}} &= \arg\min_{h'\in\mathcal H}\sum_{c=1}^C\big(\log T_c-\log\tr\mathrm{Cov}_{h'}(y_c)\big)^2\,,\\
h_{\mathrm{mean}} &= \arg\min_{h'\in\mathcal H}\sum_{c=1}^C\big\|m_c-\bar x_{h'}(y_c)\big\|\,.
\end{aligned}
\label{eq:heff}
\end{equation}
Each fit uses one bandwidth for all~$C$ conditions. The mean fit uses no variance statistics, and the trace-fitted bandwidth predicts reference means without fitting them. Both statistics are computed from the same generated samples.

\paragraph{Uncertainty.} Resample the $C$ condition indices with replacement and refit both bandwidths on each of 500 bootstrap replicates, using the same resampled indices for the two fits. The 2.5th and 97.5th percentiles give the reported 95\% intervals. Reported interval agreement means that these two intervals overlap. The grids are specified in Appendix~\ref{app:audit}.

Their agreement alone does not single out the Gaussian kernel: Laplace, Cauchy and kNN
references over the same atoms pass it almost as often. Fit quality does, though weakly
where the smoothing is the network's own: the Gaussian reference fits the variances best
at 36/48 checkpoints, 7/12 at $h{=}0$. Giving each atom a non-atomic spread
$\N(x^i,\rho^2I_d)$ lowers the held-out error at 10/48 checkpoints and raises it at 17,
and $\hat\rho=0$ lies inside its interval at 41/48. These variance fits are
compatible with an atomic-mixture reference (Table~\ref{tab:families}); they do not
establish that the network's law is atomic. Section~\ref{sec:independent} directly
tests this distinction. When is the bandwidth identified?

\begin{proposition}[Identifiability of the effective bandwidth]
\label{prop:heffid}
Assume the atoms are not all identical and all fitted traces are positive.
For $h>0$, let $D_i(y)=|y-y^i|^2$ and $\Cov_p$ the covariance over $i$ under $p^{(h)}(y)$. Then
$\partial_h\bar x_h(y)=h^{-3}\Cov_p(x^i,D_i)$ and
$\partial_h\tr\Covh(y)=h^{-3}\Cov_p(|x^i-\bar x_h(y)|^2,D_i)$, and
$h^3\partial_h\tr\Covh(y)$ tends to the unweighted covariance of $|x^i-\bar x|^2$ and $D_i$
as $h\to\infty$. Let $F(h)=(\log\tr\mathrm{Cov}_h(y_c))_{c\le C}$, let the measured
traces $T=(T_c)_c$ satisfy $\log T=F(h_{\mathrm{true}})+e$, and let $\hat h$ minimise
$|\log T-F(h)|$ over a set $\mathcal H\ni h_{\mathrm{true}}$ of bandwidths. Then
$\hat h\in\{h\in\mathcal H:|F(h)-F(h_{\mathrm{true}})|\le2|e|\}$. If this set lies in an
interval $\mathcal I\ni h_{\mathrm{true}}$ on which $u\cdot F'\ge\kappa>0$ for a unit
vector $u$, then $|\hat h-h_{\mathrm{true}}|\le2|e|/\kappa$. Likewise for
$h_{\mathrm{mean}}$, with $G(h)=(\bar x_h(y_c))_c$, $e=m-G(h_{\mathrm{true}})$ for the
generated means $m=(m_c)_c$, and $|e|$ read in the norm $\sum_c|e_c|$ that
\eqref{eq:heff} minimises. \proofin{apx:pf-heffid}
\end{proposition}

The derivatives decay at large $h$, making inversion poorly conditioned.
The following calculation is a sensitivity diagnostic, not a certified error bar:
the fitted residual need not equal the unknown error $e$, and 25 derivative evaluations
do not certify the interval infimum required by the proposition. Using that residual
and a sampled minimum of $u\cdot F'$ over $[h/1.5,1.5h]$ (25-point grid),
the bound exceeds the bootstrap half-width at all 75 synthetic checkpoints and ranks
those widths with Spearman $0.91$; on CIFAR-10 this $\kappa$ falls from $17$ at $h{=}3$ to
$0.38$ and $0.13$ at $h{=}5,6$.

\paragraph{Synthetic.} Fifteen linear-Gaussian runs ($h\in\{0,0.01,0.05,0.1,0.5\}$, three
seeds) were retrained with weights kept at eight checkpoints (Figure~\ref{fig:heffsyn}).
Where the reference varies ($h\le0.1$, from $10^4$ iterations; 48 checkpoints) the two
bandwidths agree within their intervals at all 48 (log correlation $0.97$, median ratio
$1.03$), and with one parameter it fits the per-condition variances better than the
two-parameter power law behind $\beta$. It
falls between $10^4$ and $10^5$ iterations in 12/12 runs and settles just above $h$
($1.08h$ on average for $h\ge0.05$); at $h{=}0$ it keeps falling ($0.011\pm0.006$ at
$2\times10^5$), so the residual variance of Section~\ref{sec:exp1} is a bandwidth
that has not reached zero. At $h{=}0.5$
($n_{\mathrm{eff}}\approx100$) it is poorly identified (9/15 intervals overlap).

\begin{figure}[t]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.84\textwidth]{fig_heff_synthetic.png}
\caption{Linear-Gaussian problem, three seeds per bandwidth. (a) $h_{\mathrm{eff}}$
falls with training and stops just above the training bandwidth $h$ (dashed); line:
geometric mean over seeds, band: range. (b) The bandwidth fitted to the variances
against the one implied by the means, with 95\% bootstrap intervals: two disjoint
statistics give one bandwidth. Open markers ($h{=}0.5$): the reference barely varies
and $h_{\mathrm{eff}}$ is poorly identified, as Proposition~\ref{prop:heffid} predicts.}
\label{fig:heffsyn}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{Calibration of the reference law.} Where the posterior is known, the
coverage of a sampler's credible regions can be computed for the reference law itself.

\FloatBarrier
\clearpage
\begin{theorem}[Calibration of the reference law]
\label{thm:calib}
Let $X\sim\pi$ and $Y=g(X)+\eta$, $\eta\sim\N(0,\sigma^2I_k)$, $\sigma>0$, with $\pi$ and
measurable $g$ arbitrary, and let $\pi_h(\cdot\mid y)$ be the law of $X$ given
$\widetilde Y=Y+h\varepsilon=y$ (the Bayes-formula version, defined for every $y$), a posterior
under label noise of variance $s_h^2=\sigma^2+h^2$.
(a) For fixed $h>0$ and an i.i.d.\ training set, $p_1^{(h)}(\cdot\mid y)\Rightarrow
\pi_h(\cdot\mid y)$ almost surely as $N\to\infty$, for each $y$.
(b) For any measurable rule $y\mapsto R(y)$ of regions,
$|\Pr(X\in R(Y))-\Pr(X\in R(\widetilde Y))|\le\tau_h:=
\mathrm{TV}\big(\N(0,\sigma^2I_k),\N(0,s_h^2I_k)\big)\le\sqrt{k/8}\;h^2/\sigma^2$. If
$\pi_h(R(y)\mid y)=\alpha$ for every $y$ (such regions exist when $\pi$ has no atoms), the
second probability is $\alpha$: the reference law's coverage is within $\tau_h$ of $\alpha$.
(c) If $\pi=\N(\mu_x,\Sigma_x)$, $\Sigma_x\succ0$, and $g(x)=Ax$, then $\pi_h(\cdot\mid y)=\N(\mu_h(y),\Sigma_h)$
with $\Sigma_h=(\Sigma_x^{-1}+A^\top A/s_h^2)^{-1}$, $\Gamma_h=\Sigma_hA^\top/s_h^2$,
$\mu_h(y)=\Sigma_h\Sigma_x^{-1}\mu_x+\Gamma_hy$, and $X-\mu_h(Y)\sim
\N(0,\Sigma_h-h^2\Gamma_h\Gamma_h^\top)$: for $\alpha\in(0,1)$ the level-$\alpha$ ellipsoid
at the clean label covers with probability at least $\alpha$, strictly if $h>0$ and $A\neq0$.
(d) For fixed weights $w_i\ge0$ with $\sum_iw_i=1$ and atoms i.i.d.\ with covariance $S$,
the weighted mean has covariance $S/n_{\mathrm{eff}}$ and the weighted covariance has mean
$(1-1/n_{\mathrm{eff}})S$, $n_{\mathrm{eff}}=1/\sum_iw_i^2$; at $n_{\mathrm{eff}}=1$ the
moment-matched region is a point, which an absolutely continuous $x^\star$ independent of
the atoms hits with probability $0$. \proofin{apx:pf-calib}
\end{theorem}

Part (d) is a fixed-weight, i.i.d.-atom identity. Conditional on actual labels,
the atoms can have different means and covariances, so applying that identity
locally is an approximation. The Gaussian local model used below is not a theorem
identifying the trained distribution from its fitted moments.
The theorem gives both ends of a calibration window. Smoothing costs at most $\tau_h$
of coverage for any prior (b), and over-covers when the posterior is Gaussian (c); a
vanishing effective sample collapses coverage (d). Modelling the atoms near $y^\star$
as draws of $\N(\mu_h,\Sigma_h)$ with the actual weights, (d) predicts the held-out 90\%
coverage of all 75 trained $d{=}2$ checkpoints from their $h_{\mathrm{eff}}$ with
$R^2=0.90$, $[0.85,0.93]$ resampling runs, against a ceiling of $0.97$ set by the
binomial noise of the measurement (Figure~\ref{fig:t3}; $0.59$ for the 50\% region,
where the weights' dispersion matters more). Part (b) makes the right edge prior-free: on two mixture
posteriors the reference law over-covers by at most $\tau_h$, a bound that is nearly
tight at $h=\sigma/2$ (Appendix~\ref{apx:pf-calib}). As a ranking, $h_{\mathrm{eff}}$ (Spearman $0.92$)
is ahead of the training loss ($0.85$) and keeps $0.90$ with the iteration partialled out, also
on an eight-mode mixture posterior ($0.91$, $0.91$); it predicts collapse, not the PIT
error or extraction. Given the free quantities it adds no reliable information
($\Delta R^2=+0.04$, $[-0.04,0.22]$); its use is a prediction that needs no coverage labels
(Appendix~\ref{app:calibdetails}). Theorem~\ref{thm:labelmix} bounds the coverage error of
any label-weighted mixture at finite $N$, though loosely: on this problem the bound is
vacuous at $N\le10^4$ and only locates the calibration window (Appendix~\ref{apx:thmB}).

\begin{figure}[t]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.94\textwidth]{fig_t3_coverage.png}
\caption{Theorem~\ref{thm:calib} on the linear-Gaussian problem. (a) Coverage of the
nominal 90\% region against bandwidth: infinite-data limit (c), over-covering for $h>0$;
finite effective sample (d), collapsing at small bandwidth (both for seed 0); dots:
trained checkpoints of all seeds at their $h_{\mathrm{eff}}$. (b) Coverage predicted
from $h_{\mathrm{eff}}$ against the measured coverage, for the theory (d) and for
the exact finite reference law.}
\label{fig:t3}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{CIFAR-10.} Six runs of the setup above ($h\in\{4,5\}$, three instances each)
were retrained with weights kept at 2k--60k and analysed at $M{=}128$
(Figure~\ref{fig:heffcifar}). From 5k on the two bandwidths
agree at 31/36 checkpoints; four of the five misses are at $h{=}5$ at 5k--10k.
$h_{\mathrm{eff}}$ falls monotonically in 5/6 runs and ends at $1.13$--$1.34h$; it is
still falling at 60k in four runs and has levelled off in two, so whether it has a floor
above $h$ is open. At $h{=}6$, where the reference barely varies, the two agree at 8/18;
at $h{=}0$ the means stay on the nearest atom while $h_{\mathrm{eff}}$ falls to $3.4$--$3.7$,
so no one bandwidth describes hard-conditioned training at this scale
(Appendix~\ref{app:heffends}). $\beta$ rises as $h_{\mathrm{eff}}/h$ approaches $1$ in 5/6 runs,
reaching $0.66$ at $1.13h$. At this scale $h_{\mathrm{eff}}$ fits the per-condition
variances no better than the power law (comparable from 30k on, mostly worse before),
and it does not set calibration. On 64 held-out test-split images per run ($h\in\{4,5,6\}$,
$M{=}64$ draws), coverage is read from the rank of the true image among the draws, so a
tie-free calibrated sampler scores $0.91$ at the 90\% level. Per pixel it falls from $0.93$--$0.96$
at 2k to $0.73$--$0.82$ by 20k and ends at $0.77$--$0.86$, and the sample spread ranks the
miscalibration with $|\rho|=0.90$ against $0.52$ for $h_{\mathrm{eff}}$. A joint region in
the top principal components covers less still, $0.47$--$0.78$ at 60k; in the top 8
components this is a per-direction shortfall in spread compounding over the directions
($R^2=0.81$, Appendix~\ref{app:calibdetails}).

\begin{figure}[t]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{fig_heff_cifar.png}
\caption{CIFAR-10, DDPM U-Net, three instances per bandwidth ($h\in\{4,5\}$). (a, b) $h_{\mathrm{eff}}$
from the variances (filled) and the bandwidth implied by the means (open), with 95\%
intervals; dashed: training $h$; cross: the published run at 60k. From 5k on the two agree at 31 of 36 checkpoints; the fitted bandwidths generally fall towards $h$ without reaching it. (c) $\beta$ against
$h_{\mathrm{eff}}/h$; training proceeds to the right.}
\label{fig:heffcifar}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{The audit in practice.} Appendix~\ref{app:audit} collects these diagnostics into
one procedure that needs the training set and samples, and no retraining: the reference
weights and moments at each condition; the level as an aggregate ratio, never an average
of per-condition ratios; the range of the reference across conditions, without which
agreement in level is uninformative; the slope $\beta$; $h_{\mathrm{eff}}$ and
$h_{\mathrm{mean}}$; the on-atom fraction; and, where held-out pairs exist, coverage read
from ranks, per coordinate and jointly. It locates a model relative to its training set;
at image scale it measures calibration rather than predicting it.

\FloatBarrier

\paragraph{Where the bandwidth comes from.} Two models of an imperfect learner bracket
these measurements (Appendix~\ref{app:specbias}). If the label dependence of the
conditional moments is learned by kernel gradient flow, the model's moments are well fitted by a
Nadaraya--Watson reference at one bandwidth (median log-space $R^2$ $0.97$),
$h_{\mathrm{eff}}$ falls to $h$, and for a kernel spectrum $|\omega|^{-\zeta}$ the
resolution shrinks as $t^{-1/\zeta}$ (Proposition~\ref{prop:specbias}; measured slopes
$-0.40$ and $-0.29$ against $-\frac12$ and $-\frac14$; Figure~\ref{fig:t2}). If instead
the field itself is averaged over nearby labels, Proposition~\ref{prop:filtered} proves almost-sure atomicity (Section~\ref{sec:intervention-atomicity}).



Limited label resolution alone preserves atomic endpoints in the exact flow.
At a finite terminal time, an off-atom sample can also reflect truncation or solver
error; assigning the CIFAR-10 deviations to learned spatial error requires separating
those numerical effects.
Neither model reproduces the networks' additive exponent ($p\approx1.3$ against
$2.2$--$2.8$) or their stop above $h$.

\begin{figure}[t]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.94\textwidth]{fig_t2_resolution.png}
\caption{Kernel gradient flow \eqref{eq:kgf} on the EXP-1 training set (seed 0,
$\ell=\mathrm{sd}(y)$). (a) $s(t)$, the effective bandwidth at $h=0$: power-law decay
with the slope of Proposition~\ref{prop:specbias}(b) (dotted: $-1/\zeta$) for kernels
with power-law spectra, stagnation for the Gaussian kernel. (b) Under the Laplace
kernel $h_{\mathrm{eff}}$ falls and converges to each training bandwidth $h$ (dashed).}
\label{fig:t2}
\end{figure}

% ==========================================================================
\section{Limitations}
\label{sec:limitations}

\paragraph{Distributional and numerical limits.} The five-seed validation tests a
variance-fitted reference at fresh labels, rather than fitting a distribution by its
held-out discrepancy. Its finite-sample null diagnostics do not supply equivalence
tests. The extreme hard-conditioned condition is retained in the primary mean and
diagnosed separately. The CIFAR pilot uses only eight shared images and a fixed mask;
its selected-condition solver checks cannot certify the entire image evaluation.
The constructed small-loss counterexample is an exact field, not evidence that the
trained architectures implement it; its regularity constants are not held fixed
along the vanishing-loss family.

\paragraph{What is proved and what is measured.} Proposition~\ref{prop:heffid} says when
$h_{\mathrm{eff}}$ is identified and Theorem~\ref{thm:calib} what calibration a bandwidth
buys. A spectral-bias model (Appendix~\ref{app:specbias}) reproduces the fall of
$h_{\mathrm{eff}}$ to $h$, but not the networks' rates or their gap above $h$ (open
beyond $6\times10^4$ iterations). Theorem~\ref{thm:calib}(c) is Gaussian; (b) only bounds
the deviation, needs observation noise and is informative only for $h^2\sqrt k\ll\sigma^2$,
so for noiseless inpainting the endpoint law, part (d) and the audit apply but (b) does
not. At image scale $h_{\mathrm{eff}}$ fits the variances no better than a power law,
does not predict coverage, and the measured sampler has off-atom outputs; on the synthetic problems it adds
little to free quantities once coverage labels are available, and the finite-sample bound
of Theorem~\ref{thm:labelmix} is vacuous at $N\le10^4$. The synthetic problems have
$d\le10$; the image experiments use one mask and $N{=}2000$, with instance and
optimisation randomness confounded; label spaces with no natural kernel are out of scope.

% ==========================================================================
\section{Conclusion}
\label{sec:conclusion}

Gaussian label smoothing gives an exact empirical endpoint reference. Averaging
the field over labels and applying fixed nonnegative classifier-free guidance preserve
atomic endpoints almost surely, with convergence and exceptional tie basins treated
explicitly. These statements concern exact fields, not arbitrary trained networks.
Small training loss alone cannot certify an endpoint law: a late amplification layer
can retain order-one variance at vanishing teacher loss. A finite-time certificate
follows here from an explicit transfer of error to generated trajectories; the
example and certificate specialize known endpoint-stability considerations.

The bandwidth audit describes conditional moments in the measured regimes.
Independent evaluation finds smaller reference discrepancy with label smoothing,
but does not certify equality of distributions. A hard-conditioned extrapolation
outlier persists under independent integration, and image coverage is sensitive to
the solver and the choice of marginal or joint score. These observations delimit the
transfer from exact empirical fields to learned samplers: neither moment agreement
nor training loss alone supplies that transfer.

\label{endmain}

table to $<0.05\%$), \texttt{analyze\_p7\_kernel.py}, \texttt{verify\_kernel\_theory.py},
\texttt{verify\_prop17.py}, \texttt{verify\_target\_noise.py},
\texttt{atomicity\_scaling.py}, \texttt{theory\_t1\_check.py},
\texttt{theory\_t3\_coverage.py}, \texttt{theory\_t3\_general.py},
\texttt{theory\_t2\_resolution.py}, \texttt{theory\_t2\_filtered.py}. Experiments: \texttt{run\_exp1.sh},
\texttt{run\_sweeps.py} ($+$\texttt{-{}-gpus N}), \texttt{train\_exp2},
\texttt{train\_exp3}, each followed by the matching analysis script;
\texttt{heff\_family\_test.py} for Table~\ref{tab:families}. For

requires rerunning \texttt{run\_sweeps.py}. Proofs of all
load-bearing results are given in full in Section~\ref{sec:theory} and

weight functions, the linear-Gaussian instance) is made explicit at the point it is

% ==========================================================================
\appendix
\section{Deferred proofs}
\label{app:proofs}

Every result of Section~\ref{sec:theory} is proved in its own subsection below, with its statement repeated for reference; results whose statement already lives in the appendix are proved where they are stated. The technical lemmas come first.

\subsection{Technical lemmas}
\label{apx:lemmas}

\begin{lemma}[Change of variables]
\label{lem:cov}
Fix $i$ and $t<1$. Conditionally on $I=i$, the map $X_0\mapsto X_t=(1-t)X_0+tx^i$ is an
invertible affine bijection of $\R^d$ with inverse $X_0=(X_t-tx^i)/(1-t)$; hence
$\sigma(X_t\mid I=i)=\sigma(X_0\mid I=i)$ and
\begin{equation}
p(x\mid I=i,t)=(1-t)^{-d}\,\pi_0\!\big(\tfrac{x-tx^i}{1-t}\big).
\label{eq:cov}
\end{equation}
The Jacobian factor $(1-t)^{-d}$ does not depend on $i$, so it cancels in every
posterior over $I$. Because $\pi_0$ is Gaussian, $p(x\mid I=i,t)>0$ for every $x$ and
every $t<1$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}[Proof of Lemma~\ref{lem:cov}]
    The map $\Psi(x_0)=(1-t)x_0+tx^i$ satisfies
    \begin{align*}
        \det D\Psi &= (1-t)^d, \\
        \Psi^{-1}(x) &= \frac{x-tx^i}{1-t},
    \end{align*}
    so $\sigma(X_t\mid I=i)=\sigma(X_0\mid I=i)$. Since $X_0\sim\pi_0$ is independent of $I$,
    \begin{align*}
        p(x\mid I=i,t) &= p_{X_0}\big(\Psi^{-1}(x)\big)\,|\det D\Psi|^{-1} \\
        &= (1-t)^{-d}\,\src{i}.
    \end{align*}
    The factor $(1-t)^{-d}$ does not depend on $i$ and cancels in $\Pr(I=i\mid X_t=x,t)$; positivity follows from $\pi_0>0$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Well-posedness]
\label{lem:wellposed}
Let $w_1,\dots,w_N:\R^d\times[0,1)\to[0,1]$ be $C^\infty$ with $\sum_i w_i\equiv1$, and
set $v(x,t)=\sum_i w_i(x,t)(x^i-x)/(1-t)$ and $M=\max_i|x^i|$. Then:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*),leftmargin=2em]
\item $v\in C^\infty(\R^d\times[0,1))$, hence is locally Lipschitz in $x$, uniformly on
  compact subsets of $[0,1)$;
\item for every $x_0$ the ODE $\dot x_t=v(x_t,t)$, $x_{t=0}=x_0$, has a unique solution
  on the whole of $[0,1)$, and it obeys $|x_t|+M\le(|x_0|+M)/(1-t)$;
\item writing $\Phi_{t}$ for the resulting flow map, for every $\mu_0\in\mathcal P(\R^d)$
  the curve $\mu_t:=(\Phi_t)_\#\mu_0$ is the \emph{unique} narrowly continuous measure
  solution of $\partial_t\mu_t+\nabla\!\cdot(v\mu_t)=0$ on $[0,1)$ with initial datum
  $\mu_0$.
\end{enumerate}
We do \emph{not} claim that $\lim_{t\uparrow1}\mu_t$ exists for arbitrary such weights;
where an endpoint law is asserted below (Propositions~\ref{prop:collapse}
and~\ref{prop:uncond}, Corollary~\ref{cor:repeated}, Theorem~\ref{thm:endpoint}) its existence is established
directly, from almost-sure convergence of the interpolant, and $p_1$ denotes that weak
limit.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}[Proof of Lemma~\ref{lem:wellposed}]
    (a) $v$ is a finite sum of products of $C^\infty$ functions, hence locally Lipschitz in $x$, uniformly on compacts of $[0,1)$.

    (b) Existence and uniqueness on $[0,\tau)$ follow from Picard--Lindel\"of \citep[Ch.~II]{hartman2002ode}. Since $w_i\ge0$ and $\sum_iw_i=1$,
    \begin{equation}
    \begin{split}
        |v(x,t)| &\le \sum_iw_i\,\frac{|x^i-x|}{1-t} \\
        &\le \frac{M+|x|}{1-t}.
    \end{split}
    \label{eq:vgrowth}
    \end{equation}
    For $g(t)=|x_t|+M$ this gives $g'\le|v(x_t,t)|\le g/(1-t)$, so by Gr\"onwall
    \begin{align*}
        g(t) &\le g(0)\exp\int_0^t\frac{ds}{1-s} \\
        &= \frac{g(0)}{1-t} \\
        &< \infty \qquad (t<1),
    \end{align*}
    and $\tau=1$.

    (c) For $\varphi\in C_c^\infty(\R^d)$ and $\mu_t=(\Phi_t)_\#\mu_0$,
    \begin{align*}
        \frac{d}{dt}\int\varphi\,d\mu_t &= \int\nabla\varphi(\Phi_t)\cdot v(\Phi_t,t)\,d\mu_0 \\
        &= \int\nabla\varphi\cdot v\,d\mu_t,
    \end{align*}
    the weak form of $\partial_t\mu_t+\nabla\!\cdot(v\mu_t)=0$. For uniqueness, let $(\mu_t)_{t\le T}$, $T<1$, be a narrowly continuous solution and $\psi(x,s)=\varphi(\Phi_{s,T}(x))$, which is $C^1$, compactly supported in $x$ (the flow also
exists backward on $[0,T]$, where $|v|\le(M+|x|)/(1-T)$ by \eqref{eq:vgrowth}, so
$\psi(\cdot,s)$ vanishes outside the compact set $\Phi_{T,s}(\supp\varphi)$), and satisfies
    \begin{equation*}
        \partial_s\psi+v\cdot\nabla\psi=0,\qquad\psi(\cdot,T)=\varphi .
    \end{equation*}
    The weak form extends to such time-dependent test functions by mollification in $s$, since $v$ is bounded on compacts \citep[Ch.~8]{ambrosio2008gradient}. Then
    \begin{align*}
        \frac{d}{ds}\int\psi(\cdot,s)\,d\mu_s &= \int\big(\partial_s\psi+v\cdot\nabla\psi\big)\,d\mu_s \\
        &= 0,
    \end{align*}
    so
    \begin{align*}
        \int\varphi\,d\mu_T &= \int\psi(\cdot,0)\,d\mu_0 \\
        &= \int\varphi\circ\Phi_{0,T}\,d\mu_0 \\
        &= \int\varphi\,d\big((\Phi_T)_\#\mu_0\big),
    \end{align*}
    i.e.\ $\mu_T=(\Phi_T)_\#\mu_0$ \citep[see also][Ch.~8]{ambrosio2008gradient}.
\end{proof}

Both $v_{\mathrm{unc}}^\star$ and $\vstar$ have this form with smooth weights, so
Lemma~\ref{lem:wellposed} applies and no ``assume the flow is well defined'' hypothesis
is needed.

\begin{lemma}[Equivalent mixture coupling]
\label{lem:mixture-coupling}
Fix $y$ and consider the coupling $I\sim p^{(h)}(y)$, $X_0\sim\pi_0$ (independent of
$I$), $X_1:=x^I$, with the same linear interpolant $X_t=(1-t)X_0+tX_1$. Its
conditional velocity field $\E[X_1-X_0\mid X_t=x,t]$ equals $\vstar(x,t,y)$ of
\eqref{eq:kernel-field} for every $x,t<1$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}[Proof of Lemma~\ref{lem:mixture-coupling}]
    By Bayes, Lemma~\ref{lem:cov} and $\pih{i}(y)\propto \KK{i}$,
    \begin{align*}
        \Pr(I=i\mid X_t=x,t) &\propto \pih{i}(y)\,(1-t)^{-d}\,\src{i} \\
        &\propto \src{i}\KK{i},
    \end{align*}
    the numerator of $\wih{i}$ in \eqref{eq:kernel-field}. Both are normalised over $i$, so $\Pr(I=i\mid X_t=x,t)=\wih{i}$. As $X_1-X_0=\fat{i}$ given $I=i$,
    \begin{align*}
        \E[X_1-X_0\mid X_t=x,t] &= \sum_i\wih{i}\,\fat{i} \\
        &= \vstar(x,t,y).\qedhere
    \end{align*}
\end{proof}

\subsection{Proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:projection}}
\label{apx:pf-projection}

\begin{quote}\small
\noindent\textbf{Proposition~\ref{prop:projection}} ($L^2$ projection).
For every square-integrable $v$,
$L_h(v)=\E[\Var(U\mid X_t,t,\widetilde Y)]+\E\,|v-\E[U\mid X_t,t,\widetilde Y]|^2$,
so the a.e.-unique minimiser is $\vstar(x,t,y)=\E[U\mid X_t=x,t,\widetilde Y=y]$ with
$\inf_v L_h=\E[\Var(U\mid X_t,t,\widetilde Y)]$.
\end{quote}

\begin{proof}[Proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:projection}]
    Let $\mathcal G=\sigma(X_t,t,\widetilde Y)$ and $m=\E[U\mid\mathcal G]$. Then
    \begin{align*}
        L_h(v) &= \E|U-m|^2+\E|m-v|^2+2\,\E\langle U-m,\,m-v\rangle \\
        &= \E[\Var(U\mid\mathcal G)]+\E|v-m|^2,
    \end{align*}
    since
    \begin{align*}
        \E\langle U-m,\,m-v\rangle &= \E\big\langle\E[U-m\mid\mathcal G],\,m-v\big\rangle \\
        &= 0.
    \end{align*}
    The last term vanishes iff $v=m$ a.e., so $\vstar=m$ and $\inf_vL_h=\E[\Var(U\mid\mathcal G)]$.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:collapse}}
\label{apx:pf-collapse}

\begin{quote}\small
\noindent\textbf{Proposition~\ref{prop:collapse}} (Exact conditional collapse; $h=0$).
Let $h=0$. Then
(a) $v_0^\star(x,t,y^i)=(x^i-x)/(1-t)$ for $t<1$ $(\star)$;
(b) $\inf_v L_0(v)=0$;
(c) the ODE $\dot x_t=(x^i-x_t)/(1-t)$ has the explicit solution
$x_t=(1-t)x_0+tx^i$, so $x_t\to x^i$ for every $x_0$; and
(d) $p_1^{(0)}(\cdot\mid y^i)=\delta_{x^i}$, hence
$\Cov[p_1^{(0)}(\cdot\mid y^i)]=0$.
\end{quote}

\begin{proof}[Proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:collapse}]
    By Assumption~\ref{as:standing}(iii), $Y=y^i$ gives $I=i$ and $X_1=x^i$; by Lemma~\ref{lem:cov}, $X_0=\frac{x-tx^i}{1-t}$ given $X_t=x$. Hence
    \begin{equation}
        U = x^i-X_0 = \fat{i},
        \label{eq:pf:collapse:2}
    \end{equation}
    a function of the conditioning variables. By Proposition~\ref{prop:projection},
    \begin{align*}
        v_0^\star(x,t,y^i) &= \E[U\mid X_t=x,t,Y=y^i] \\
        &= \fat{i},
    \end{align*}
    which is (a). By \eqref{eq:pf:collapse:2},
    \begin{align*}
        U-v_0^\star(X_t,t,Y) &= 0 \quad\text{a.s.}, \\
        L_0(v_0^\star) &= 0 = \inf_vL_0,
    \end{align*}
    which is (b). Along $\dot x_t=\frac{x^i-x_t}{1-t}$,
    \begin{align*}
        \frac{d}{dt}\,\frac{x_t-x^i}{1-t} &= \frac{\dot x_t(1-t)+x_t-x^i}{(1-t)^2} \\
        &= 0,
    \end{align*}
    so
    \begin{align*}
        x_t &= (1-t)x_0+tx^i \\
        &\to x^i \qquad (t\uparrow1),
    \end{align*}
    and $p_1^{(0)}(\cdot\mid y^i)=\delta_{x^i}$ with $\Cov=0$, which is (c) and (d).
\end{proof}

\subsection{Proof of Corollary~\ref{cor:repeated}}
\label{apx:pf-repeated}

\begin{corollary}[Repeated labels]
\label{cor:repeated}
Drop the distinctness in Assumption~\ref{as:standing}(iii), let $h=0$, and for a query label $y$ let
$\mathcal I_y=\{i:y^i=y\}\ne\emptyset$. Then
\begin{align*}
    \vstarz(x,t,y) &= \sum_{i\in\mathcal I_y}q_i^y(x,t)\,\fat{i}, \qquad
    q_i^y\propto\src{i}\ \text{ on }\mathcal I_y, \\
    p_1^{(0)}(\cdot\mid y) &= \frac1{|\mathcal I_y|}\sum_{i\in\mathcal I_y}\delta_{x^i},
\end{align*}
whose covariance is the within-label scatter $|\mathcal I_y|^{-1}\sum_{i\in\mathcal I_y}(x^i-\bar x_y)(x^i-\bar x_y)^\top$,
$\bar x_y=|\mathcal I_y|^{-1}\sum_{i\in\mathcal I_y}x^i$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}[Proof of Corollary~\ref{cor:repeated}]
    Given $Y=y$, $I$ is uniform on $\mathcal I_y$ and $X_0\sim\pi_0$ is independent of $I$. By Bayes and Lemma~\ref{lem:cov},
    \begin{align*}
        \Pr(I=i\mid X_t=x,t,Y=y) &= \frac{\src{i}}{\sum_{j\in\mathcal I_y}\src{j}} \\
        &= q_i^y(x,t),
    \end{align*}
    and given $I=i$, $U=\fat{i}$ by \eqref{eq:pf:collapse:2}, so by Proposition~\ref{prop:projection}
    \begin{align*}
        \vstarz(x,t,y) &= \E[U\mid X_t=x,t,Y=y] \\
        &= \sum_{i\in\mathcal I_y}q_i^y\,\fat{i}.
    \end{align*}
    The weights $q_i^y$ are smooth, positive and sum to one. As in the proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:endpoint}, with $I$ uniform on $\mathcal I_y$, the flow is well posed, $(\Phi_t)_\#\pi_0=\law(X_t)$, and $X_t\to x^I$ a.s., so
    \begin{align*}
        p_1^{(0)}(\cdot\mid y) &= \law(x^I) \\
        &= \frac1{|\mathcal I_y|}\sum_{i\in\mathcal I_y}\delta_{x^i},\qedhere
    \end{align*}
\end{proof}

\subsection{Proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:uncond}}
\label{apx:pf-uncond}

\begin{proposition}[Unconditional endpoint is the full empirical law]
\label{prop:uncond}
For $L_{\mathrm{unc}}(v)=\E|X_1-X_0-v(X_t,t)|^2$ and $t<1$, with
$q_i(x,t)=\Pr(I=i\mid X_t=x,t)\propto\pi_0\!\big(\tfrac{x-tx^i}{1-t}\big)$,
\[
v_{\mathrm{unc}}^\star(x,t)=\sum_i q_i(x,t)\,\frac{x^i-x}{1-t},\qquad
p_1^{\mathrm{unc}}=\rhohat_X=\tfrac1N\sum_i\delta_{x^i}.
\]
Moreover $\inf_v L_{\mathrm{unc}}>0$ whenever $x^i\ne x^j$ for some $i\ne j$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}[Proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:uncond}]
    By Bayes and Lemma~\ref{lem:cov} ($\frac1N(1-t)^{-d}$ cancels),
    \begin{align*}
        q_i(x,t) &= \frac{\src{i}}{\sum_j\src{j}} \\
        &> 0 .
    \end{align*}
    Given $I=i$, $U=\fat{i}$ by \eqref{eq:pf:collapse:2}, so by Proposition~\ref{prop:projection}
    \begin{align*}
        v_{\mathrm{unc}}^\star(x,t) &= \E[U\mid X_t=x,t] \\
        &= \sum_iq_i(x,t)\,\fat{i}.
    \end{align*}
    This has the form of Lemma~\ref{lem:wellposed}. As in the proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:endpoint}, with the uniform law on $I$, $p_t=\law(X_t)$, and $X_t\to X_1\sim\rhohat_X$ a.s., so $p_1^{\mathrm{unc}}=\rhohat_X$.

    With $u_i=\fat{i}$ and $\bar u=\sum_iq_iu_i$,
    \begin{align*}
        \Var(U\mid X_t=x,t) &= \sum_iq_i\,|u_i-\bar u|^2 \\
        &> 0
    \end{align*}
    whenever $x^i\ne x^j$ for some $i,j$ ($q_i>0$ and $u_i\ne u_j$). Hence $\inf_vL_{\mathrm{unc}}=\E[\Var(U\mid X_t,t)]>0$.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Proof of Corollary~\ref{cor:distinction}}
\label{apx:pf-distinction}

\begin{quote}\small
\noindent\textbf{Corollary~\ref{cor:distinction}} (The correct conditional/unconditional distinction).
Both exact population flows are supported on the training set:
$p_1^{(0)}(\cdot\mid y^i)=\delta_{x^i}$ and
$p_1^{\mathrm{unc}}=\frac1N\sum_i\delta_{x^i}$. The difference is between
single-example and full-empirical-measure memorisation, not between memorising and not,
and it shows in the conditional second moment:
\begin{equation*}
\Cov[p_1^{(0)}(\cdot\mid y^i)]=0
\qquad\text{vs.}\qquad
\Cov[p_1^{\mathrm{unc}}]=\Xhat.
\end{equation*}
\end{quote}

\begin{proof}[Proof of Corollary~\ref{cor:distinction}]
    By Proposition~\ref{prop:collapse}(d), $p_1^{(0)}(\cdot\mid y^i)=\delta_{x^i}$ and $\Cov=0$. By Proposition~\ref{prop:uncond},
    \begin{align*}
        \Cov[p_1^{\mathrm{unc}}] &= \frac1N\sum_i(x^i-\bar x)(x^i-\bar x)^\top \\
        &= \Xhat .
    \end{align*}
    Both laws are mixtures of point masses at training examples.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:kernel-field}}
\label{apx:pf-kernel-field}

\begin{quote}\small
\noindent\textbf{Proposition~\ref{prop:kernel-field}} (Kernel-weighted population minimiser).
For $h>0$, $t<1$,
\begin{equation*}
\vstar(x,t,y)=\sum_i w_i^{(h)}(x,t,y)\,\frac{x^i-x}{1-t},\quad
w_i^{(h)}=\frac{\pi_0\!\big(\tfrac{x-tx^i}{1-t}\big)K_h(y-y^i)}
{\sum_j\pi_0\!\big(\tfrac{x-tx^j}{1-t}\big)K_h(y-y^j)}.
\end{equation*}
\end{quote}

\begin{proof}[Proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:kernel-field}]
    It suffices to compute the posterior of the index $I$ given $(X_t,\widetilde Y)$.
    Given $I=i$, $X_t=(1-t)X_0+tx^i$ and $\widetilde Y=y^i+h\eps$ with $X_0\perp\eps$, so $X_t$ and $\widetilde Y$ are conditionally independent and their joint density factorises. By Bayes and Lemma~\ref{lem:cov},
    \begin{align*}
        \Pr(I=i\mid X_t=x,t,\widetilde Y=y) &= \frac{\src{i}\KK{i}}{\sum_j\src{j}\KK{j}} \\
        &= \wih{i},
    \end{align*}
    since $\frac1N(1-t)^{-d}$ cancels. Given $I=i$ and $X_t=x$, $U=\fat{i}$ by \eqref{eq:pf:collapse:2}, so by Proposition~\ref{prop:projection}
    \begin{align*}
        \vstar(x,t,y) &= \E[U\mid X_t=x,t,\widetilde Y=y] \\
        &= \sum_i\wih{i}(x,t,y)\,\fat{i}.\qedhere
    \end{align*}
\end{proof}

\subsection{Proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:endpoint}}
\label{apx:pf-endpoint}

\begin{quote}\small
\noindent\textbf{Theorem~\ref{thm:endpoint}} (Endpoint law under label smoothing).
For every $h>0$ and query $y$,
\begin{equation*}
p_1^{(h)}(\cdot\mid y)=\sum_{i=1}^N p_i^{(h)}(y)\,\delta_{x^i}.
\end{equation*}
\end{quote}

\begin{proof}[Proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:endpoint}]
    The marginals of the coupling below solve the continuity equation of the flow, so they equal the flow's marginals, and they converge to $\law(x^I)$. Here $p_1^{(h)}(\cdot\mid y):=\lim_{t\uparrow1}(\Phi_t)_\#\pi_0$ for the flow $\Phi$ of $\vstar(\cdot,\cdot,y)$.
    By Lemma~\ref{lem:mixture-coupling}, $\vstar(x,t,y)=\E[X_1-X_0\mid X_t=x,t]$ for the coupling $I\sim p^{(h)}(y)$, $X_1=x^I$. The weights $\wih{i}$ are smooth, positive and sum to one, so by Lemma~\ref{lem:wellposed} the flow $(\Phi_t)_{t<1}$ exists and
    \begin{equation}
        \partial_tp_t+\nabla\!\cdot\big(p_t\vstar\big)=0,\qquad p_0=\pi_0,
        \label{eq:pf:endpoint:2}
    \end{equation}
    has a unique solution. $(\Phi_t)_\#\pi_0$ solves it, and so does $\law(X_t)$: for $\varphi\in C_c^\infty$,
    \begin{align*}
        \frac{d}{dt}\E\,\varphi(X_t) &= \E\langle\nabla\varphi(X_t),\,X_1-X_0\rangle \\
        &= \E\langle\nabla\varphi(X_t),\,\vstar(X_t,t,y)\rangle,
    \end{align*}
    by conditioning on $X_t$ (Lemma~\ref{lem:mixture-coupling}). Hence
    \begin{align*}
        (\Phi_t)_\#\pi_0 &= \law(X_t) =: p_t .
    \end{align*}
    Since $X_t=(1-t)X_0+tx^I\to x^I$ a.s.,
    \begin{align*}
        p_1 &= \lim_{t\uparrow1}p_t \\
        &= \law(x^I) \\
        &= \sum_i\pih{i}(y)\,\delta_{x^i}.\qedhere
    \end{align*}
\end{proof}

This is convergence in law of the marginals of the flow, which is what $p_1$ denotes. Convergence of
individual trajectories is not needed for this coupling argument.
Propositions~\ref{prop:filtered} and~\ref{prop:cfgatom} separately prove pathwise
convergence for the averaged and guided fields.

\subsection{Proof of Corollary~\ref{cor:zerobw}}
\label{apx:pf-zerobw}

\begin{quote}\small
\noindent\textbf{Corollary~\ref{cor:zerobw}} (Zero bandwidth at fixed $N$).
If $y$ has a unique nearest label $i^\star=\arg\min_i|y-y^i|$, then
$p_i^{(h)}(y)\to\mathbf 1\{i=i^\star\}$ as $h\to0$, so
$p_1^{(h)}(\cdot\mid y)\Rightarrow\delta_{x^{i^\star}}$; at $y=y^i$ this
recovers Proposition~\ref{prop:collapse}(d).
\end{quote}

\begin{proof}[Proof of Corollary~\ref{cor:zerobw}]
    For $i\ne i^\star$,
    \begin{align*}
        \frac{\KK{i}}{\KK{i^\star}} &= \exp\Big[-\frac{|y-y^i|^2-|y-y^{i^\star}|^2}{2h^2}\Big] \\
        &\xrightarrow[h\to0]{} 0,
    \end{align*}
    so $\pih{i}(y)\to\ind\{i=i^\star\}$, and $p_1^{(h)}\Rightarrow\delta_{x^{i^\star}}$ by Theorem~\ref{thm:endpoint}.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Proof of Corollary~\ref{cor:infbw}}
\label{apx:pf-infbw}

\begin{quote}\small
\noindent\textbf{Corollary~\ref{cor:infbw}} (Infinite bandwidth at fixed $N$).
$p_i^{(h)}(y)\to1/N$ for every $i$ as $h\to\infty$, so
$p_1^{(h)}(\cdot\mid y)\Rightarrow p_1^{\mathrm{unc}}$.
\end{quote}

\begin{proof}[Proof of Corollary~\ref{cor:infbw}]
    Uniformly in $i$, $\KK{i}=(2\pi h^2)^{-k/2}\big[1+O(h^{-2})\big]$. The prefactor cancels:
    \begin{align*}
        \pih{i}(y) &= \frac{1+O(h^{-2})}{N+O(h^{-2})} \\
        &\longrightarrow \frac1N .
    \end{align*}
    By Theorem~\ref{thm:endpoint}, $p_1^{(h)}(\cdot\mid y)=\sum_i\pih{i}(y)\delta_{x^i}\Rightarrow\frac1N\sum_i\delta_{x^i}=\rhohat_X$,
    and $\rhohat_X=p_1^{\mathrm{unc}}$ by Proposition~\ref{prop:uncond}.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:moments}}
\label{apx:pf-moments}

\begin{quote}\small
\noindent\textbf{Proposition~\ref{prop:moments}} (Exact finite-$N$ moments).
$\bar x_h(y)=\sum_i p_i^{(h)}(y)\,x^i$ and
$\Covh(y)=\sum_i p_i^{(h)}(y)\,(x^i-\bar x_h(y))(x^i-\bar x_h(y))^\top$, both computable
