from the training set for any $h>0$; they are the reference for generated moments.
$\tr\Covh(y)$ need not be monotone in $h$.
\end{quote}

\begin{proof}[Proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:moments}]
    By Theorem~\ref{thm:endpoint} the endpoint law is $\sum_i\pih{i}\delta_{x^i}$, so
    \begin{align*}
        \bar x_h(y) &= \sum_i\pih{i}x^i, \\
        \Covh(y) &= \sum_i\pih{i}(x^i-\bar x_h)(x^i-\bar x_h)^\top .
    \end{align*}
    For non-monotonicity take $k=d=1$, $y=0$ and $(y^i,x^i)=(0,0),(1,10),(10,0)$. Then
    \begin{align*}
        h\to0: &\qquad p^{(h)}\to(1,0,0), && \tr\Covh\to0, \\
        h=2: &\qquad p^{(h)}=(0.531,\,0.469,\,2{\times}10^{-6}), && \tr\Covh=24.90, \\
        h\to\infty: &\qquad p^{(h)}\to(\tfrac13,\tfrac13,\tfrac13), && \tr\Covh\to\tr\Xhat=\tfrac{200}{9}=22.22
    \end{align*}
    (Corollaries~\ref{cor:zerobw}, \ref{cor:infbw}).
\end{proof}

\subsection{Proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:atomicity}}
\label{apx:pf-atomicity}

\begin{quote}\small
\noindent\textbf{Proposition~\ref{prop:atomicity}} (Label smoothing never leaves the training set).
For every $h>0$, and for $h=0$ at $y=y^i$, $\operatorname{supp}(p_1^{(h)}(\cdot\mid y))
\subseteq\{x^1,\dots,x^N\}$. Consequently, if the true posterior $p(\cdot\mid y)$ puts no
mass on $\{x^i\}$,
\begin{equation*}
W_2^2\big(p_1^{(h)}(\cdot\mid y),\,p(\cdot\mid y)\big)\ \ge\
\int\operatorname{dist}\big(x,\{x^i\}\big)^2\,p(x\mid y)\,dx\ >\ 0,
\end{equation*}
and this lower bound does not depend on $h$.
\end{quote}

\begin{proof}[Proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:atomicity}]
    Any coupling with an atomic law moves each posterior draw at least to its nearest atom, and that bound involves the atoms alone.
    For $h>0$, Theorem~\ref{thm:endpoint} gives $p_1^{(h)}=\sum_i\pih{i}\delta_{x^i}$; for $h=0$
    at $y=y^i$, Proposition~\ref{prop:collapse}(d) gives $p_1^{(0)}=\delta_{x^i}$. Either way
    $\supp\,p_1^{(h)}\subseteq\{x^i\}$. For any coupling $(X,X')$ of $p_1^{(h)}$ and $p(\cdot\mid y)$, $X\in\{x^i\}$ a.s., so
    \begin{align*}
        \E|X-X'|^2 &\ge \E\dist(X',\{x^i\})^2 \\
        &= \int\dist(x,\{x^i\})^2\,p(x\mid y)\,dx =: F(y).
    \end{align*}
    Taking the infimum over couplings gives \eqref{eq:w2floor}. $F$ does not involve $h$, and $F(y)>0$ since $\{x^i\}$ is $p(\cdot\mid y)$-null.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:tgtnoise}}
\label{apx:pf-tgtnoise}

\begin{quote}\small
\noindent\textbf{Proposition~\ref{prop:tgtnoise}} (Endpoint smoothing).
Fix $\rho\ge0$ and replace the endpoint by $X_1=x^I+\rho\,\xi$ with $\xi\sim\N(0,I_d)$ independent of
$(I,X_0,\varepsilon)$ and redrawn every step, keeping label bandwidth $h>0$. Write
$s_t^2=(1-t)^2+t^2\rho^2$. Then $\vstar$ is again a weighted average of per-atom
fields, now with spatial weights $\varphi_{s_t}(x-tx^i)$ in place of
$\pi_0(\tfrac{x-tx^i}{1-t})$ (explicitly, \eqref{eq:tgtfield} in the appendix), and
the endpoint law is
\begin{equation*}
p_1(\cdot\mid y)=\sum_i p_i^{(h)}(y)\,\N(x^i,\rho^2I_d),
\qquad
\text{mean}=\bar x_h(y),\quad \Cov=\Covh(y)+\rho^2I_d.
\end{equation*}
For every $\rho>0$ this law is absolutely continuous, so \eqref{eq:w2floor} does not
apply to it; moreover $s_1=\rho>0$, so $\vstar$ extends continuously to $t=1$ and the
$1/(1-t)$ blow-up of $(\star)$ disappears.
\end{quote}

\begin{proof}[Proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:tgtnoise}]
    When $\rho=0$, the field and endpoint law reduce to Theorem~\ref{thm:endpoint}, with $\N(x^i,0)$ interpreted as $\delta_{x^i}$. For the remaining argument assume $\rho>0$. Given $I=i$,
    \begin{align*}
        r:=x-tx^i &= (1-t)X_0+t\rho\xi \\
        &\sim \N(0,s_t^2I_d), \qquad s_t^2=(1-t)^2+t^2\rho^2,
    \end{align*}
    so $p(x\mid I=i,t)=\varphi_{s_t}(x-tx^i)$ and, as in Proposition~\ref{prop:kernel-field},
    \begin{align*}
        w_i &= \Pr(I=i\mid X_t=x,t,\widetilde Y=y) \\
        &\propto \varphi_{s_t}(x-tx^i)\,\KK{i}.
    \end{align*}
    Since $(X_0,\xi,r)$ is jointly Gaussian,
    \begin{align*}
        \E[X_0\mid r] &= \frac{1-t}{s_t^2}\,r, \\
        \E[\xi\mid r] &= \frac{t\rho}{s_t^2}\,r,
    \end{align*}
    and with $U=x^i+\rho\xi-X_0$,
    \begin{equation}
        \E[U\mid X_t=x,t,I=i] = x^i+\frac{t\rho^2-(1-t)}{s_t^2}\,(x-tx^i).
        \label{eq:pf:tgtnoise:4}
    \end{equation}
    By Proposition~\ref{prop:projection},
    \begin{equation}
        \vstar(x,t,y) = \sum_iw_i\Big[x^i+\frac{t\rho^2-(1-t)}{s_t^2}\,(x-tx^i)\Big].
        \label{eq:tgtfield}
    \end{equation}
    The field \eqref{eq:tgtfield} is smooth on $\R^d\times[0,1]$ with at most linear growth, so the flow is global and the uniqueness argument of Lemma~\ref{lem:wellposed}(c) applies verbatim.
    For the coupling $I\sim p^{(h)}(y)$, $X_0\sim\pi_0$, $\xi\sim\N(0,I_d)$ independent, $X_1=x^I+\rho\xi$, the index posterior is $\propto \pih{i}\varphi_{s_t}(x-tx^i)\propto w_i$ and \eqref{eq:pf:tgtnoise:4} holds, so its conditional velocity is $\vstar$. As in the proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:endpoint}, and since $X_t\to X_1$ a.s.,
    \begin{align*}
        p_1 &= \law(x^I+\rho\xi) \\
        &= \sum_i\pih{i}(y)\,\N(x^i,\rho^2I_d),
    \end{align*}
    with
    \begin{align*}
        \text{mean} &= \bar x_h(y), \\
        \Cov &= \rho^2I_d+\Covh(y).
    \end{align*}
    It is absolutely continuous, so \eqref{eq:w2floor} does not apply. Since $s_1=\rho>0$, $\frac{t\rho^2-(1-t)}{s_t^2}\to1$, and $\vstar$ is bounded on bounded sets up to $t=1$.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:prop17}}
\label{apx:pf-prop17}

\begin{quote}\small
\noindent\textbf{Proposition~\ref{prop:prop17}} (Closed form and endpoint invariance in $\sigma$).
Let $h=0$ and $v_\sigma^\star=\E[\dot X_t\mid X_t,t,Y]$ for target
\eqref{eq:c2target}, the a.e.-unique minimiser of $\E\big[t(1-t)\,|v-\dot X_t|^2\big]$. Then
for $t\in(0,1)$:
(a) $v_\sigma^\star(x,t,y^i)=x^i+c(t)(x-tx^i)$ with
$c(t)=\frac{-(1-t)+\frac{\sigma^2}2(1-2t)}{s_t^2}=\frac12\frac{d}{dt}\log s_t^2$;
(b) the flow is $x_t=t\,x^i+s_t\,x_0$; and
(c) since $s_1=0$,
\begin{equation*}
p_1^{(0)}(\cdot\mid y^i)=\delta_{x^i}\quad\text{for every }\sigma\ge0.
\end{equation*}
\end{quote}

\begin{proof}[Proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:prop17}]
    The weighted loss is $\tfrac16$ times the loss of Proposition~\ref{prop:projection}
    under the probability measure whose $t$-marginal has density $6t(1-t)$. Under it the
    target is square integrable,
    $\E[t(1-t)|\dot X_t|^2]\le2\E|X_1-X_0|^2+2d\sup_t t(1-t)\dot\gamma(t)^2<\infty$ with
    $t(1-t)\dot\gamma^2=\sigma^2(1-2t)^2/4$, and the conditional law given $(X_t,t,Y)$ is
    unchanged, so the a.e.-unique minimiser is $\E[\dot X_t\mid X_t,t,Y]$.
    Given $I=i$ (pinned by $Y=y^i$),
    \begin{align*}
        r:=x-tx^i &= (1-t)X_0+\gamma Z \\
        &\sim \N(0,s_t^2I_d), \qquad s_t^2=(1-t)^2+\gamma^2,
    \end{align*}
    with $\Cov(X_0,r)=(1-t)I_d$ and $\Cov(Z,r)=\gamma I_d$, so
    \begin{align*}
        \E[X_0\mid r] &= \frac{1-t}{s_t^2}\,r, \\
        \E[Z\mid r] &= \frac{\gamma}{s_t^2}\,r.
    \end{align*}
    Since $\dot X_t=x^i-X_0+\dot\gamma Z$,
    \begin{align*}
        v_\sigma^\star(x,t,y^i) &= x^i+\frac{-(1-t)+\dot\gamma\gamma}{s_t^2}\,r, \\
        \dot\gamma\gamma &= \tfrac12(\gamma^2)' = \tfrac{\sigma^2}2(1-2t),
    \end{align*}
    which is (a) with $c(t)=\frac{-(1-t)+\frac{\sigma^2}2(1-2t)}{s_t^2}$. Moreover
    \begin{align*}
        (s_t^2)' &= -2(1-t)+2\dot\gamma\gamma \\
        &= 2s_t^2\,c(t),
    \end{align*}
    so $c=\frac12(\log s_t^2)'$.

    (b) $u_t=x_t-tx^i$ satisfies $\dot u_t=c(t)u_t$, so
    \begin{align*}
        u_t &= x_0\exp\!\big(\tfrac12[\log s_t^2-\log s_0^2]\big) \\
        &= s_t\,x_0,
    \end{align*}
    that is, $x_t=tx^i+s_tx_0$.

    (c) $s_t^2=(1-t)\big[(1-t)+\sigma^2t\big]$, so $s_1=0$ and $x_t\to x^i$ for all $x_0$ and $\sigma\ge0$.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Proof of Corollary~\ref{cor:prop18}}
\label{apx:pf-prop18}

\begin{corollary}[What interpolant noise does and does not change]
\label{cor:prop18}
Interpolant noise does not change the population endpoint law
\eqref{eq:invariance} for any $\sigma$; it \emph{does} raise the irreducible error
($\inf_v L_\sigma=+\infty$ for $\sigma>0$, whereas $\inf_v L_0=0$) and halves the Lipschitz
blow-up rate: $\lim_{t\uparrow1}(1-t)|c(t)|=1$ for $\sigma=0$ and $\tfrac12$ for
$\sigma>0$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}[Proof of Corollary~\ref{cor:prop18}]
    The endpoint is Proposition~\ref{prop:prop17}(c). For $\sigma=0$, $\dot X_t=X_1-X_0$ and $\inf_vL_0=0$ (Proposition~\ref{prop:collapse}). For $\sigma>0$ the target is not square integrable, but for each $t\in(0,1)$ its conditional law given $(X_t,t,Y)$ is Gaussian with finite covariance, so for every measurable $v$, $\E[|v-\dot X_t|^2\mid X_t,t,Y]=|v-v_\sigma^\star|^2+\Var(\dot X_t\mid X_t,t,Y)$ in $[0,\infty]$, and $\inf_vL_\sigma=\E\Var(\dot X_t\mid X_t,t,Y)$ ($\Var$ the trace). With $u=(-1,\dot\gamma)$, $w=(1-t,\gamma)$, $|w|^2=s_t^2$ and $u\cdot w=c\,s_t^2$, each coordinate has
    \begin{align*}
        \Var(\dot X_t\mid r,I=i) &= |u|^2-\frac{(u\cdot w)^2}{|w|^2} \\
        &= 1+\dot\gamma^2-c(t)^2s_t^2 .
    \end{align*}
    Near $t=0$, $c$ is bounded and $\dot\gamma^2\sim\sigma^2/(4t)$, so $\int_0^1\Var\,dt=+\infty$ and $\inf_vL_\sigma=+\infty$: every $v$ has infinite unweighted loss, and $v_\sigma^\star$ minimises the $t(1-t)$-weighted loss (Proposition~\ref{prop:prop17}), not $L_\sigma$. Finally
    \begin{align*}
        (1-t)\,c(t) &= \frac{-(1-t)+\frac{\sigma^2}2(1-2t)}{(1-t)+\sigma^2t} \\
        &\longrightarrow
        \begin{cases}
            -1, & \sigma=0, \\
            -\tfrac12, & \sigma>0 .
        \end{cases}\qedhere
    \end{align*}
\end{proof}

\subsection{Proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:factors}}
\label{apx:pf-factors}

\begin{quote}\small
\noindent\textbf{Proposition~\ref{prop:factors}} (The two mechanisms act on different factors).
Let the atoms be not all equal and $y$ have a unique nearest label $i^\star$. Label smoothing
changes the endpoint law: $p_1^{(h)}(\cdot\mid y)$ tends to $\delta_{x^{i^\star}}$ as
$h\to0$ and to $\rhohat_X$ as $h\to\infty$, and these differ. Interpolant noise does not:
although it replaces the spatial factor $\N(tx^i,(1-t)^2I_d)$ of Lemma~\ref{lem:cov} by
$\N(tx^i,s_t^2I_d)$, identically for every $i$, $p_1^{(0)}(\cdot\mid y^i)=\delta_{x^i}$ for
every $\sigma\ge0$ (Proposition~\ref{prop:prop17}). The two remedies are therefore not
interchangeable.
\end{quote}

\begin{proof}[Proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:factors}]
    Label factor: $p_1^{(h)}=\sum_i\pih{i}\delta_{x^i}$ depends on $h$ through $\KK{i}$, and by Corollaries~\ref{cor:zerobw}, \ref{cor:infbw}
    \begin{align*}
        p_1^{(h)} &\Rightarrow \delta_{x^{i^\star}} \quad (h\to0,\ \text{unique nearest label}), \\
        p_1^{(h)} &\Rightarrow \rhohat_X \quad (h\to\infty),
    \end{align*}
    which are distinct because the atoms are not all equal. Spatial factor: for $h=0$ and any $\sigma\ge0$,
    \begin{align*}
        \N\big(tx^i,(1-t)^2I_d\big) &\longrightarrow \N\big(tx^i,s_t^2I_d\big) \quad \forall i, \\
        p_1^{(0)}(\cdot\mid y^i) &= \delta_{x^i} \quad \forall\sigma
    \end{align*}
    (Proposition~\ref{prop:prop17}(c)).
\end{proof}

\subsection{Proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:expansion}}
\label{apx:pf-expansion}

\begin{assumption}[Random design, at a query point $y$]
\label{as:design}
The pairs $(x^j,y^j)$ are i.i.d.\ with marginal label density $\rho_Y$; write
$\mu(y)=\E[X\mid Y=y]$, $\Sigma(y)=\Cov(X\mid Y=y)$, $J(y)=\partial\mu/\partial y$ and
$m_4(y)=\E[\|X\|^4\mid Y=y]$. Fix $y$ in the interior of $\operatorname{supp}(\rho_Y)$
and a ball $B(y,r)$ contained in that interior. On $B(y,r)$:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*),leftmargin=2em,itemsep=1pt]
\item $\rho_Y$ is positive and $C^2$;
\item $\mu$ is $C^3$, with $M_3:=\sup_{B(y,r)}\|\nabla^3\mu\|<\infty$;
\item $\Sigma(\cdot)$ is \emph{constant} (homoscedasticity);
\item $m_4$ is bounded.
\end{enumerate}
Globally, $\rho_Y$ is bounded and $|\mu(y')|+\|\Sigma(y')\|+m_4(y')\le C(1+|y'|^m)$ for
some $C,m$.
\end{assumption}

Item~(iv) is not implied by (i)--(iii): those bound the conditional mean and covariance,
whereas $\Covh$ is a quadratic functional of $X$ whose \emph{variance} is what the
stochastic term must control. It is the hypothesis the standard kernel-smoothing rate
assumes, and the fluctuation estimate in the proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:expansion} is the only place it is used.

\begin{proposition}[Bandwidth expansion of the conditional covariance; random design]
\label{prop:expansion}
Under Assumption~\ref{as:design}, pointwise at $y$, as $Nh^k\to\infty$ and $h\to0$ (the stronger condition $Nh^{k+4}\to\infty$ suffices to separate the $h^2$ term from the generic stochastic error below),
\begin{equation}
\Covh(y)=\Sigma(y)+h^2 J(y)J(y)^\top+O(h^4)+O_P\big((Nh^k)^{-1/2}\big),
\label{eq:expansion}
\end{equation}
so $\tr\Covh(y)=\tr\Sigma(y)+h^2\|J(y)\|_F^2+\cdots$. Homoscedasticity is load-bearing rather than cosmetic, the expansion is
pointwise rather than uniform in $y$, and both the regularity budget and a
heteroscedastic counterexample are discussed in Appendix~\ref{app:proofs}.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}[Proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:expansion}]
    Let $s=\nabla\log\rho_Y(y)$, $q_h\propto K_h(y-\cdot)\rho_Y$, $\E_h[f]=\E[K_h(y-Y)f]/\E[K_h(y-Y)]$ and $u=(Y-y)/h$. Let $\Cov_h^{\mathrm{pop}}:=\Cov_h[X]$ be the population counterpart of $\Covh$; its deviation from $\Covh$ at $h=h_N$ is bounded in the last part. By total covariance
    \begin{equation}
        \Cov_h^{\mathrm{pop}} = \E_h[\Sigma(Y)]+\Cov_h[\mu(Y)].
        \label{eq:pf:expansion:2}
    \end{equation}
    Substituting $y'=y+hu$, $K_h(y-y')=h^{-k}\varphi(u)$, and Taylor expanding $\rho_Y$,
    \begin{equation}
    \begin{split}
        \tilde q_h(u) &= \frac{\varphi(u)\rho_Y(y+hu)}{\int\varphi\,\rho_Y(y+h\cdot)} \\
        &= \varphi(u)\big[1+h\,s^\top u+O(h^2)\big].
    \end{split}
    \label{eq:pf:expansion:3}
    \end{equation}
    With $\E_\varphi[u]=0$, $\E_\varphi[uu^\top]=I_k$, $\E_\varphi[u_au_bu_c]=0$ and $\E_\varphi[u_au_bu_cu_d]=\delta_{ab}\delta_{cd}+\delta_{ac}\delta_{bd}+\delta_{ad}\delta_{bc}$,
    \begin{equation}
    \begin{split}
        \E_{\tilde q_h}[u] &= h\,s+O(h^2), \\
        \E_{\tilde q_h}[uu^\top] &= I_k+O(h^2), \\
        \Cov_{\tilde q_h}[u] &= I_k+O(h^2).
    \end{split}
    \label{eq:pf:expansion:6}
    \end{equation}

    \emph{Within-group.} $\Sigma\equiv\Sigma(y)$ on $B(y,r)$, and outside
    \begin{align*}
        \int_{|u|>r/h}(1+|y+hu|^{2m})\varphi(u)\,du &\le C\,e^{-r^2/4h^2} \\
        &= o(h^p) \quad \forall p,
    \end{align*}
    so $\E_h[\Sigma(Y)]=\Sigma(y)+O(h^4)$. Without homoscedasticity, for $\Sigma\in C^2$, \eqref{eq:pf:expansion:6} gives
    \begin{align*}
        \E_h[\Sigma(Y)] &= \Sigma+h\,\nabla\Sigma\cdot\E[u]+\tfrac{h^2}2\nabla^2\Sigma\cdot\E[uu^\top]+o(h^2) \\
        &= \Sigma+h^2\big[\nabla\Sigma\cdot s+\tfrac12\tr\nabla^2\Sigma\big]+o(h^2),
    \end{align*}
    of the order of $h^2JJ^\top$. For example $k=d=1$, $Y\sim\mathrm{Unif}(-2,2)$, $X\mid Y=y\sim\N(0,1+y^2)$ has $J\equiv0$ but, at the query $y=0$,
$\Cov_h^{\mathrm{pop}}(0)=1+h^2+O(e^{-c/h^2})$ for some $c>0$.
The boundary correction is exponentially small, rather than identically zero.

    \emph{Between-group.} Since $\mu\in C^3$,
    \begin{align*}
        \mu(y+hu) &= \mu(y)+hJu+\tfrac{h^2}2Q(u)+R_h(u), \\
        Q(u)_a &= \sum_{b,c}\partial^2_{bc}\mu_a(y)\,u_bu_c, \\
        |R_h(u)| &\le \tfrac{M_3}6\,h^3|u|^3 .
    \end{align*}
    The moments of $\tilde q_h$ are bounded for $h\le1$, so the covariances involving $R_h$ are $O(h^4)$, $O(h^5)$, $O(h^6)$, and
    \begin{align*}
        \Cov_h[\mu(Y)] &= h^2J\,\Cov[u]\,J^\top+h^3\big[J\,\Cov(u,Q)+\mathrm{sym}\big] \\
        &\quad+h^4\Cov[Q]+O(h^4).
    \end{align*}
    By \eqref{eq:pf:expansion:3}, $\E_{\tilde q_h}[u_au_bu_c]=h\,[s_c\delta_{ab}+s_b\delta_{ac}+s_a\delta_{bc}]+O(h^2)$, so $\Cov(u,Q)=O(h)$; with \eqref{eq:pf:expansion:6},
    \begin{equation}
    \begin{split}
        \Cov_h[\mu(Y)] &= h^2JJ^\top+O(h^4), \\
        \Cov_h^{\mathrm{pop}} &= \Sigma(y)+h^2JJ^\top+O(h^4).
    \end{split}
    \label{eq:pf:expansion:1}
    \end{equation}

    \emph{Fluctuation.} With $S_N=\frac1N\sum_j\KK{j}$, $T_N=\frac1N\sum_j\KK{j}x^j$ and $\bar x_h=T_N/S_N$,
    \begin{align*}
        \Var\big(K_h(y-Y)\big) &= O(h^{-k}), \\
        \Var\big(K_h(y-Y)XX^\top\big) &= O(h^{-k}),
    \end{align*}
    the latter using $m_4$ bounded near $y$ (the only use of (iv)). Hence $S_N,T_N=\E[\cdot]+O_P(\sqrt{h^{-k}/N})$, with $\E[K_h(y-Y)]\to\rho_Y(y)>0$, and by the delta method
    \begin{align*}
        \bar x_h &= \E_h[X]+O_P\big((Nh^k)^{-1/2}\big), \\
        \Covh &= \Cov_h^{\mathrm{pop}}+O_P\big((Nh^k)^{-1/2}\big).
    \end{align*}
    With \eqref{eq:pf:expansion:1} this is \eqref{eq:expansion}.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:nwrate}}
\label{apx:pf-nwrate}

\begin{proposition}[Population smoothing and finite-sample Nadaraya--Watson error]
\label{prop:nwrate}
Under Assumption~\ref{as:design}, at a fixed interior query, define
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\mu_h^\infty(y)=\frac{\E[K_h(y-Y)X]}{\E K_h(y-Y)},\qquad
b(y)=\Delta\mu(y)+2J(y)\nabla\log\rho_Y(y).
\end{aligned}
\label{eq:nwbiascoef}
\end{equation}
As $h\to0$ and $Nh^k\to\infty$, the empirical estimator satisfies
\[
\bar x_h(y)-\mu(y)=\tfrac{h^2}{2}b(y)+o(h^2)
+O_P((Nh^k)^{-1/2}).
\]
If in addition $|X|\le R_X$ almost surely, then for all sufficiently small $h$,
\begin{align*}
\E|\bar x_h-\mu_h^\infty|^2&\le C(Nh^k)^{-1}+Ce^{-cNh^k},\\
|\E\bar x_h-\mu_h^\infty|&\le C(Nh^k)^{-1}+Ce^{-cNh^k}.
\end{align*}
In particular, for $Nh^k\ge1$,
$\E|\bar x_h-\mu(y)|^2\le C[h^4+(Nh^k)^{-1}]$.
Choosing $h\asymp N^{-1/(k+4)}$ balances this upper bound; it is not an
unconditional assertion about the exact optimal bandwidth.
In the linear-Gaussian instance, without imposing the additional response bound,
the deterministic population bias is exactly
\begin{equation}
\mu_h^\infty(y)-\mu(y)=-Bh^2(\Sigma_Y+h^2I_k)^{-1}y,
\label{eq:lgbias}
\end{equation}
where here $B=A^\top\Sigma_Y^{-1}$ and $\Sigma_Y=AA^\top+\sigma_{\mathrm{obs}}^2I_k$.
\end{proposition}

The scalar $R_X$ bounds responses; $B$ is the regression matrix.
Formula~\eqref{eq:lgbias} concerns the population
ratio, not the random finite-$N$ estimator. Its leading coefficient can vanish:
$b(y)=-2B\Sigma_Y^{-1}y$, which need not be nonzero for every $y\ne0$ unless
$B$ is injective. Affine regression alone therefore does not remove
design-density bias, nor guarantee a nonzero bias coefficient at every query.
The additional bounded-response assertion does not apply to an unbounded
Gaussian response. Assumption~\ref{as:design} alone supplies the stochastic
$O_P$ statement, not the finite expected-risk bounds.

\begin{proof}[Proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:nwrate}]
Local Gaussian-convolution Taylor expansions give
\[
\E K_h=\rho_Y(y)+\tfrac{h^2}{2}\Delta\rho_Y(y)+o(h^2),\qquad
\E[K_hX]=\rho_Y(y)\mu(y)+\tfrac{h^2}{2}\Delta(\rho_Y\mu)(y)+o(h^2).
\]
The polynomial global conditional-moment bounds and Gaussian tails justify
localization to a ball about $y$. Dividing the expansions and using the product
rule gives $\mu_h^\infty-\mu=h^2b/2+o(h^2)$. The fluctuation argument in
Proposition~\ref{prop:expansion} gives
$\bar x_h-\mu_h^\infty=O_P((Nh^k)^{-1/2})$.

For the additional expected-risk assertion, put $W_i=K_h(y-Y_i)$,
$m=\E W_i$, $\theta=\mu_h^\infty$ and
$\overline Z=N^{-1}\sum_iW_i(X_i-\theta)$.
Write $M=\sup\rho_Y$, $a=\rho_Y(y)/2$, $\kappa=\sup K$, and $r_K=\int K^2$.
For small $h$, $a\le m\le M$, $0<W_i\le\kappa h^{-k}$,
$\E W_i^2\le Mr_Kh^{-k}$, and $\E\overline Z=0$.
Bernstein's inequality gives, for $G=\{\overline W\ge m/2\}$,
\[
\Pr(G^c)\le\exp\left[-\frac{Nm^2}{8(\Var W_i+\kappa h^{-k}m/6)}\right]
\le e^{-cNh^k}.
\]
Both $\bar x_h$ and $\theta$ have norm at most $R_X$,
and $\E|\overline Z|^2\le4R_X^2Mr_K/(Nh^k)$ by independence and centering.
On $G$, $|\bar x_h-\theta|^2\le4|\overline Z|^2/m^2$; on $G^c$ it is at most
$4R_X^2$. This proves the first expected bound.

For the bias bound let $R=\bar x_h-\theta$. Since
$\E[R\overline W]=\E\overline Z=0$,
$\E R=-m^{-1}\E[R(\overline W-m)]$.
On $G$, Cauchy--Schwarz and $|R|\le2|\overline Z|/m$ bound this contribution by
\[
\frac2{m^2}\sqrt{\E|\overline Z|^2\Var\overline W}
\le C(Nh^k)^{-1}.
\]
On $G^c$, $|R|\le2R_X$ and $|\overline W-m|\le m$, giving $2R_X\Pr(G^c)$.
Combining with the population bias proves the MSE bound. At $Nh^k\ge1$ the
exponential term is absorbed by $C/(Nh^k)$.

Finally, completing the square in $K_h(y-Y)\rho_Y(Y)$ for Gaussian $Y$ gives
its reweighted mean $\Sigma_Y(\Sigma_Y+h^2I_k)^{-1}y$.
Multiplication by the regression matrix $B$ proves~\eqref{eq:lgbias}.
\end{proof}

The global response assumption cannot simply be omitted from this proof.
Choose a nonnegative label with bounded density, smooth and positive near $y=1$,
and tail $\Pr(Y>u)\asymp1/\log u$. Let $X=Y+Z$ with independent
$Z\sim\mathrm{Unif}[0,1]$. Its local mean is affine, its conditional covariance
is $1/12$, and its conditional fourth moment has polynomial growth, so
Assumption~\ref{as:design} holds. Nevertheless every positive-kernel estimator
satisfies $\bar x_h(1)\ge\min_iY_i$ and
$\E\min_iY_i=\int_0^\infty\Pr(Y>u)^N\,du=\infty$ for every finite $N$.
Thus the assumptions of the $O_P$ result do not imply a finite expected bias or MSE.

\subsection{Proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:liplb}}
\label{apx:pf-liplb}

\begin{quote}\small
\noindent\textbf{Proposition~\ref{prop:liplb}} (Lipschitz lower bound).
Fix $i$, $t<1$, $f_i(x,t)=(x^i-x)/(1-t)$, and suppose $v(\cdot,t,y^i)$ is
$L$-Lipschitz in $x$. Under $X_t\mid I=i\sim\N(tx^i,(1-t)^2 I_d)$,
$\E[|v(X_t,t,y^i)-f_i(X_t,t)|^2\mid I=i]\ge d\,[1-L(1-t)]_+^2$.
\end{quote}

\begin{proof}[Proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:liplb}]
    Let $X,X'$ be i.i.d.\ $\N(tx^i,(1-t)^2I_d)$ and $e=f_i-v$. Then
    \begin{align*}
        \E|f_i(X)-f_i(X')|^2 &= \frac{\E|X-X'|^2}{(1-t)^2} = 2d, \\
        \E|v(X)-v(X')|^2 &\le L^2\,\E|X-X'|^2 = 2dL^2(1-t)^2, \\
        \E|e(X)-e(X')|^2 &= 2\E|e(X)|^2-2\big|\E e(X)\big|^2 \\
        &\le 2\,\E|e(X)|^2 .
    \end{align*}
    The triangle inequality in $L^2(\Omega)$ for $f_i(X)-f_i(X')=[e(X)-e(X')]+[v(X)-v(X')]$ gives
    \begin{align*}
        \sqrt{2d} &\le \sqrt{2\,\E|e(X)|^2}+\sqrt{2d}\,L(1-t), \\
        \E|e(X)|^2 &\ge d\,[1-L(1-t)]_+^2 .
    \end{align*}
    (The identity for $\E|e(X)-e(X')|^2$, rather than the bound $4\E|e|^2$, keeps the constant $d$.)
\end{proof}

\subsection{Proof of Corollary~\ref{cor:floor}}
\label{apx:pf-floor}

\begin{quote}\small
\noindent\textbf{Corollary~\ref{cor:floor}} (Loss floor under a uniform Lipschitz constraint).
For $\mathcal F_L=\{v:\operatorname{Lip}_x v(\cdot,t,y^i)\le L\ \forall t,i\}$ with
$L\ge1$ and $t\sim\mathrm{Unif}(0,1)$,
\begin{equation*}
\inf_{v\in\mathcal F_L}L_0(v)\ =\ d\int_{1-1/L}^1[1-L(1-t)]^2\,dt\ =\ \frac{d}{3L}\ >\ 0.
\end{equation*}
The infimum is attained by the measurable field
$v_L(x,t,y^i)=x^i-\min\{L,(1-t)^{-1}\}(x-tx^i)$ for $t<1$.
More generally, if the Lipschitz constant is allowed to depend on time,
$\operatorname{Lip}_xv(\cdot,t,y^i)\le L(t)$, the same pointwise bound integrates to
\begin{equation*}
\inf_{v\in\mathcal F_{L(\cdot)}}L_0(v)\ \ge\ d\int_0^1\big[1-L(t)(1-t)\big]_+^2\,dt .
\end{equation*}
\end{quote}

\begin{proof}[Proof of Corollary~\ref{cor:floor}]
    We have
    \begin{align*}
        \int_0^{1/L}(1-Ls)^2\,ds &= \Big[s-Ls^2+\tfrac{L^2s^3}3\Big]_0^{1/L} \\
        &= \frac1{3L}.
    \end{align*}
    Proposition~\ref{prop:liplb} holds for every $i$, every $v\in\mathcal F_L$ and $t\ge1-1/L$; averaging over $I$ and $t$,
    \begin{align*}
        \inf_{v\in\mathcal F_L}L_0(v) &\ge d\int_{1-1/L}^1[1-L(1-t)]^2\,dt \\
        &= \frac d{3L}.
    \end{align*}
For the displayed $v_L$, the residual evaluated at
$X_t=tx^i+(1-t)X_0$ is $[1-\min\{L,(1-t)^{-1}\}(1-t)]X_0$.
It attains the pointwise lower bound for every time and identifying label,
and its sampled second moment is uniformly bounded. Integrating proves equality.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:cfg}}
\label{apx:pf-cfg}

\begin{proposition}[Guidance cannot leave the affine hull of the training set]
\label{prop:cfg}
Fix $w\ge0$. For $h>0$ at every query $y$, and for $h=0$ at an identifying observed label $y^i$, the following holds for $t<1$:
\begin{equation}
v_w(x,t)=\frac{m_w(x,t)-x}{1-t},\qquad
m_w(x,t)=\sum_i\Big[(1+w)w_i^{(h)}(x,t,y)-w\,q_i(x,t)\Big]x^i,
\label{eq:cfgfield}
\end{equation}
where $q_i$ are the unconditional index weights of Proposition~\ref{prop:uncond}. The coefficients sum to $1$ but are in general \emph{not} nonnegative; hence
$m_w(x,t)\in\mathcal A:=\operatorname{aff}\{x^1,\dots,x^N\}$ for every $(x,t)$. Let
$\Pi$ be the orthogonal projection onto $\mathcal A$ and $q_t:=x_t-\Pi x_t$. Then along
any solution of $\dot x_t=v_w(x_t,t)$,
\begin{equation}
q_t=(1-t)\,q_0 ,
\label{eq:cfgorth}
\end{equation}
so $q_t\to0$ and every weak limit of the guided flow's marginals is supported in $\mathcal A$,
for every $w$ and every initial law. In particular, if $N\le d$ then $\dim\mathcal A\le
N-1<d$, so $\mathcal A$ is Lebesgue-null and \emph{no} guidance weight makes the
conditional law absolutely continuous.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}[Proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:cfg}]
    By Propositions~\ref{prop:kernel-field} and~\ref{prop:uncond},
    \begin{align*}
        \vstar &= \sum_i\wih{i}\,\fat{i}, \\
        v_{\mathrm{unc}}^\star &= \sum_iq_i\,\fat{i}.
    \end{align*}
    In $v_w=(1+w)\vstar-w\,v_{\mathrm{unc}}^\star$ the $x$-terms give $-(1+w)x+wx=-x$, so $v_w=\frac{m_w-x}{1-t}$ with coefficients $(1+w)\wih{i}-wq_i$ summing to $1$; thus $m_w\in\mathcal A$. For the orthogonal projection $\Pi$ onto $\mathcal A$ (affine, $\Pi m_w=m_w$),
    \begin{align*}
        \dot q_t &= -\frac{q_t}{1-t} \\
        \Longrightarrow\ q_t &= (1-t)\,q_0 ,
    \end{align*}
    so $\dist(x_t,\mathcal A)\to0$ and, $\mathcal A$ being closed, the endpoint law is supported in $\mathcal A$. Well-posedness: $|m_w|\le(1+2w)\max_i|x^i|$, so \eqref{eq:vgrowth} holds with $M\to(1+2w)M$. For a fixed $p_0\in\mathcal A$ and $R=(1+2w)M+|p_0|$, integrating $\frac d{dt}\frac{\Pi x_t-p_0}{1-t}=\frac{m_w-p_0}{(1-t)^2}$ gives $|\Pi x_t-p_0|\le(1-t)|\Pi x_0-p_0|+Rt$, so the trajectories are bounded, the marginals are tight, weak limits exist along subsequences, and each is supported in $\mathcal A$.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:nearduplicate}}
\label{apx:pf-nearduplicate}

\begin{quote}\small
\noindent\textbf{Proposition~\ref{prop:nearduplicate}} (Near-duplicate labels).
Fix $h>0$, a query $y$, and a nonempty subset $C\subseteq\{1,\dots,N\}$ with
$|y-y^i|\le\varepsilon$ for $i\in C$ and $|y-y^j|\ge\Delta$ for $j\notin C$, where
$0\le\varepsilon<\Delta$. For the Gaussian kernel of bandwidth $h$, put
\[
\eta:=\frac{N-|C|}{|C|}\exp\!\Big(\!-\frac{\Delta^2-\varepsilon^2}{2h^2}\Big),
\qquad
\Lambda:=\exp\!\Big(\frac{\varepsilon^2}{2h^2}\Big)\ \ge 1 .
\]
Then \emph{(a)} $\sum_{j\notin C}p_j^{(h)}(y)\le\eta$; \emph{(b)}
$p_i^{(h)}(y)/p_{i'}^{(h)}(y)\le\Lambda$ for all $i,i'\in C$; and \emph{(c)}
$n_{\mathrm{eff}}(y)\ge|C|/(\Lambda+|C|\eta^2)$.
Consequently, as $\varepsilon/h\to0$ and $\Delta/h\to\infty$ the index posterior
converges to the uniform law on $C$, the endpoint law to
$|C|^{-1}\sum_{i\in C}\delta_{x^i}$, and $n_{\mathrm{eff}}\to|C|$.
\end{quote}

\begin{proof}[Proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:nearduplicate}]
    Let $K_i=c\,e^{-|y-y^i|^2/2h^2}$.

    (a)
    \begin{align*}
        \sum_{j\notin C}\pih{j} &\le \frac{\sum_{j\notin C}K_j}{\sum_{i\in C}K_i} \\
        &\le \frac{(N-|C|)\,e^{-\Delta^2/2h^2}}{|C|\,e^{-\eps^2/2h^2}} = \eta .
    \end{align*}

    (b) For $i,i'\in C$,
    \begin{align*}
        \frac{\pih{i}}{\pih{i'}} &= \frac{K_i}{K_{i'}} = \exp\frac{|y-y^{i'}|^2-|y-y^i|^2}{2h^2} \\
        &\le e^{\eps^2/2h^2} = \Lambda .
    \end{align*}

    (c) Let $S=\sum_{i\in C}\pih{i}$. By (b), $|C|\,\pih{i}\le\Lambda S$, so
    \begin{align*}
        \sum_{i\in C}(\pih{i})^2 &\le S\max_{i\in C}\pih{i} \le \frac\Lambda{|C|}, \\
        \sum_{j\notin C}(\pih{j})^2 &\le \eta^2,
    \end{align*}
    and
    \begin{align*}
        n_{\mathrm{eff}} &= \Big(\sum_i(\pih{i})^2\Big)^{-1} \\
        &\ge \frac{|C|}{\Lambda+|C|\eta^2}.
    \end{align*}
    As $\eps/h\to0$ and $\Delta/h\to\infty$: $\Lambda\to1$, $\eta\to0$, $S\ge1-\eta\to1$ and $p_i/p_{i'}\in[\Lambda^{-1},\Lambda]$, so the posterior tends to the uniform law on $C$ and, by Theorem~\ref{thm:endpoint}, the endpoint law to $|C|^{-1}\sum_{i\in C}\delta_{x^i}$. Also $\sum_i(\pih{i})^2\ge S^2/|C|$ gives $n_{\mathrm{eff}}\le|C|/S^2\to|C|$.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:cfgatom}}
\label{apx:pf-cfgatom}

\begin{quote}\small
\noindent\textbf{Proposition~\ref{prop:cfgatom}} (Guidance lands on a training atom).
Assume $x^1,\dots,x^N$ are distinct and fix a finite $w\ge0$.
The exact guided field has a unique trajectory on $[0,1)$ from every finite
initial point. Then:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*),leftmargin=2em]
\item if $h>0$, every trajectory converges to a point
  $z\in\operatorname{aff}N(z)$, where $N(z)$ is its set of nearest atoms.
  For an absolutely continuous source, the endpoint is a training atom almost surely;
\item if $h=0$ at an identifying label $y^i$, every trajectory converges to $x^i$,
  without an exceptional set of initial points.
\end{enumerate}
\end{quote}

\begin{proof}[Proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:cfgatom}]
Write $a_j=x^j$, $A=\{a_1,\dots,a_N\}$, $\eps=1-t$,
$\tau=-\log\eps$, $\alpha=\eps^2/t$, and $\beta=1/\alpha$, starting
the energy argument at any $t_0>0$. Dots in part (a) denote $\tau$ derivatives.
Set $b_j=x\cdot a_j-t|a_j|^2/2$ and
$q_{p,j}\propto p_j\exp(b_j/\alpha)$, normalized over $j$.
For $h>0$, take $p_c=(p_j^{(h)}(y))_j$ and $p_u=(1/N)_j$;
both are fixed strictly positive vectors. Their means are $m_c,m_u$.

\emph{(a) Positive bandwidth.}
\emph{Convergence.}
Put $H_p=\alpha\log\sum_jp_je^{b_j/\alpha}$ and
\[
U=\tfrac12\lVert x\rVert^2-(1+w)H_{p_c}+wH_{p_u}.
\]
The mean $(1+w)m_c-wm_u$ is bounded, so trajectories and $\tau$-speeds
are bounded and finite-time flows are well posed. We have $\nabla_xU=-\dot x$.
For fixed positive $p$, $D_{\rm KL}(q_p\Vert p)\le\log(1/\min_jp_j)$.
Direct differentiation gives $\nabla_xH_p=m_p$,
$\partial_\alpha H_p=-D_{\rm KL}(q_p\Vert p)$ and
$\partial_tH_p=-\tfrac12\sum_jq_{p,j}|a_j|^2$, treating $t$ and $\alpha$
as independent arguments. Consequently,
\[
\dot U=-\lVert\dot x\rVert^2+\mathcal R(\tau),\qquad
|\mathcal R(\tau)|\le C(|\dot\alpha|+\dot t).
\]
The latter bound is integrable. The min--max bounds on $H_p$ show that $U$
is bounded below on a bounded trajectory. Hence its squared speed is integrable.

Near any cluster point $z$, the non-nearest softmax weights tend uniformly to
zero at large times. Thus the signed mean approaches $\operatorname{aff}N(z)$.
If $z$ were outside that affine hull, its orthogonal distance would force a
positive lower speed bound in a fixed neighborhood at late times. Bounded upper
speed and recurrent visits would then contradict finite energy, as in
the convergence argument in the proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:filtered}. Each cluster point consequently lies in
$\operatorname{aff}N(z)$. There are finitely many such equidistant candidates,
and connectedness of the omega-limit set proves convergence.

\emph{The non-atom basins have zero volume.}
Consider a limit $z\notin A$, and let $r>0$ be the common distance of its nearest
atoms. Write $\mu_c=m_c-z$, $\mu_u=m_u-z$, and
$\mu_w=(1+w)\mu_c-w\mu_u$. Write $\Cov_c,\Cov_u$ for covariance under
$q_{p_c},q_{p_u}$, respectively. The signed covariance trace is
\begin{align*}
T&=(1+w)\operatorname{tr}\Cov_c(a)-w\operatorname{tr}\Cov_u(a)\\
 &=r^2-(1+w)\lVert\mu_c\rVert^2+w\lVert\mu_u\rVert^2+o(1)\\
 &=r^2-\lVert\mu_w\rVert^2+
 \frac{w}{1+w}\lVert\mu_u-\mu_w\rVert^2+o(1)
 \ge r^2-\lVert\mu_w\rVert^2+o(1).
\end{align*}
The error tends to zero along the whole trajectory, since non-nearest mass
vanishes and the atoms are finite. Since $\mu_w=\dot x+x-z$, convergence and
finite energy give $\int_n^{n+1}\lVert\mu_w\rVert^2\,d\tau\to0$.
On each late unit interval the ratio $\beta_{\max}/\beta_{\min}$ is bounded.
Writing the preceding error as at worst $-\eta_n$ throughout that interval,
where $\eta_n\to0$, yields
\[
\int_n^{n+1}\beta T\,d\tau
\ge(r^2-\eta_n)\beta(n)-\beta(n+1)
\int_n^{n+1}\lVert\mu_w\rVert^2\,d\tau
\ge\tfrac14 r^2\beta(n)
\]
eventually. The divergence is $\beta T-d$, so its accumulated integral tends
to infinity along integer times. The same bounded-image-ball,
change-of-variables, and Fatou argument as in Proposition~\ref{prop:filtered}
makes every non-atom basin null. Absolute continuity completes the proof.

\emph{(b) Hard conditioning.}
Dots now denote derivatives in the original time $t$.
The conditional mean is $a_i$ and the unconditional mean is
$m=\sum_jq_j a_j$, with $q_j\propto\exp(-|x-ta_j|^2/(2\eps^2))$.
The following estimate also quantifies convergence: for $0<t_0<t<1$,
\begin{equation}
|x_t-ta_i|^2\le\eps^2\left[
\frac{|x_{t_0}-t_0a_i|^2}{(1-t_0)^2}
+2w\log N\log\frac{t(1-t_0)}{t_0(1-t)}\right].
\label{eq:guided-hard-rate}
\end{equation}
On every compact time interval below $1$ the field is smooth, locally Lipschitz,
and has linear growth, since $m$ lies in the convex hull of the finite atoms.
These properties give existence and uniqueness on $[0,1)$.

Write $D_j=\lVert x-ta_j\rVert^2$ and $H(q)=-\sum_jq_j\log q_j$.
The Gibbs identity and the contribution of index $i$ to the partition function give
\[
\sum_jq_jD_j=2\eps^2\bigl(H(q)-\log\sum_je^{-D_j/(2\eps^2)}\bigr)
\le D_i+2\eps^2\log N.
\]
With $z=x-ta_i$, expanding $D_j-D_i$ therefore gives
\[
2t z\cdot(a_i-m)+t^2\sum_jq_j\lVert a_i-a_j\rVert^2
\le2\eps^2\log N.
\]
Along the flow, $\dot z=(-z+w(a_i-m))/\eps$. Dropping the nonnegative
sum for $t>0$ and using $w\ge0$, $Y=\lVert z\rVert^2$ satisfies
\[
Y'\le-\frac{2Y}{\eps}+\frac{2w\eps\log N}{t},\qquad
\left(\frac{Y}{\eps^2}\right)'\le\frac{2w\log N}{t(1-t)}.
\]
Integration yields \eqref{eq:guided-hard-rate}. Its right side tends to zero, so
$x_t-ta_i\to0$ and $x_t\to a_i$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
Almost-sure atomicity cannot be strengthened to all initial points in part (a).
For symmetric atoms $\{-a,a\}$ and equal conditional weights, $x_0=0$ gives
$x_t=0$ for every $t$ and every $w$. The non-atom basins have Lebesgue measure
zero by the Jacobian argument. The endpoint weights in part (a) are not identified
by this result and need not equal the original label weights.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:survival}}
\label{apx:pf-survival}

\begin{quote}\small
\noindent\textbf{Proposition~\ref{prop:survival}} (Error survival).
Fix $i$, let $h=0$ with distinct labels, and let $v$ be any locally Lipschitz field.
Write $\Delta(x,t):=v(x,t,y^i)-\vstarz(x,t,y^i)$ for its error against the exact
collapsed field, let $x^v_t$ solve $\dot x_t=v(x_t,t,y^i)$ from $x_0$ (we assume it exists on $[0,t]$), and let
$x^\star_t=(1-t)x_0+tx^i$ be the exact trajectory from the same $x_0$. Then for
$t<1$, with $e_t:=x^v_t-x^\star_t$, the \emph{identity}
\begin{equation*}
e_t\;=\;(1-t)\int_0^t\frac{\Delta(x^v_s,s)}{1-s}\,ds
\end{equation*}
holds, and in particular
\begin{equation*}
|e_t|\;\le\;(1-t)\int_0^t\frac{|\Delta(x^v_s,s)|}{1-s}\,ds .
\end{equation*}
Consequently:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*),leftmargin=2em]
\item \emph{(bounded errors do not survive)} if
  $\int_0^t|\Delta(x^v_s,s)|^2ds\le\varepsilon^2$ then
  $|e_t|\le\varepsilon\sqrt{(1-t)t}\le\varepsilon\sqrt{1-t}$, so a sampler stopping
  at $t=1-\delta$ has $|e_{1-\delta}|\le\varepsilon\sqrt\delta$, which vanishes with
  $\delta$ \emph{for every} finite $\varepsilon$;
\item \emph{(what the bound gives)} if $|\Delta(x,t)|\le c\,(1-t)^{-a}$ with $a>0$
  then $|e_t|\le\frac{c}{a}\big((1-t)^{1-a}-(1-t)\big)$, which at $a=1$ reads
  $|e_t|\le c\,t\le c$. Hence $\lim_{t\uparrow1}|e_t|=0$ when $a<1$, and
  $\limsup_{t\uparrow1}|e_t|\le c$ when $a=1$. For $a>1$ the right-hand side diverges
  and \eqref{eq:survival} constrains $e_t$ not at all---a diverging upper bound is
  not a diverging quantity, and $e\equiv0$ satisfies it. That case is settled by
  (c), not by (b);
\item \emph{(the bound is attained, so the threshold is real)} fix a unit vector $u$
  and take the spatially constant error $\Delta(x,t)=c\,(1-t)^{-a}u$, for which
  $v=\vstarz+\Delta$ is locally Lipschitz with $\Delta$ having Lipschitz constant
  $L_\Delta=0$ in $x$. Then \eqref{eq:survival-id} evaluates in closed form:
  \begin{equation*}
  e_t=\frac{c}{a}\big((1-t)^{1-a}-(1-t)\big)\,u\quad(a>0),
  \qquad e_t=-c\,(1-t)\log(1-t)\,u\quad(a=0),
  \end{equation*}
  so \eqref{eq:survival} holds with equality for every $t<1$. Therefore
  $\lim_{t\uparrow1}|e_t|$ is $0$ for $a<1$, is exactly $c$ for $a=1$, and is $+\infty$
  for $a>1$: the trichotomy is realised and not merely permitted. These fields have
  $\mathcal L_i=\infty$ for $a\ge\frac12$, so the trichotomy concerns pathwise errors and
  not models with finite loss;
\item \emph{(what a surviving deviation requires)} write
  $I(t):=\int_0^t\Delta(x^v_s,s)(1-s)^{-1}ds$ for the accumulated weighted error, so
  that \eqref{eq:survival-id} reads $e_t=(1-t)I(t)$. Then for any $\Delta$ and any
  $e_\star\in\R^d$,
  \begin{equation*}
  \lim_{t\uparrow1}e_t=e_\star
  \iff
  I(t)=\frac{e_\star}{1-t}+o\big((1-t)^{-1}\big) .
  \end{equation*}
  Since $e_t=(1-t)I(t)$ identically, the behaviour of $e_t$ at the endpoint is exactly
  the behaviour of $I$ at the scale $(1-t)^{-1}$, and the cases are exhaustive: if
  $|I(t)|=o((1-t)^{-1})$ then $e_t\to0$; if $(1-t)I(t)$ converges to some
  $e_\star\ne0$---equivalently, if $I$ sits at that scale \emph{with a limiting
  direction}---then $e_t\to e_\star$; if $(1-t)|I(t)|\to\infty$ then $|e_t|\to\infty$
  and there is no endpoint; and if $e_t=(1-t)I(t)$ has no limit in $\R^d\cup\{\infty\}$,
  bounded or not, there is no endpoint either. In particular a nonzero endpoint deviation \emph{requires} the
  accumulated weighted error to attain exactly the $(1-t)^{-1}$ scale, and to do so in a
  direction that does not cancel: growing more slowly than that scale is annihilated
  by the factor $(1-t)$, whatever the pointwise behaviour of $\Delta$ that produced it.
\end{enumerate}
\end{quote}

\begin{proof}[Proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:survival}]
    We have $e_0=0$, and $\vstarz$ is affine in $x$ with linear part $-\frac1{1-t}I_d$, so
    \begin{align*}
        \dot e_t &= \Delta(x^v_t,t)+\big[\vstarz(x^v_t,t,y^i)-\vstarz(x^\star_t,t,y^i)\big] \\
        &= \Delta(x^v_t,t)-\frac{e_t}{1-t}.
    \end{align*}
    Hence, a.e.,
    \begin{align*}
        \frac d{dt}\Big[\frac{e_t}{1-t}\Big] &= \frac{\dot e_t}{1-t}+\frac{e_t}{(1-t)^2} \\
        &= \frac{\Delta(x^v_t,t)}{1-t},
    \end{align*}
    and integrating with $e_0=0$ gives \eqref{eq:survival-id}; the triangle inequality gives \eqref{eq:survival}.

    (a) By Cauchy--Schwarz,
    \begin{align*}
        |e_t| &\le (1-t)\Big(\int_0^t|\Delta|^2\Big)^{1/2}\Big(\int_0^t\frac{ds}{(1-s)^2}\Big)^{1/2} \\
        &\le (1-t)\,\eps\sqrt{\tfrac t{1-t}} \\
        &= \eps\sqrt{t(1-t)} \\
        &\le \eps\sqrt{1-t}.
    \end{align*}

    (b) Since $\int_0^t(1-s)^{-1-a}ds=\frac1a\big((1-t)^{-a}-1\big)$,
    \begin{align*}
        |e_t| &\le \frac ca\big((1-t)^{1-a}-(1-t)\big) \\
        &= c\,t \le c \qquad (a=1),
    \end{align*}
    and the bound tends to $0$, $c$, $+\infty$ for $a<1$, $a=1$, $a>1$.

    (c) For $\Delta=c(1-t)^{-a}u$, $|u|=1$, the integrand is trajectory-free:
    \begin{equation*}
        e_t =
        \begin{cases}
            \dfrac ca\big((1-t)^{1-a}-(1-t)\big)\,u, & a>0, \\[6pt]
            -c\,(1-t)\log(1-t)\,u, & a=0,
        \end{cases}
    \end{equation*}
    which is \eqref{eq:survival-sharp}. The direction is fixed, so \eqref{eq:survival} is an equality, and with $\epsilon=1-t$,
    \begin{align*}
        |e_t| &= \frac ca\big(\epsilon^{1-a}-\epsilon\big) \\
        &\to 0,\ c,\ \infty \qquad (a<1,\ a=1,\ a>1).
    \end{align*}
    Also $v=\vstarz+\Delta$ is locally Lipschitz with $L_\Delta=0$, so Assumption~\ref{as:lipschitz} holds.

    (d) With $I(t)=\int_0^t\frac{\Delta(x^v_s,s)}{1-s}\,ds$,
    \begin{align*}
        e_t &= (1-t)\,I(t), \\
        e_t-e_\star &= (1-t)\Big[I(t)-\frac{e_\star}{1-t}\Big],
    \end{align*}
    so $e_t\to e_\star$ iff $I(t)=\frac{e_\star}{1-t}+o\big((1-t)^{-1}\big)$, which is \eqref{eq:survival-conv}. The four cases are the behaviours of $(1-t)I(t)$.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Proof of Corollary~\ref{cor:lossform}}
\label{apx:pf-lossform}

\begin{quote}\small
\noindent\textbf{Corollary~\ref{cor:lossform}} (Loss form).
In the setting of Proposition~\ref{prop:survival} and under
Assumption~\ref{as:lipschitz}, let
\[
\mathcal L_i(v)\;:=\;\E_{t\sim\mathrm{Unif}(0,1)}\,\E_{X_0\sim\pi_0}
\big|v(X_t,t,y^i)-U\big|^2,\qquad X_t=(1-t)X_0+tx^i,\ U=x^i-X_0,
\]
be the flow-matching loss at index $i$, and write
$\mathcal E(t):=\big(\E_{X_0}|x^v_t-x^\star_t|^2\big)^{1/2}$ for the root-mean-square
deviation over initial conditions. Then for $t\le T$, and for $\delta$ with $1-\delta\le T$,
\begin{equation*}
\mathcal E(t)\;\le\;e^{L_\Delta t}\,\sqrt{\mathcal L_i(v)}\;\sqrt{t(1-t)},
\qquad\text{so}\qquad
\mathcal E(1-\delta)\;\le\;e^{L_\Delta}\sqrt{\mathcal L_i(v)\,\delta}.
\end{equation*}
Consequently the conditional second moment about the memorised atom obeys
$\big(\E|x^v_{1-\delta}-x^i|^2\big)^{1/2}\le e^{L_\Delta}\sqrt{\mathcal L_i(v)\delta}
+\delta\,\big(\E|X_0-x^i|^2\big)^{1/2}$.
\end{quote}

\begin{proof}[Proof of Corollary~\ref{cor:lossform}]
    $U=\vstarz(X_t,t,y^i)$ a.s.\ (Proposition~\ref{prop:collapse}(a)), so
    \begin{align*}
        \mathcal L_i(v) &= \int_0^1\ell(s)^2\,ds, \\
        \ell(s)^2 &= \E_{X_0}|\Delta(x^\star_s,s)|^2 .
    \end{align*}
    Assume that $x^v$ exists on $[0,T]$ for $\pi_0$-a.e.\ $X_0$, with $T\ge1-\delta$. A pathwise Gr\"onwall gives $|e_t|\le e^{L_\Delta t}\int_0^t|\Delta(x^\star_s,s)|\,ds$, which is in $L^2(X_0)$ because $\ell\in L^2(0,1)$, so $\mathcal E(t)<\infty$.
    Taking $L^2(X_0)$ norms in \eqref{eq:survival} (Minkowski) and using Assumption~\ref{as:lipschitz},
    \begin{align*}
        \mathcal E(t) &\le (1-t)\int_0^t\frac{\|\Delta(x^v_s,s)\|_{L^2}}{1-s}\,ds, \\
        \|\Delta(x^v_s,s)\|_{L^2} &\le \ell(s)+L_\Delta\,\mathcal E(s).
    \end{align*}
    With $F=\frac{\mathcal E}{1-t}$ and $G=\int_0^t\frac{\ell(s)}{1-s}\,ds$,
    \begin{align*}
        F(t) &\le G(t)+L_\Delta\int_0^tF(s)\,ds \\
        \Longrightarrow\ F(t) &\le G(t)\,e^{L_\Delta t},
    \end{align*}
    by Gr\"onwall ($G$ is nondecreasing). By Cauchy--Schwarz,
    \begin{align*}
        G(t) &\le \Big(\int_0^t\ell^2\Big)^{1/2}\Big(\int_0^t\frac{ds}{(1-s)^2}\Big)^{1/2} \\
        &\le \sqrt{\mathcal L_i(v)}\,\sqrt{\tfrac t{1-t}},
    \end{align*}
    so
    \begin{align*}
        \mathcal E(t) &= (1-t)\,F(t) \\
        &\le e^{L_\Delta t}\sqrt{\mathcal L_i(v)}\,\sqrt{t(1-t)},
    \end{align*}
    which is \eqref{eq:lossform} ($t=1-\delta$). Finally $|x^\star_{1-\delta}-x^i|=\delta\,|X_0-x^i|$, and the triangle inequality in $L^2(X_0)$ gives the last display.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:heffid}}
\label{apx:pf-heffid}

\begin{proof}[Proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:heffid}]
Fix $y$ and write $p_i=p_i^{(h)}(y)=\exp(-D_i/2h^2)/Z$ with $Z=\sum_j\exp(-D_j/2h^2)$.
\emph{Derivatives.} $\partial_h(-D_i/2h^2)=D_i/h^3$ and
$\partial_h\log Z=\sum_jp_jD_j/h^3=:\bar D/h^3$, so
\begin{equation}
\partial_hp_i=p_i\,(D_i-\bar D)/h^3 .
\label{eq:pf:heffid:1}
\end{equation}
Hence for any $f$ not depending on $h$,
$\partial_h\sum_ip_if(x^i)=\sum_ip_i(D_i-\bar D)f(x^i)/h^3=\Cov_p(f(x^i),D_i)/h^3$. With
$f(x)=x$ this is the first identity. For the second,
$\tr\Covh=\sum_ip_i|x^i|^2-|\bar x_h|^2$, so
\begin{align*}
h^3\partial_h\tr\Covh
&=\Cov_p(|x^i|^2,D_i)-2\,\bar x_h\cdot\Cov_p(x^i,D_i)
=\Cov_p\big(|x^i|^2-2\bar x_h\cdot x^i+|\bar x_h|^2,\,D_i\big),
\end{align*}
since adding the constant $|\bar x_h|^2$ does not change a covariance, and the first
argument is $|x^i-\bar x_h|^2$.
\emph{Large $h$.} As $h\to\infty$, $p_i=1/N+O(h^{-2})$ uniformly in $i$, $\bar x_h\to\bar x$,
and the covariance is a polynomial in $(p,\bar x_h)$ with bounded coefficients, so
$h^3\partial_h\tr\Covh\to\frac1N\sum_i(|x^i-\bar x|^2-\bar a)(D_i-\bar D_\infty)$ with $\bar a$
and $\bar D_\infty$ the unweighted means; the error is $O(h^{-2})$.
\emph{Stability.} Write $h_\circ=h_{\mathrm{true}}\in\mathcal H$. By optimality of $\hat h$,
$|F(\hat h)-F(h_\circ)-e|=|\log T-F(\hat h)|\le|\log T-F(h_\circ)|=|e|$, so
$|F(\hat h)-F(h_\circ)|\le2|e|$, which is the first claim. If that set lies in
$\mathcal I$, then $\hat h\in\mathcal I$, and by the mean-value theorem applied to
the scalar function $u\cdot F$ on the segment between $h_\circ$ and $\hat h$,
$|F(\hat h)-F(h_\circ)|\ge|u\cdot(F(\hat h)-F(h_\circ))|=|u\cdot F'(\xi)|\,|\hat h-h_\circ|
\ge\kappa|\hat h-h_\circ|$ for some $\xi\in\mathcal I$. Combining the two gives
$|\hat h-h_\circ|\le2|e|/\kappa$. If a grid $\mathcal H$ does not contain
$h_{\mathrm{true}}$, the same argument at the grid point $g$ nearest it, with
$e'=\log T-F(g)$ and the set and interval taken around $g$, with slope bound $\kappa_g$
there, gives $|\hat h-g|\le2|e'|/\kappa_g$, hence
$|\hat h-h_{\mathrm{true}}|\le2|e'|/\kappa_g+|g-h_{\mathrm{true}}|$ (any $g\in\mathcal H$ works).
The first step uses only that $\hat h$ minimises a distance to a curve, in any norm; the
second needs $\|w\|\ge|u\cdot w|$, which the norm $\|w\|=\sum_c|w_c|$ minimised by
$h_{\mathrm{mean}}$ satisfies because it dominates the Euclidean norm. So the argument
applies to $h_{\mathrm{mean}}$ and $G$ with $|e|$ read in that norm.
\end{proof}

The identities are checked against centred finite differences in
\texttt{scripts/theory\_t1\_check.py} (relative error below $10^{-9}$ for
$h\in[0.02,1]$), together with the $O(h^{-2})$ convergence of the large-$h$ limit
(relative deviation $0.47$, $0.03$, $3\times10^{-3}$, $3\times10^{-4}$ at
$h=3,10,30,100$) and the bound against the bootstrap interval widths of all synthetic
checkpoints. On the CIFAR-10 training set ($N{=}2000$, 48 conditions)
$\kappa=17.1$, $3.8$, $0.38$, $0.13$ at $h=3,4,5,6$; on the linear-Gaussian runs the
median $\kappa$ at $h_{\mathrm{eff}}$ is $1.4$ for $h{=}0.5$ against $5$--$33$ for
$h\le0.1$, which is why $h{=}0.5$ synthetic and $h{=}6$ on CIFAR-10 are poorly identified.

\begin{remark}[$\kappa>0$ is a property of the conditions, not of the kernel]
\label{rem:kappa}
Identifiability can fail with the exact Gaussian kernel. With $N=2$, $y^1\ne y^2$, and a
single condition at the midpoint $y_c=\frac12(y^1+y^2)$, $p^{(h)}(y_c)=(\frac12,\frac12)$ for
every $h$, so $\bar x_h(y_c)$ and $\tr\Covh(y_c)$ do not depend on $h$ and $F'=G'=0$. A
sufficient condition can be checked on the training set: since
$\frac{d}{dh}\log\tr\Covh(y_c)=\Cov_p(|x^i-\bar x_h(y_c)|^2,D_i)/(h^3\tr\Covh(y_c))$, if for
one condition $c_0$ this covariance is at least $q>0$ and $\tr\Covh(y_{c_0})\le V_{\max}$
for all $h$ in $\mathcal I\subseteq(0,b]$, then $u=e_{c_0}$ gives $\kappa\ge q/(b^3V_{\max})$.
Writing $\Cov_p(A,D)=\frac12\sum_{i,j}p_ip_j(A_i-A_j)(D_i-D_j)$ shows what the condition
asks: training points farther from the condition in label space must also lie farther
from the reference mean.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:calib}}
\label{apx:pf-calib}

\begin{proof}[Proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:calib}]
\emph{(a)} Let $\widetilde Y=Y+h\varepsilon$ with $\varepsilon\sim\N(0,I_k)$ independent;
given $X=x$, $\widetilde Y\sim\N(g(x),s_h^2I_k)$, so $\widetilde Y$ has the everywhere
positive density $p_{\widetilde Y}(y)=\E\,\N(y;g(X),s_h^2I_k)$. For a bounded continuous
$\varphi$, by Theorem~\ref{thm:endpoint},
\begin{equation*}
\int\varphi\,dp_1^{(h)}(\cdot\mid y)
=\frac{\frac1N\sum_iK_h(y-y^i)\varphi(x^i)}{\frac1N\sum_iK_h(y-y^i)}
\xrightarrow{\text{a.s.}}
\frac{\E[K_h(y-Y)\varphi(X)]}{\E[K_h(y-Y)]}
\end{equation*}
by the strong law of large numbers, since $K_h$ is bounded and the denominator's limit is
positive. Conditioning on $X$ and using $\int K_h(y-y')\N(y';g(x),\sigma^2I)\,dy'=
\N(y;g(x),s_h^2I)$, the numerator is $\E[\varphi(X)\N(y;g(X),s_h^2I)]
=p_{\widetilde Y}(y)\,\E[\varphi(X)\mid\widetilde Y=y]$ and the denominator is
$p_{\widetilde Y}(y)$. Taking $\varphi$ in a countable convergence-determining class gives
weak convergence almost surely.

\emph{(b)} Given $X=x$, $Y\sim\N(g(x),\sigma^2I_k)$ and $\widetilde Y\sim\N(g(x),s_h^2I_k)$,
so the total variation between the laws of $(X,Y)$ and $(X,\widetilde Y)$ is
$\E_X\,\mathrm{TV}(\N(g(X),\sigma^2I),\N(g(X),s_h^2I))=\tau_h$, by translation
invariance. The function $(x,y)\mapsto\ind\{x\in R(y)\}$ is measurable by assumption and
takes values in $[0,1]$, so its expectations under the two laws differ by at most $\tau_h$. By Pinsker's inequality and
$\mathrm{KL}(\N(0,\sigma^2I_k)\,\|\,\N(0,s_h^2I_k))=\frac k2(r-1-\log r)$ with
$r=\sigma^2/s_h^2=1/(1+u)$, $u=h^2/\sigma^2$, we get $\tau_h^2\le\frac k4(r-1-\log r)
=\frac k4\big(\log(1+u)-\frac u{1+u}\big)\le\frac k8u^2$, the last step because the
bracket vanishes at $u=0$ and has derivative $u/(1+u)^2\le u$. Finally, if
$\pi_h(R(y)\mid y)=\alpha$ for every $y$, then
$\Pr(X\in R(\widetilde Y))=\E[\pi_h(R(\widetilde Y)\mid\widetilde Y)]=\alpha$. Such regions
exist when $\pi$ has no atoms: then $\pi_h(\cdot\mid y)\ll\pi$ has none, its image under a
Borel isomorphism $\psi$ of $\R^d$ with $[0,1]$ has a continuous distribution function
$G_y$, and $R(y)=\psi^{-1}\big([0,G_y^{-1}(\alpha)]\big)$ is a measurable choice. If $\pi$
has atoms, $\pi_h(\cdot\mid y)$ carries them at every $y$ and an exact level need not exist;
the first inequality still holds.

\emph{(c)} $\widetilde Y=AX+\tilde\eta$ with $\tilde\eta\sim\N(0,s_h^2I_k)$, so Gaussian
conditioning gives $\pi_h(\cdot\mid y)=\N(\mu_h(y),\Sigma_h)$. $\mu_h$ is affine,
$\mu_h(\widetilde Y)-\mu_h(Y)=h\Gamma_h\varepsilon$, and
$X-\mu_h(\widetilde Y)\sim\N(0,\Sigma_h)$. Since $X-\mu_h(Y)$ is a function of $(X,\eta)$,
which is independent of $\varepsilon$,
\begin{equation*}
\Sigma_h=\Cov\big(X-\mu_h(Y)-h\Gamma_h\varepsilon\big)=\Cov\big(X-\mu_h(Y)\big)+h^2\Gamma_h\Gamma_h^\top,
\end{equation*}
and $X-\mu_h(Y)$ is Gaussian with mean $\E X-\mu_h(\E Y)=0$, which is the claim. Write
$z=\Sigma_h^{-1/2}(X-\mu_h(Y))\sim\N(0,I-h^2MM^\top)$ with
$M=\Sigma_h^{-1/2}\Gamma_h$. The eigenvalues $\lambda_j$ of $I-h^2MM^\top$ lie in $[0,1]$, so
$|z|^2\overset{d}{=}\sum_j\lambda_jg_j^2\le\sum_jg_j^2\sim\chi^2_d$ for i.i.d.\ standard
normal $g_j$, and $\Pr(|z|^2\le q_\alpha)\ge\Pr(\chi^2_d\le q_\alpha)=\alpha$. The gain is
exactly $\Pr\big(\sum_i\lambda_ig_i^2\le q_\alpha<\sum_ig_i^2\big)$. If $h>0$ and $A\ne0$
then $\Gamma_h\ne0$ and some $\lambda_j<1$; for $\alpha\in(0,1)$, $0<q_\alpha<\infty$, and the
event contains the nonempty open set of $g$ with
$g_j^2\in(q_\alpha,q_\alpha/\lambda_j)$ (read $q_\alpha/0=\infty$) and
$\sum_{i\ne j}g_i^2<q_\alpha-\lambda_jg_j^2$, which has positive Gaussian probability. So the
coverage is strictly larger than $\alpha$.

\emph{(d)} Let the weights $w_i\ge0$, $\sum_iw_i=1$, be fixed and let $a_i$ be i.i.d.\ with
mean $m$ and covariance $S$. Then
$\Cov(\sum_iw_ia_i)=\sum_iw_i^2S=S/n_{\mathrm{eff}}$, and with $\bar a=\sum_iw_ia_i$,
\begin{equation*}
\E\sum_iw_i(a_i-\bar a)(a_i-\bar a)^\top
=\E\sum_iw_i(a_i-m)(a_i-m)^\top-\E(\bar a-m)(\bar a-m)^\top
=S-S/n_{\mathrm{eff}} .
\end{equation*}
If $n_{\mathrm{eff}}=1$ then $\sum_iw_i^2=\sum_iw_i$ with $w_i\in[0,1]$, so one weight is
$1$; the weighted covariance is $0$, the moment-matched region is the single point $\bar a$,
and an absolutely continuous $x^\star$ independent of the atoms falls in it with
probability $0$ (Fubini).
\end{proof}

Each part is checked by simulation in \texttt{scripts/theory\_t3\_coverage.py}: the
large-$N$ mixture against $(\mu_h,\Sigma_h)$ (mean error $\le0.018$, covariance error
$\le0.75\%$ at $N=4\times10^5$); the identity of (c) to $10^{-15}$ and the coverage of
$\N(\mu_h,\Sigma_h)$ against Monte Carlo to $0.002$; and the two identities of (d) to
$2\times10^{-3}$. Part (b) is checked in \texttt{scripts/theory\_t3\_general.py} with
exact highest-density regions of the mixture posteriors of EXP-2 ($d=2$ with a
projection, and $d=8$; 1000 pairs per cell, $h/\sigma\in\{0,\frac12,1,2,4\}$): the
coverage at clean and at noisy labels differs by at most $0.96\,\tau_h$ (at
$h=\sigma/2$) and by much less at larger $h$, the coverage at noisy labels is within
$0.031$ of $\alpha$, and the coverage at clean labels is at least $\alpha$ in every
cell, so the over-coverage of (c) persists for these non-Gaussian posteriors.

\section{Interventions, the large-sample limit, and approximate fields}
\label{app:interventions}

This appendix holds the statements summarised in Section~\ref{sec:interventions};
their proofs are in Appendix~\ref{app:proofs}.

\subsection{A finite-sample coverage bound for label-weighted mixtures}
\label{apx:thmB}

Theorem~\ref{thm:calib} describes the reference law as $N\to\infty$ (part (a)) or with
fixed weights (part (d)). The bound below holds at finite $N$ for any weights computed
from the labels alone, Nadaraya--Watson weights at any bandwidth among them. Let
$(x^i,y^i)$ be i.i.d., $(X^*,Y^*)$ a further independent draw, $s:\R^d\to\R$ measurable,
and $F(u\mid y)$ the conditional CDF of $s(X)$ given $Y=y$ under a fixed regular
conditional law $\kappa(y,\cdot)$. Let $p_i(y)\ge0$, $\sum_ip_i(y)=1$, be jointly
measurable functions of $y$ and the labels $y^1,\dots,y^N$ only, and write
$\widehat F(u\mid y)=\sum_ip_i(y)\mathbf 1\{s(x^i)\le u\}$,
$n_{\mathrm{eff}}(y)=(\sum_ip_i(y)^2)^{-1}$, $r(y)=\sum_ip_i(y)|y^i-y|$ and
$a_i=\E_{Y^*}p_i(Y^*)$. For $\alpha\in(0,1)$ let $q_{\mathrm{lo}}(y),q_{\mathrm{hi}}(y)$ be
measurable $\beta$-quantiles of $\widehat F(\cdot\mid y)$ (any $q$ with
$\widehat F(q-\mid y)\le\beta\le\widehat F(q\mid y)$) at $\beta=\frac{1\mp\alpha}2$, and
$C_\alpha=\Pr\big(s(X^*)\in[q_{\mathrm{lo}}(Y^*),q_{\mathrm{hi}}(Y^*)]\big)$ with the
training set fixed.

\begin{theorem}[Coverage of label-weighted mixtures]
\label{thm:labelmix}
Assume $u\mapsto F(u\mid y)$ is continuous for every $y$ and
$|F(u\mid y)-F(u\mid y')|\le L|y-y'|$ for all $u,y,y'$. Then for every $\delta\in(0,1)$,
with probability at least $1-\delta$ over the training set, simultaneously for all
$\alpha\in(0,1)$,
\[
|C_\alpha-\alpha|\le2L\,\E\,r(Y^*)+8\,\E\,n_{\mathrm{eff}}(Y^*)^{-1/2}
+\sqrt{2a_{\max}\log(1/\delta)},\qquad a_{\max}=\max_ia_i .
\]
Without continuity of $F(\cdot\mid y)$, the lower bound $C_\alpha\ge\alpha-(\cdot)$ still
holds.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
\emph{Quantiles.} If $\sup_u|\widehat G-G|\le D$ and $q_\beta$ is a $\beta$-quantile of
$\widehat G$, then $G(q_\beta)\ge\beta-D$ and $G(q_\beta-)\le\beta+D$ (the bound passes to
left limits), and $q_{\beta_-}\le q_{\beta_+}$ for $\beta_\pm=\frac{1\pm\alpha}2$. So
$Z\sim G$ falls in $[q_{\beta_-},q_{\beta_+}]$ with probability at least $\alpha-2D$, and
at most $\alpha+2D$ when $G$ is continuous. With
$D(y)=\sup_u|\widehat F(u\mid y)-F(u\mid y)|$ and the disintegration of the law of
$(X^*,Y^*)$ through $\kappa$, $|C_\alpha-\alpha|\le2\,\E D(Y^*)$ for every $\alpha$.

\emph{Bias and fluctuation.} With $\xi_i(u)=\mathbf 1\{s(x^i)\le u\}-F(u\mid y^i)$,
$\widehat F-F=\sum_ip_i\xi_i+\sum_ip_i\big(F(u\mid y^i)-F(u\mid y)\big)$, and the second
sum is at most $L\,r(y)$ in absolute value. Given the labels, the $x^i$ are independent with
$x^i\sim\kappa(y^i,\cdot)$, so the $\xi_i$ are independent and centred. Symmetrisation
with Rademacher signs, then ordering the $s(x^i)$ (ties by index), turns
$\sup_u|\sum_i\varepsilon_ip_i\mathbf 1\{s(x^i)\le u\}|$ into the maximum of the partial
sums of a martingale, and Doob's $L^2$ inequality gives
$\E[\sup_u|\sum_ip_i\xi_i(u)|\mid\text{labels}]\le4(\sum_ip_i^2)^{1/2}$. So
$\Phi:=\E_{Y^*}\sup_u|\sum_ip_i(Y^*)\xi_i(u)|$ has conditional mean at most
$4\,\E\,n_{\mathrm{eff}}(Y^*)^{-1/2}$.

\emph{Concentration.} Changing one $x^i$ moves $\Phi$ by at most $a_i$, so by McDiarmid's
inequality, given the labels, $\Phi\le\E[\Phi\mid\text{labels}]
+(\frac12\sum_ia_i^2\log\frac1\delta)^{1/2}$ with probability at least $1-\delta$, and
$\sum_ia_i^2\le a_{\max}$ because $\sum_ia_i=1$. Combining with
$|C_\alpha-\alpha|\le2L\,\E r+2\Phi$ gives the claim, on an event that does not involve
$\alpha$.
\end{proof}

\paragraph{Selecting a bandwidth from responses.}
The theorem requires weights determined by labels alone at each fixed bandwidth.
If a bandwidth is selected using responses or generated samples, apply the theorem
simultaneously to a fixed grid $\mathcal H$ of $G$ bandwidths, with failure probability
$\delta/G$ at each point. A conditional union bound then replaces
$\log(1/\delta)$ by $\log(G/\delta)$ and permits any measurable selection from
that grid. A continuous search needs a separate uniform argument.

\paragraph{On the linear-Gaussian problem.} With $s(x)=x_1$ on the problem of
Section~\ref{sec:exp1} ($d=2$, $k=1$, $\sigma=0.1$), $F(\cdot\mid y)$ is Gaussian with mean
linear in $y$, so $L$ is exact. Over five training sets for each
$N\in\{200,2000,10^4\}$, 25 bandwidths in $[0.003,3]$ and $\alpha\in\{0.5,0.9\}$, the bound
never fails (0/750 cases beyond two Monte Carlo standard errors at 4000 test pairs), but it
is loose: its minimum over $h$ is $2.34$, $1.07$ and $0.62$, while the best measured
$|C_{0.9}-0.9|$ is $0.007$, $0.002$ and $0.004$. A factor of about $5.6$ is lost after the
first step ($2\,\E D(Y^*)$ is that much smaller), in the constant of the fluctuation term at
small $h$ and in $2L\,r$ at large $h$. What the bound does carry is the location of the
window: its minimiser, $h=0.40$, $0.17$ and $0.095$, lies a factor $1.8$ above the
bandwidth with the smallest measured error at every $N$, and both shrink as $N$ grows.
Marginal coverage cannot see over-smoothing, so at large $h$ the measured error is small
again ($0.03$ at $h=3$) while the bound, which controls the conditional CDF, grows
(\texttt{scripts/theory\_b\_check.py}).

\subsection{Escaping the obstruction: smoothing the endpoint}
\label{sec:theory-remedy}

Proposition~\ref{prop:atomicity} rules out any remedy leaving the endpoint law on the
training atoms: label smoothing, and trivially the interpolant noise of
Section~\ref{sec:theory-noise}, since $\gamma(1)=0$. A remedy must move the support. Of
the three places to inject noise (label, path, endpoint), only the endpoint does.

\begin{proposition}[Endpoint smoothing]
\label{prop:tgtnoise}
Fix $\rho\ge0$ and replace the endpoint by $X_1=x^I+\rho\,\xi$ with $\xi\sim\N(0,I_d)$ independent of
$(I,X_0,\varepsilon)$ and redrawn every step, keeping label bandwidth $h>0$. Write
$s_t^2=(1-t)^2+t^2\rho^2$. Then $\vstar$ is again a weighted average of per-atom
fields, now with spatial weights $\varphi_{s_t}(x-tx^i)$ in place of
$\pi_0(\tfrac{x-tx^i}{1-t})$ (explicitly, \eqref{eq:tgtfield} in the appendix), and
the endpoint law is
\begin{equation}
p_1(\cdot\mid y)=\sum_i p_i^{(h)}(y)\,\N(x^i,\rho^2I_d),
\qquad
\text{mean}=\bar x_h(y),\quad \Cov=\Covh(y)+\rho^2I_d.
\label{eq:tgtendpoint}
\end{equation}
For every $\rho>0$ this law is absolutely continuous, so \eqref{eq:w2floor} does not
apply to it; moreover $s_1=\rho>0$, so $\vstar$ extends continuously to $t=1$ and the
$1/(1-t)$ blow-up of $(\star)$ disappears. \proofin{apx:pf-tgtnoise}
\end{proposition}

Two caveats, both quantified in Section~\ref{sec:exp1}. Removing a lower bound does not
reduce the distance: \eqref{eq:tgtendpoint} is a kernel density estimate that pays
$+\rho^2I_d$ of variance for its continuity. The atom obstruction is a lower bound,
not an upper bound on the gain from smoothing a particular mixture. For example,
$P=(\delta_{-1}+\delta_1)/2$ has zero nearest-atom obstruction, but smoothing
$Q=\delta_{-1}$ by Gaussian noise of standard deviation $\sqrt{2/\pi}$ reduces
$W_2^2(Q,P)$ by $2/\pi$: monotone coupling gives the smoothed cost
$2+\rho^2-2\rho\sqrt{2/\pi}$.
Also, simultaneous moment matching requires some $h$ with
$\bar x_h=\mu_{\mathrm{post}}$ and
$\Sigma_{\mathrm{post}}-\Covh=\rho^2I_d\succeq0$; two scalar controls do not
guarantee these vector and matrix equations.

One place in this paper leaves the fixed-$\D$ setting. It asks what $\Covh$ converges to
when the training set is an i.i.d.\ sample and $N\to\infty$, the only regime in which
label smoothing is more than a reweighting. Under local homoscedasticity ($\Sigma$ constant near $y$), $h\to0$ and $Nh^{k+4}\to\infty$,
Proposition~\ref{prop:expansion} (appendix) gives
$\Covh(y)=\Sigma(y)+h^2J(y)J(y)^\top+O(h^4)+O_P((Nh^k)^{-1/2})$. The between-group
inflation $h^2\|J\|_F^2$ produces the over-smoothing branch of the empirical U-curve and
is a parameter-free prediction in the linear-Gaussian model, where $\mu$ is affine. It
should not be expected to describe an image model, and the across-condition slope of
$0.481$ in Section~\ref{sec:cifarddpm} is what that failure to transfer looks like.
Proposition~\ref{prop:nwrate} gives the companion Nadaraya--Watson rates for
$\bar x_h(y)$ at a fixed interior query, separating population bias from sampling
error. This joint $N\to\infty$, $h\to0$ limit differs from the fixed-$N$ collapse
of Corollary~\ref{cor:zerobw}; a query selected from the training labels additionally
requires a random-design-point argument.

\subsection{Stochastic interpolant noise does not help}
\label{sec:theory-noise}

Another place to inject randomness is the interpolant path itself. This never changes the
population endpoint at $h=0$, at any noise level. Replace the interpolant by $X_t=(1-t)X_0+tX_1+\gamma(t)Z$ with
$Z\sim\N(0,I_d)$ independent and $\gamma(t)=\sigma\sqrt{t(1-t)}$, so
$\gamma(0)=\gamma(1)=0$. A single $Z$ defines the whole trajectory, and the pathwise-derivative target is
\begin{equation}
\dot X_t=X_1-X_0+\dot\gamma(t)Z,\qquad
\dot\gamma(t)=\frac{\sigma(1-2t)}{2\sqrt{t(1-t)}};
\label{eq:c2target}
\end{equation}
regressing on $X_1-X_0$ alone would be a different objective. For $\sigma>0$ this target is
not square integrable ($\dot\gamma^2\sim\sigma^2/4t$ near $t=0$), nor is its conditional
variance, so the unweighted loss is infinite for every $v$ (Corollary~\ref{cor:prop18}). The population field is then the
minimiser of the loss with time weight $t(1-t)$, which is finite since
$t(1-t)\dot\gamma^2=\sigma^2(1-2t)^2/4$; a positive weight that depends on $t$ alone leaves
the minimiser equal to the per-time conditional mean. Write
$s_t^2:=(1-t)^2+\gamma(t)^2=(1-t)[(1-t)+\sigma^2t]$, so $s_0=1$ and $s_1=0$.

\begin{proposition}[Closed form and endpoint invariance in $\sigma$]
\label{prop:prop17}
Let $h=0$ and $v_\sigma^\star=\E[\dot X_t\mid X_t,t,Y]$ for target
\eqref{eq:c2target}, the a.e.-unique minimiser of $\E\big[t(1-t)\,|v-\dot X_t|^2\big]$. Then
for $t\in(0,1)$:
(a) $v_\sigma^\star(x,t,y^i)=x^i+c(t)(x-tx^i)$ with
$c(t)=\frac{-(1-t)+\frac{\sigma^2}2(1-2t)}{s_t^2}=\frac12\frac{d}{dt}\log s_t^2$;
(b) the flow is $x_t=t\,x^i+s_t\,x_0$; and
(c) since $s_1=0$,
\begin{equation}
p_1^{(0)}(\cdot\mid y^i)=\delta_{x^i}\quad\text{for every }\sigma\ge0.
\label{eq:invariance}
\end{equation} \proofin{apx:pf-prop17}
\end{proposition}

Part~(b) gives the flow in closed form and (c) follows because $s_1=0$, so no
integrator and no early stopping enter the argument: the endpoint is fixed by the
interpolant itself. The reason is structural. For a continuous additive Gaussian schedule, the condition $\gamma(t)\to0$ as $t\uparrow1$ removes the interpolant noise in the endpoint limit. For $\gamma=\sigma\sqrt{t(1-t)}$ interpolant noise cannot change the endpoint law, and
any continuous additive Gaussian schedule with $\gamma(t)\to0$ as $t\uparrow1$ has interpolant limit $X_t\to x^I$ (Remark~\ref{rem:notsampler} and
Figure~\ref{fig:interpolant}, appendix).
Corollary~\ref{cor:prop18} records what it does change: it raises the irreducible error
and halves the Lipschitz blow-up rate, and neither touches $p_1$.
Proposition~\ref{prop:factors} locates the difference: the two noises multiply different
factors of the same index posterior, and only the label factor survives to $t=1$. This
distinguishes this exact conditional endpoint calculation from the stochasticity and finite-pair attainability mechanisms studied in the unconditional setting by \citet{gradvar2025}.

\subsection{Classifier-free guidance does not escape either}
\label{sec:theory-cfg}

For the classifier-free guided field \citep{ho2022classifier}
$v_w:=(1+w)\vstar-w\,v_{\mathrm{unc}}^\star$, the trajectory's
component orthogonal to $\operatorname{aff}\{x^1,\dots,x^N\}$ decays as $(1-t)$, so every
guided endpoint law lies in the affine hull of the atoms, a null set when $N\le d$, and
every trajectory converges, with an atomic endpoint almost surely under an
absolutely continuous source
(Propositions~\ref{prop:cfg} and~\ref{prop:cfgatom}, appendix).

The four interventions thus fall into an order: inert (interpolant noise, at every noise
level), atom-preserving (guidance), reweighting (label smoothing, so the atomicity ceiling
still binds), and support-moving (endpoint smoothing, at $+\rho^2I_d$ of variance).

\subsection{What survives the flow, and what paces the collapse}
\label{sec:survival}

For a model with velocity error $\Delta$ against $(\star)$, the deviation of its
trajectory from the exact one is $e_t=(1-t)I(t)$ with
$I(t)=\int_0^t\Delta(x_s,s)(1-s)^{-1}ds$ (Proposition~\ref{prop:survival}, appendix): the
collapsed field contracts at a diverging rate, so a deviation survives to the endpoint
only through an error accumulating at rate $(1-t)^{-1}$. Under a Lipschitz hypothesis on
the error that holds only for fields reproducing the linear part of $(\star)$, it is
bounded by the loss not yet removed (Corollary~\ref{cor:lossform}; empirical slope
$0.485$ against $\tfrac12$, Appendix~\ref{app:survival}).

\subsection{Scope}
\label{sec:scope}

The theory describes the empirical minimiser, not how many SGD steps reach it, nor how
observed collapse depends on $N$ at fixed architecture (a non-collapsing $N{=}5000$ run
probes representation and optimisation), on $\sigma_{\mathrm{obs}}$, or on $h$ on a
given instance. It predicts the destination, not the speed
(Figure~\ref{fig:collapsetime}, appendix); Section~\ref{sec:heff} measures how far a
trained model has gone.

\section{Finite-sample memorisation and exact non-representability}
\label{sec:theory-d2}

This subsection adapts a finite-batch representability argument from the unconditional gradient-variance literature~\citep{gradvar2025} and adds an exact non-representability observation: (i)~why a fixed finite \emph{unconditional} batch already admits a
zero-loss memorising field despite Proposition~\ref{prop:uncond}'s positive
population loss, and (ii)~why no finite network can exactly represent the collapsed
field $(\star)$. Unlike Sections~\ref{sec:theory-hard}--\ref{sec:theory-floor}, the
finite sample itself is treated as random here.

\begin{lemma}[Generic non-intersection of finite interpolant segments]
\label{lem:nonintersect}
Let $x_0^1,\dots,x_0^N\stackrel{\mathrm{iid}}{\sim}\pi_0$ and, independently,
$z^1,\dots,z^N\stackrel{\mathrm{iid}}{\sim}\rho_1$ be $N$ unconditional
source--target pairs (no labels), both laws absolutely continuous on $\R^d$, and
$\ell_i(t)=(1-t)x_0^i+tz^i$, $t\in[0,1]$.
(a) If $d\ge2$: for any fixed $i\ne j$,
$\Pr[\exists\,t\in(0,1):\ell_i(t)=\ell_j(t)]=0$.
(b) If $d>2$: for any fixed $i\ne j$,
$\Pr[\exists\,t_i,t_j\in(0,1),\,t_i\ne t_j:\ell_i(t_i)=\ell_j(t_j)]=0$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}[Proof of Lemma~\ref{lem:nonintersect}]
    (a) We have $\ell_i(\hat t)=\ell_j(\hat t)$ iff $z^j=z^i+\frac{1-\hat t}{\hat t}(x_0^i-x_0^j)$. With $s=\frac{1-t}t\in(0,\infty)$,
    \begin{align*}
        \{\exists\,t:\ell_i(t)=\ell_j(t)\} &= \{z^j\in R\}, \\
        R &= \{z^i+s\,(x_0^i-x_0^j):s>0\}.
    \end{align*}
    $R$ is a half-line (a point if $x_0^i=x_0^j$), hence Lebesgue-null for $d\ge2$; $z^j\perp(x_0^i,x_0^j,z^i)$ has an absolutely continuous law, so the probability is $0$. A union bound over pairs finishes (a).

    (b) Here
    \begin{equation*}
        z^j\in\Big\{\big[(1-t_i)x_0^i+t_iz^i-(1-t_j)x_0^j\big]/t_j:\ (t_i,t_j)\in(0,1)^2\Big\},
    \end{equation*}
    a countable union of Lipschitz images of bounded planar sets, of Hausdorff dimension $\le2<d$, hence null.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[A memorising field always exists on a fixed finite unconditional batch]
\label{prop:memorize}
Fix $N$ unconditional pairs $\{(x_0^i,z^i)\}_{i=1}^N$ as in Lemma~\ref{lem:nonintersect}
with $d>2$ and the $z^i$ pairwise distinct a.s., and sample $m$ time points
$t^{(i,j)}\sim\mathrm{Unif}(0,1)$, $j=1,\dots,m$, independently across $(i,j)$. Let
$X_t^{(i,j)}=(1-t^{(i,j)})x_0^i+t^{(i,j)}z^i$ and
\[
L_{\mathrm{MC}}^{\mathrm{unc}}(v)=\frac1{Nm}\sum_{i=1}^N\sum_{j=1}^m
\big|(z^i-x_0^i)-v\big(X_t^{(i,j)},t^{(i,j)}\big)\big|^2.
\]
Then, almost surely over the draw of $\{(x_0^i,z^i)\}$ and $\{t^{(i,j)}\}$, some
$v$ attains $L_{\mathrm{MC}}^{\mathrm{unc}}(v)=0$---even though
$\inf_v L_{\mathrm{unc}}(v)>0$ at the population level whenever the $z^i$ are not
all equal (Proposition~\ref{prop:uncond}).
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}[Proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:memorize}]
    By Lemma~\ref{lem:nonintersect}(a)--(b) and a union bound over $\binom N2$ pairs, a.s.\ the segments $\{\ell_i(t)\}$ are pairwise disjoint, so $(X_t^{(i,j)},t^{(i,j)})$ never coincide across $i$ and $i(x,t)$ is defined on the sample. Set
    \begin{align*}
        v(x,t) &= z^{i(x,t)}-x_0^{i(x,t)} \quad\text{on the sample}, \\
        v(x,t) &= 0 \quad\text{elsewhere}.
    \end{align*}
    Then
    \begin{align*}
        v\big(X_t^{(i,j)},t^{(i,j)}\big) &= z^i-x_0^i \quad\forall(i,j), \\
        L_{\mathrm{MC}}^{\mathrm{unc}}(v) &= 0 .\qedhere
    \end{align*}
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Fixed-table interpolation and fresh draws]
\label{rem:resample}
Proposition~\ref{prop:memorize} constructs a field after observing a fixed finite sample. If that finite lookup table is then held fixed, an independently resampled input almost surely lies outside its entries. This distinguishes empirical interpolation from performance on fresh draws, but does not prove that resampling drives SGD to the population optimum. Indeed, a measurable table chosen after an entire countable stream can fit all its distinct inputs simultaneously. Generalisation requires additional restrictions on the fitted class and its training procedure. The larger-$N$ trend in P6 (Table~\ref{tab:sweeps}) is therefore an empirical observation, not a consequence of this lookup construction. At an identifying conditional label, the explicit population collapse field fits every source draw (Proposition~\ref{prop:collapse}); unconditional population memorisation instead concerns the full empirical target law (Proposition~\ref{prop:uncond}).
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[The collapse field cannot be exactly represented by a finite network]
\label{lem:norepr}
Let $g(t)=1/(1-t)$ on $[0,1)$ and let $f_\theta:\R\to\R$ be any finite-depth,
finite-width feedforward network with finite real weights, built from affine layers
and globally Lipschitz activations (this covers ReLU, leaky-ReLU, tanh, sigmoid,
GELU/SiLU, softplus, and every activation used in our experiments). Then
$\sup_{t\in[0,1)}|f_\theta(t)-g(t)|=\infty$.
\end{remark}
\begin{proof}[Proof of Remark~\ref{lem:norepr}]
    $f_\theta$ is a composition of finitely many affine maps and Lipschitz activations, hence $K$-Lipschitz with $K<\infty$. For $t_0\in[0,1)$,
    \begin{align*}
        |f_\theta(t)| &\le |f_\theta(t_0)|+K\,|t-t_0| \\
        &\le |f_\theta(t_0)|+K \qquad (t\in[0,1)),
    \end{align*}
    so $f_\theta$ is bounded on $[0,1)$, whereas $g(t)=\frac1{1-t}\to\infty$. Hence $\sup_{t\in[0,1)}|f_\theta(t)-g(t)|=\infty$.
\end{proof}

For $x\ne x^i$ fixed, $t\mapsto v_0^\star(x,t,y^i)=(x^i-x)/(1-t)$ has exactly this
$1/(1-t)$ blow-up along the direction $x^i-x$, so Remark~\ref{lem:norepr} (applied
coordinatewise to that direction) shows that \emph{no} finite network of the kind
used in Section~\ref{sec:experiments} can represent $v_0^\star$ \emph{exactly} on
all of $[0,1)$: for every fixed parameter vector $\theta$ there is some $(x,t)$ at
which $v_\theta\ne v_0^\star$. This does not contradict universal approximation,
which guarantees only \emph{uniform} approximation on \emph{compact} sets: every
$[0,1-\delta]$, $\delta>0$, is disjoint from the singularity at $t=1$---exactly the
regime the sampler must traverse to reach the collapse point $x^i$, and exactly
where the ODE solver's $t=1$ handling matters in practice. We stop short, however,
of claiming this pointwise non-representability forces
$L^\star_{\mathrm{model}}-L^\star_{\mathrm{all}}>0$ for an architecture whose
weights (hence Lipschitz constant) are unconstrained: pointwise blow-up on a
shrinking neighbourhood of $t=1$ is compatible, in principle, with a sequence of
increasingly ill-conditioned finite networks driving the \emph{expected} loss
$L_0(v_\theta)$ arbitrarily close to $0$ (consistent with the growing empirical
Lipschitz constant we measure in Figure~\ref{fig:lipschitz}). The genuine,
quantitative loss floor we prove, $d/(3L)$, therefore applies only to the
Lipschitz-capped class $\mathcal F_L$ of Corollary~\ref{cor:floor}, and should not
be read as a statement about arbitrary finite architectures.


\section{Material deferred from the main text}
\label{app:deferred}

This appendix collects material the main text quotes but does not have room to state.
We begin with the remark Section~\ref{sec:theory-noise} points to.

\begin{remark}[This is not an artefact of how we integrate]
\label{rem:notsampler}
Part~(b) of Proposition~\ref{prop:prop17} gives the flow in \emph{closed form} and
part~(c) follows because $s_1=0$: no integrator appears in the argument and no early
stopping, so the endpoint is pinned by the interpolant itself. For a continuous additive Gaussian schedule, $\gamma(t)\to0$ as $t\uparrow1$ ensures that the interpolant converges to the prescribed empirical target. When its conditional velocity has a well-posed flow and uniquely transports these marginals, that flow has the same weak endpoint law. The value $\gamma(1)=0$ alone is insufficient without an endpoint-limit condition. Proposition~\ref{prop:prop17} verifies the flow and the limit explicitly for the specified square-root schedule; it makes no claim about arbitrary learned fields.
\end{remark}

The following material supplies the statements and discussions deferred from
the main text, in the order in which they are used.

\begin{figure}[htb]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.72\textwidth]{fig_window.png}
\caption{The bandwidth window of Proposition~\ref{prop:nearduplicate}. With labels
in clusters of size $|C|$ at within-cluster scale $\varepsilon$ and between-cluster
scale $\Delta$, $n_{\mathrm{eff}}$ plateaus at $|C|$ exactly while
$\varepsilon\ll h\ll\Delta$: the model behaves as though conditioned on the cluster,
and the effective sample size counts the cluster rather than the dataset. The
lowest curve is the case that matters for the hypothesis (clusters loose relative
to their separation ($\varepsilon=0.3$)) where there is \emph{no} plateau at all
and $n_{\mathrm{eff}}$ runs from $1$ to $N$ with no usable $h$ in between.}
\label{fig:window}
\end{figure}

% ---- near-duplicate proposition ----
\begin{proposition}[Near-duplicate labels]
\label{prop:nearduplicate}
Fix $h>0$, a query $y$, and a nonempty subset $C\subseteq\{1,\dots,N\}$ with
$|y-y^i|\le\varepsilon$ for $i\in C$ and $|y-y^j|\ge\Delta$ for $j\notin C$, where
$0\le\varepsilon<\Delta$. For the Gaussian kernel of bandwidth $h$, put
\[
\eta:=\frac{N-|C|}{|C|}\exp\!\Big(\!-\frac{\Delta^2-\varepsilon^2}{2h^2}\Big),
\qquad
\Lambda:=\exp\!\Big(\frac{\varepsilon^2}{2h^2}\Big)\ \ge 1 .
\]
Then \emph{(a)} $\sum_{j\notin C}p_j^{(h)}(y)\le\eta$; \emph{(b)}
$p_i^{(h)}(y)/p_{i'}^{(h)}(y)\le\Lambda$ for all $i,i'\in C$; and \emph{(c)}
$n_{\mathrm{eff}}(y)\ge|C|/(\Lambda+|C|\eta^2)$.
Consequently, as $\varepsilon/h\to0$ and $\Delta/h\to\infty$ the index posterior
converges to the uniform law on $C$, the endpoint law to
$|C|^{-1}\sum_{i\in C}\delta_{x^i}$, and $n_{\mathrm{eff}}\to|C|$.
\end{proposition}

Figure~\ref{fig:window} plots the two bounds against $h$ and marks the usable window
they leave.

% ---- two-factor separation proposition ----
\begin{proposition}[The two mechanisms act on different factors]
\label{prop:factors}
Let the atoms be not all equal and $y$ have a unique nearest label $i^\star$. Label smoothing
changes the endpoint law: $p_1^{(h)}(\cdot\mid y)$ tends to $\delta_{x^{i^\star}}$ as
$h\to0$ and to $\rhohat_X$ as $h\to\infty$, and these differ. Interpolant noise does not:
although it replaces the spatial factor $\N(tx^i,(1-t)^2I_d)$ of Lemma~\ref{lem:cov} by
$\N(tx^i,s_t^2I_d)$, identically for every $i$, $p_1^{(0)}(\cdot\mid y^i)=\delta_{x^i}$ for
every $\sigma\ge0$ (Proposition~\ref{prop:prop17}). The two remedies are therefore not
interchangeable.
\end{proposition}

% ---- finite-sample memorisation summary subsection ----
\subsection{Finite-sample memorisation (summary)}
\label{sec:d2summary}

Appendix~\ref{sec:theory-d2} adapts the finite-batch representability argument of the
unconditional gradient-variance literature~\citep{gradvar2025}: a fixed finite
unconditional batch admits a zero-loss measurable field despite the positive
population loss (Proposition~\ref{prop:memorize}). A table fitted to that finite
batch does not generalise to fresh draws, but this observation alone neither
excludes interpolation of a countable stream nor proves SGD convergence
(Remark~\ref{rem:resample}). The appendix also records that no finite-depth, finite-width network with
globally Lipschitz activations can equal the collapsed field's $1/(1-t)$ blow-up
\emph{pointwise} on all of $[0,1)$ (Remark~\ref{lem:norepr}). We state it as a
remark rather than a contribution because its reach is narrow: our sampler, like every
practical one, stops at $t=1-10^{-3}$, where $1/(1-t)$ is merely $10^3$ and a finite
network represents it comfortably. It rules out exact representation on the open
interval, not adequacy on the interval anyone integrates over. We keep that
pointwise claim separate from a loss floor: the only class for which we prove a
strictly positive floor is the Lipschitz-capped $\mathcal F_L$ of
Corollary~\ref{cor:floor}. The contrast with Proposition~\ref{prop:collapse} is
the point---conditional collapse needs no genericity argument and no fixed-batch
caveat, because $y^i$ pins down $i$ for every $x_0$, not merely with probability
one on a single draw.

\subsection{Scope: what is and is not predicted}

The following EXP-1 table records the finite-training observables quoted in the main text. We then discuss the separate CIFAR-10 instance and the distinction between numerical samples and exact endpoint statements.

% ---- EXP-1 headline table ----

\midrule

velocity rel.\ error vs.\ ($\star$) & $0.78\pm0.03$ & $0.75\pm0.07$ & $0.50\pm0.04$ & $0.40\pm0.05$ & $0.168\pm0.052$\\
$\|\text{mean}-x^i\|$ & $0.90\pm0.16$ & $0.87\pm0.20$ & $0.58\pm0.13$ & $0.38\pm0.10$ & $0.137\pm0.065$\\
