% ---- 5.4 problem-specification justification (C8) ----
The CIFAR-10 inpainting instance is deliberately chosen to be a conditional inverse problem
rather than a classification-style one: the conditioning variable is the observed half
of the image, so it is continuous and high-dimensional ($k{=}1536$); many completions
are consistent with any given $y$, so a collapsed sampler is a real failure rather than
a near-optimal answer; and training runs long past the point of good calibration, with
checkpoints early and late. Those are the conditions under which posterior collapse is
worth measuring at all.

% ---- 5.4 atomicity, n_eff and seed paragraphs ----
\paragraph{Measured samples and exact atomic endpoints.}
Proposition~\ref{prop:atomicity} proves that the smoothed population law is supported on
the training set for \emph{every} $h$. The measured finite-time sampler departs from it: the
fraction of generated samples lying on a training atom is $1.000$, $0.829$, $0.717$ and
$0.620$ at $h=0,4,5,6$, so at $h{=}6$ roughly $38\%$ of samples are off-atom. ``On an
atom'' is the nearest-neighbour criterion of \citet{yoon2023memorization} as used by
\citet{buchanan2025edge}, at their default: a sample counts as on-atom when its squared
distance to the nearest training image is at most $\tfrac19$ of its squared distance to
the second-nearest, i.e.\ a ratio of raw distances below $\tfrac13$. It is a relative
test and not floating-point equality, which for continuous image output would be
vacuous; the criterion is fixed across all four bandwidths, so the trend is a trend in
the data and not in the threshold
(\texttt{src/metrics/memorization.py}). The value
of exactly $1.000$ at $h{=}0$ is a control at that bandwidth, but the same integrator
need not incur the same error for a different field. The measured sampler combines
the learned field, finite terminal time and numerical solver. Its off-atom fraction
therefore does not isolate finite-capacity error. Proposition~\ref{prop:atomicity}
constrains the exact population endpoint; comparing it with the measured sampler
requires separating learned-field, truncation and discretisation effects.

% ---- Limitations: atomicity paragraph, trimmed ----
\paragraph{Limits of the population-to-sampler comparison.}
Proposition~\ref{prop:atomicity} shows the smoothed law is supported on the
training set for every $h$, and Section~\ref{sec:cifarddpm} measures the fraction of
generated samples actually on an atom as $1.000$, $0.829$, $0.717$, $0.620$ at
$h=0,4,5,6$ using a relative nearest-neighbour criterion. This compares an exact
limiting support statement with finite-time numerical samples. The measurements do
not separately quantify the contributions of the learned field, truncation and solver.

% ---- Limitations: remedies paragraph, trimmed ----
\paragraph{We do not benchmark against the remedies practitioners already use.} Early
stopping, weight averaging, condition dropout, caption randomisation and
classifier-free guidance scale are all deployed against exactly the symptom studied
here, and we compare against none of them. Our claim is therefore diagnostic, not
prescriptive: $\Covh$ is a reference a trained model can be audited against, and label
smoothing is what that reference describes---but which intervention one should actually
reach for, and at what cost to sample quality, is not settled by anything in this paper.
Fixed Gaussian perturbation of a conditioning embedding during training fits our label-noise model. General caption replacement, token perturbations and inference-time noise schedules need not do so; their endpoint laws are not identified by Theorem~\ref{thm:endpoint}.

% ---- moved from the introduction: what each factor is good for ----
\paragraph{The two factors are not one result applied to opposite halves of a product.}
The sharpest way to see this is to ask what each factor of \eqref{eq:kernel-field} can
be used for. The spatial factor is imposed by the clock: its bandwidth is $1-t$, it is
common to the conditional and unconditional fields alike, and it cannot be tuned, swept,
or removed. The label factor is a modelling choice, and everything this paper extracts
follows from that: an $h$-indexed family with a parameter-free reference curve to audit
against (Proposition~\ref{prop:moments}), the two limits $h\to0$ and $h\to\infty$
(Corollaries~\ref{cor:zerobw} and~\ref{cor:infbw}), a bandwidth window set by the
geometry of the labels (Proposition~\ref{prop:nearduplicate}), and an $h$-independent
obstruction, which is a statement about the whole family and so needs a family to state
(Proposition~\ref{prop:atomicity}). Classifier-free guidance is the cleanest case: the
conditional and unconditional fields differ in exactly one place (the label factor,
since the spatial factor is identical in both) so guidance is \emph{entirely} a
label-factor phenomenon, and Propositions~\ref{prop:cfg} and~\ref{prop:cfgatom} are not
available from a reading of the other factor at all. Figure~\ref{fig:factorisation} shows
the two factors separately, and their product, on the real $d{=}2$ instance.

\begin{figure}[htb]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.98\textwidth]{fig_factorisation.png}
\caption{The index posterior, factor by factor, on the real $d{=}2$ instance
($N{=}200$, $h{=}0.1$), with atoms coloured and sized by their weight. The
\emph{label} factor selects a band of eligible atoms and does not move with $t$
($n_{\mathrm{eff}}=17.6$). The \emph{spatial} factor at mid-time selects a different,
broader region ($29.1$). Neither alone picks out an atom; their product (the weight
the flow actually follows) is supported on the intersection ($5.5$), and by $t=0.95$
it has collapsed onto one ($1.00$). The two bandwidths are not on the same footing:
$1-t$ is imposed by the clock and is identical in the unconditional field, whereas
$h$ is a modelling choice. Everything this paper extracts comes from the right-hand
factor, which is what separates it from a reading of the left one
\citep{smola2026support}.}
\label{fig:factorisation}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{When does the flow commit?} Theorem~\ref{thm:endpoint} gives the
destination and not the speed. Along exact trajectories the effective number of atoms
$n_{\mathrm{eff}}(t)=1/\sum_iw_i^2$ of the index posterior falls from the size of the
label window, which $h$ sets, to one. Figure~\ref{fig:collapsetime}(a) shows this on the
EXP-1 instance: the label factor sets where each curve starts and the spatial factor
brings every curve to one by $1-t\approx0.03$. Panel~(b) computes, for standard Gaussian
atoms, the time $t_c$ at which the spatial factor alone has selected one atom (median
$n_{\mathrm{eff}}\le2$). It falls from $0.86$--$0.97$ at $d=2$ to $0.06$ at $d=3072$, so
at image dimensions the flow commits within the first tenth of its clock. The dotted
curve $t_c/(1-t_c)=2\sqrt{\log N/d}$ comes from a Gaussian-fluctuation argument that we do
not prove; it is a heuristic that tracks the computed values, not a result of this paper.
This is the flow-matching counterpart of the collapse time of \citet{biroli2024dynamical},
computed for the exact field.

\begin{figure}[htb]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.98\textwidth]{fig_collapse_time.png}
\caption{When the index posterior commits to one atom, from exact trajectories of the
population flow (no training). (a)~$n_{\mathrm{eff}}$ against $1-t$ on the EXP-1 instance
($d{=}2$, $N{=}200$) for three bandwidths and the unconditional field; lines are medians
over $160$ source draws, bands the $10$--$90\%$ range. The label factor sets the start,
the spatial factor the end. (b)~Collapse time $t_c$ of the spatial factor against
dimension for Gaussian atoms and three dataset sizes; dotted, a heuristic scaling.}
\label{fig:collapsetime}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{The optimal field, drawn.} Figure~\ref{fig:velocityfield} plots
$\vstar(x,t,y)=\sum_iw_i(x^i-x)/(1-t)$ on the same instance. At $h=0$ every arrow points
at the one atom the label identifies, at every time. At $h=h^\star$, where
$\tr\Covh=\tr\Sigmapost$, the field points at many atoms at $t=0.5$ and commits to the
nearest of them by $t=0.9$, so the smoothed flow is still a partition of the plane into
basins of attraction of the atoms.

\begin{figure}[htb]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.98\textwidth]{fig_velocity_field.png}
\caption{The population velocity field on the EXP-1 instance at $t=0.5$ and $t=0.9$. Arrows
have unit length and are coloured by the atom of largest weight, with opacity given by
that weight; black dots are the atoms, with area proportional to the label weight
$p_i^{(h)}(y)$. (a,b)~$h=0$: one atom attracts everywhere. (c,d)~$h=h^\star\approx0.70$:
the field commits atom by atom as $t\to1$.}
\label{fig:velocityfield}
\end{figure}

\begin{table}[htb]
\centering\footnotesize
\setlength{\tabcolsep}{4pt}
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.05}
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.20\textwidth}p{0.15\textwidth}p{0.28\textwidth}p{0.30\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
work & conditioning & relevant conclusion & distinction studied here\\
\midrule
\citet{scarvelis2025closedform} & unconditional & closed-form empirical denoiser is a

the \emph{conditional} case, where the label identifies one atom rather than
reweighting all of them\\
\citet{chen2026scoresmoothing} & unconditional & smoothing the empirical score as the
escape from memorisation & smoothing the \emph{label}, and the exact endpoint law it
induces\\
\citet{gao2024flow}; \citet{bertrand2025closed} & unconditional & the closed-form
optimal velocity is a weighted average of the directions $x^i-x$ and its flow reaches only
training points; generalisation appears when the network fails to approximate it & the
\emph{conditional} closed form, and what conditioning noise does to the endpoint law
rather than to the field\\
\citet{pfrommer2025conditional} & conditional & trained conditional models are smooth in
the conditioning variable and deviate from the denoising path (``self-guidance'') &
the exact population counterpart: noise on the label gives a kernel over the atoms, with
a moment reference and an atomicity floor\\
\citet{smola2026support} & conditional & the \emph{spatial} factor
$\pi_0(\tfrac{x-tx^i}{1-t})$ is a Nadaraya--Watson smoother: on fixed atoms the query is $x/t$ and bandwidth $(1-t)/t$; on moving centers $tx^i$ the scale is $1-t$ & the \emph{label} factor
$K_h(y-y^i)$ of the same product \eqref{eq:kernel-field}, whose bandwidth is a
modelling choice; and the endpoint law and its moments rather than the field. The two
readings compose; neither implies the other\\
\citet{physicscfm2026} & conditional & for an overtrained network of separable form
$\sum_k\varphi_k(y)b_k(x,t)$, every sample ends at one point
$\bar x(y)=\sum_jx^j\varphi_j(y)$, a training point at an interpolatory label
(``variance collapse''); with indicator bases, samples end on training points near $y$
(``selective memorisation''); early stopping on the test loss & the population minimiser
over all square-integrable fields, with no architectural assumption: the same collapse at
$h=0$ (Proposition~\ref{prop:collapse}), but under label noise a mixture
$\sum_ip_i^{(h)}(y)\delta_{x^i}$ rather than a point at its mean, which is where their
partition-of-unity case ends; the atomicity floor; the audit of trained models\\
\citet{gu2023memorization} & conditional & unique/random labels sharply increase
memorisation at CIFAR and FFHQ scale, empirically & \emph{why}: informativeness is
irrelevant, identification is everything
(Proposition~\ref{prop:collapse}), which our shuffled-label experiment tests directly\\
\citet{somepalli2023copying} & text & replication is common with text conditioning and
rare without it; randomising captions mitigates & the conditional side of this
asymmetry, Eq.~\eqref{eq:distinction}; fixed Gaussian training-embedding noise fits our theorem, while general caption interventions need not\\
\citet{ho2022cascaded}; \citet{sadat2024cads} & conditional & conditioning noise as
practice; annealed at inference to restore conditional diversity & an exact empirical mixture for fixed Gaussian training-label noise; time-dependent inference noise is outside this endpoint theorem\\
\citet{buchanan2025edge} & unconditional & memorisation/generalisation phase transition
as denoiser capacity $M$ ranges over $K\to N$ & the label/bandwidth analogue
$h\colon0\to\infty$ (Corollaries~\ref{cor:zerobw},~\ref{cor:infbw}); neither result
subsumes the other\\
\citet{li2026memory} & unconditional & oracle flows and SDEs recover the training
samples; under discretisation and estimation error the endpoint is a training sample
plus controlled perturbations & the conditional label factor and its endpoint law, the
$h$-independent atomicity floor, and a survival identity that ties the retained
deviation to an error accumulating at rate $(1-t)^{-1}$
(Proposition~\ref{prop:survival}) rather than bounding it\\

\end{tabular}
\caption{Selected connections to empirical-flow, conditioning and regularization

% ---- regularity conventions (B7) ----
\paragraph{Three conventions, stated rather than assumed.} \emph{(i) Versions.} The
minimiser of an $L^2$ projection is unique only up to a null set
(Proposition~\ref{prop:projection}), yet the results below assert formulas ``for every
$x$ and $t<1$''. What we exhibit in each case is a distinguished \emph{continuous}
version: the weights in \eqref{eq:kernel-field} are ratios of everywhere-positive
smooth functions (Lemma~\ref{lem:cov}), so the formula defines a continuous field on
$\R^d\times[0,1)$, and every pointwise claim is a claim about that version. Any other
minimiser agrees with it a.e.\ in $(x,t,y)$. A query $y$ is a null slice, so at fixed
$h>0$ it is the choice of the version continuous in $(x,t,y)$ that fixes the field at
$y$, and we use it throughout. At $h=0$ and $y=y^i$ the label has positive mass and
there is no additional null-label-slice ambiguity; we still use the continuous representative in $(x,t)$. \emph{(ii) Endpoints.}
$p_1$ always denotes the weak limit $\lim_{t\uparrow1}p_t$, never a value attained at
$t=1$; the field is undefined there. We establish that the limit exists wherever we use
it, each time from almost-sure convergence of the interpolant rather than by appeal to a
general theorem, and Lemma~\ref{lem:wellposed} is deliberately narrowed so as not to
claim existence in general. \emph{(iii) Where the flow is well posed.} The $1/(1-t)$
factor means no statement here extends to the closed interval. All flows are solved on
$[0,1)$, where Lemma~\ref{lem:wellposed} gives existence, uniqueness and a growth bound;
the blow-up at $t=1$ is the mechanism under study, not a technical nuisance to be
regularised away.

% ---- atomicity floor scaling (B5) ----
\paragraph{How large is the floor, and where does it bind?}
Proposition~\ref{prop:atomicity} gives an $h$-independent floor but not its size, and
the paper measures it small (Appendix~\ref{sec:aux}), which invites the objection that
the obstruction is a curiosity. Optimal quantization provides asymptotic context, with explicit assumptions. For a probability law on $\R^d$ with a nonzero absolutely continuous part and a finite $(2+\eta)$ moment for some $\eta>0$, Zador's theorem~\citep[Ch.~6]{graf2000quantization} gives optimal $N$-point $W_2$ error asymptotic to a distribution-dependent constant times $N^{-1/d}$, at fixed $d$. Error using any particular training codebook is at least the optimal codebook error. This comparison does not determine the random-design rate or a finite-$N$ error ratio.

We measure the latter setting for a standard Gaussian target and i.i.d.\ Gaussian atoms (\texttt{scripts/verify\_atom\_floor\_scaling.py}). For the squared floor $F_N$, log--log fits over $N\in[200,3200]$ give slopes $-0.612$, $-0.379$, and $-0.249$ at $d=3,5,8$, compared with $-2/d=-0.667$, $-0.400$, and $-0.250$. These finite-range measurements are compatible with the candidate exponents; they do not establish an asymptotic law. At $d=2$ the fitted slope is $-0.846$. Over the tested range, $F_NN/\log N$ varies by a factor $1.05$, whereas $F_NN$ varies by $1.52$, so a logarithmic correction describes these measurements better. We do not infer a $\Theta(\log N/N)$ theorem from this fit.

For comparison, the bare exponent factor $N^{-1/d}$ equals $0.071$ at $(d,N)=(2,200)$ and $0.9975$ at $(3072,2000)$. These are not approximation errors or ratios to one-atom errors. Full rank alone does not provide such a finite-dimensional guarantee, and extending Euclidean quantization asymptotics to a lower-dimensional image posterior requires additional support assumptions. We therefore use the directly measured floor in the synthetic settings and make no quantitative dimension-based claim for CIFAR-10.

% ---- guidance commentary ----
Proposition~\ref{prop:cfgatom} in Section~\ref{sec:intervention-atomicity} gives the endpoint statement.

So guidance can change \emph{which} atom is reached once the label is smoothed (the
selected atom depends on the trajectory, hence on $w$).
Under an absolutely continuous source it reaches an atom almost surely;
exceptional initial points can converge to non-atom ties. Under hard conditioning,
every trajectory reaches the conditioned atom, though guidance can change its path.
Figure~\ref{fig:guidance} integrates the exact guided field and shows both halves.

\begin{figure}[htb]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.94\textwidth]{fig_guidance.png}
\caption{Proposition~\ref{prop:cfg}, drawn. \textbf{Left}: the component of the
guided trajectory orthogonal to the affine hull of the training set, normalised by
its initial value. For every guidance weight it lies exactly on $(1-t)$ over six
decades---the identity $q_t=(1-t)q_0$ the proof turns on, which holds because the
guided target is an affine combination of atoms and so is annihilated by the
orthogonal projection. \textbf{Right}, in three dimensions because the result is
about dimension: six atoms spanning a plane in $\R^3$, and trajectories started
deliberately off that plane. Whatever $w$, they are pulled into it. When $N\le d$
the hull is Lebesgue-null, so no guidance weight yields an absolutely continuous
conditional law.}
\label{fig:guidance}
\end{figure}

Two things are worth separating here. What Proposition~\ref{prop:cfg} proves is a
statement about \emph{dimension}: guidance moves mass around inside a set of dimension
at most $N-1$ and cannot leave it. In the image regime that is already decisive
($N{=}2000$ atoms in $d{=}3072$ leaves a null set) but at $N>d$ the affine hull is
all of $\R^d$ and the proposition says nothing. What we \emph{observe}, integrating the
exact guided field, is stronger: endpoints land on the atoms themselves, to integrator
accuracy, at every $w$ we tried, and increasing $w$ changes \emph{which} atom is
reached rather than whether an atom is reached
(\texttt{scripts/verify\_cfg\_affine.py}; the identity \eqref{eq:cfgorth} is
reproduced to six decimal places for $h\in\{0,0.7\}$ and $w\in\{0,3,12\}$). Proposition~\ref{prop:cfgatom} proves convergence for every trajectory and
atomic endpoints almost surely under the Gaussian source. The proof combines a signed
log-partition energy with a volume argument for the exceptional tie basins; under hard
conditioning it gives the stronger all-initial-point bound~\eqref{eq:guided-hard-rate}.

% ---- near-duplicate commentary ----
This is the regime that occurs in practice (captions or labels that are close but
not identical) and it makes the bandwidth window explicit. Both conditions can hold
together in the asymptotic regime $\varepsilon\ll h\ll\Delta$, so a well-separated bandwidth window is suggested when
clusters are tight relative to their separation; inside that window the model behaves as
though conditioned on the cluster, with $n_{\mathrm{eff}}$ counting the cluster and not
the dataset. Note what does \emph{not} improve: the endpoint law is still a finite sum
of point masses, now $|C|$ of them, so Proposition~\ref{prop:atomicity} applies
unchanged. Near-duplicate labels buy effective sample size, not continuity.

% ---- bandwidth-expansion caveats (B8) ----
Both restrictions matter for what follows: a pointwise, homoscedastic expansion should
\emph{not} be expected to describe an image model, where $\Sigma(\cdot)$ certainly
varies with the conditioning image and most evaluation conditions sit near the boundary
of a $1536$-dimensional label support. The across-condition slope of $0.481$ measured
in Section~\ref{sec:cifarddpm} is what that failure to transfer looks like, and we read
it that way rather than as a refutation of \eqref{eq:expansion} at the point where it
is claimed.

% ---- Lipschitz representation floor (B4 / former contribution 5) ----
\subsection{Finite capacity: a genuine representation floor}
\label{sec:theory-floor}

The results so far describe the population minimiser over \emph{all}
square-integrable fields; a trained network is restricted to a finite-capacity
class. Here is one concrete way that restriction alone forces a positive loss floor,
independent of any optimisation difficulty.

\begin{proposition}[Lipschitz lower bound]
\label{prop:liplb}
Fix $i$, $t<1$, $f_i(x,t)=(x^i-x)/(1-t)$, and suppose $v(\cdot,t,y^i)$ is
$L$-Lipschitz in $x$. Under $X_t\mid I=i\sim\N(tx^i,(1-t)^2 I_d)$,
$\E[|v(X_t,t,y^i)-f_i(X_t,t)|^2\mid I=i]\ge d\,[1-L(1-t)]_+^2$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{corollary}[Loss floor under a uniform Lipschitz constraint]
\label{cor:floor}
For $\mathcal F_L=\{v:\operatorname{Lip}_x v(\cdot,t,y^i)\le L\ \forall t,i\}$ with
$L\ge1$ and $t\sim\mathrm{Unif}(0,1)$,
\begin{equation}
\inf_{v\in\mathcal F_L}L_0(v)\ =\ d\int_{1-1/L}^1[1-L(1-t)]^2\,dt\ =\ \frac{d}{3L}\ >\ 0.
\label{eq:floor}
\end{equation}
The infimum is attained by the measurable field
$v_L(x,t,y^i)=x^i-\min\{L,(1-t)^{-1}\}(x-tx^i)$ for $t<1$.
More generally, if the Lipschitz constant is allowed to depend on time,
$\operatorname{Lip}_xv(\cdot,t,y^i)\le L(t)$, the same pointwise bound integrates to
\begin{equation}
\inf_{v\in\mathcal F_{L(\cdot)}}L_0(v)\ \ge\ d\int_0^1\big[1-L(t)(1-t)\big]_+^2\,dt .
\label{eq:floorlt}
\end{equation}
\end{corollary}

\eqref{eq:floorlt} is the honest form of the statement, and it changes how
\eqref{eq:floor} should be read. The uniform-$L$ floor is an artefact of the
uniformity: the integrand in \eqref{eq:floorlt} vanishes identically as soon as
$L(t)\ge1/(1-t)$ for a.e.\ $t$, so a network whose Lipschitz constant in $x$ grows like
$1/(1-t)$ near the endpoint---which a time-conditioned network is free to do, and which
the target field itself does---faces no floor at all from this mechanism. That is
consistent with what we measure: Appendix~\ref{sec:aux} finds the $d/(3L)$ value some
$50\times$ below the observed plateau. We therefore do not offer this as an explanation
of the plateau, and say so in Section~\ref{sec:limitations}. Equality in
\eqref{eq:floor} concerns the full measurable Lipschitz class, not the smaller class
represented by a fixed architecture. The lower bound alone therefore cannot exclude
a larger architectural approximation error. Equation~\eqref{eq:floorlt} localises what would
have to be measured (the profile $L(t)$ near $t=1$, not a single constant) for the
question to be settled.

Two warnings temper \eqref{eq:floor}. First, the bound is \emph{conditional} on the
constraint: an MLP with unbounded weights lies in no fixed $\mathcal F_L$, and by
universal approximation $\inf_\theta L(v_\theta)$ may be $0$; what we prove is that
any architecture with $\operatorname{Lip}_x v_\theta\le L$ cannot beat $d/(3L)$.
Second, it does not bind in our experiments: with $d=2$ and a trained
$\operatorname{Lip}_x$ of order $10^1$--$10^2$, the floor is of order $10^{-2}$,
one to two orders of magnitude below the loss plateau $0.48$ observed at $2\times10^5$ iterations (it falls to $0.36$ by $7\times10^5$, so the gap is if anything understated).
Corollary~\ref{cor:floor} is therefore best used as a \emph{measurement} (estimate
$L$, compare $d/(3L)$ to the plateau, report which gap dominates
(Appendix~\ref{sec:aux})). A sampled spatial-gradient estimate does not certify
a global Lipschitz cap, and this comparison does not rule out representation error.

\subsection{Where the standard remedies stand}
\label{app:remedies}

Five interventions are deployed
against this symptom in practice. The exact-field results describe some restricted counterparts, without benchmarking the practical methods. \emph{Guidance} cannot leave the training set's affine hull at any weight
and lands on an atom---under hard conditioning the conditioned atom itself, so there it
is inert at the endpoint. \emph{Caption randomisation} includes interventions outside our model; Theorem~\ref{thm:endpoint} applies to fixed additive Gaussian noise on a conditioning embedding during training.
\emph{Condition dropout} leaves the minimiser of Proposition~\ref{prop:projection}
unchanged conditionally on a real label, so it too does not move the conditional
endpoint; what it supplies is the unconditional field guidance then combines.
\emph{Early stopping} we measure: at $h{=}6$ the aggregate ratio passes through $0.98$ at
$8000$ iterations and drifts to $1.18$ by $60000$, while at $h{=}4$ it never arrives
within the budget (Appendix~\ref{app:deferred}). \emph{Weight averaging} we have not
analysed. What we do not offer is a comparison at matched sample quality, the axis a
practitioner actually trades against.

Figure~\ref{fig:interventions} draws four specified interventions as flows rather
than as statements: each panel integrates the exact population field of that
intervention from the same $160$ draws of $x_0$, on the instance of
Figure~\ref{fig:mechanism}, so the taxonomy (inert, atom-preserving, reweighting,
support-moving) can be read off the endpoints.

\begin{figure}[htb]
\centering
% Three panels above, two below, at the same enlarged panel scale.
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth,trim=0 25.2bp 424.37bp 0,clip]{fig_interventions.png}\\[7pt]
\includegraphics[width=0.6667\textwidth,trim=636.55bp 25.2bp 0 0,clip]{fig_interventions.png}
\caption{The four interventions, as exact population flows on one condition of the
EXP-1 instance. (a) hard conditioning: every path lands on the labelled atom. (b) label
smoothing at $h^\star$: the same atoms, reweighted by $p^{(h)}_i$ (marker area), which
is Theorem~\ref{thm:endpoint}. (c) interpolant noise at $\sigma{=}0.5$: the paths are
different and the endpoint is not---$\gamma(1)=0$, so the endpoint law is the same point
mass at every noise level (Proposition~\ref{prop:prop17}). (d) guidance at $w{=}3$:
atom-preserving, and under hard conditioning it is the conditioned atom itself
(Proposition~\ref{prop:cfg}). (e) endpoint smoothing at $\rho{=}0.30$: the only one of
the four whose support leaves the atoms (Proposition~\ref{prop:tgtnoise}). Sky blue denotes source draws, black training atoms, orange generated endpoints, and blue shading the posterior. Produced by
\texttt{scripts/make\_flow\_portraits.py}, which checks its own field against
\eqref{eq:kernel-field} before drawing.}
\label{fig:interventions}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Error survival and the loss form: statements and discussion}
\label{app:survivaltheory}

Section~\ref{sec:survival} states what follows in summary. The results of
Section~\ref{sec:theory} describe the population minimiser and none says what happens to
a model merely \emph{close} to it; the proposition below closes that gap, and the answer
is a contraction estimate rather than the growth estimate one might expect.

\begin{proposition}[Error survival]
\label{prop:survival}
Fix $i$, let $h=0$ with distinct labels, and let $v$ be any locally Lipschitz field.
Write $\Delta(x,t):=v(x,t,y^i)-\vstarz(x,t,y^i)$ for its error against the exact
collapsed field, let $x^v_t$ solve $\dot x_t=v(x_t,t,y^i)$ from $x_0$ (we assume it exists on $[0,t]$), and let
$x^\star_t=(1-t)x_0+tx^i$ be the exact trajectory from the same $x_0$. Then for
$t<1$, with $e_t:=x^v_t-x^\star_t$, the \emph{identity}
\begin{equation}
e_t\;=\;(1-t)\int_0^t\frac{\Delta(x^v_s,s)}{1-s}\,ds
\label{eq:survival-id}
\end{equation}
holds, and in particular
\begin{equation}
|e_t|\;\le\;(1-t)\int_0^t\frac{|\Delta(x^v_s,s)|}{1-s}\,ds .
\label{eq:survival}
\end{equation}
Consequently:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*),leftmargin=2em]
\item \emph{(bounded errors do not survive)} if
  $\int_0^t|\Delta(x^v_s,s)|^2ds\le\varepsilon^2$ then
  $|e_t|\le\varepsilon\sqrt{(1-t)t}\le\varepsilon\sqrt{1-t}$, so a sampler stopping
  at $t=1-\delta$ has $|e_{1-\delta}|\le\varepsilon\sqrt\delta$, which vanishes with
  $\delta$ \emph{for every} finite $\varepsilon$;
\item \emph{(what the bound gives)} if $|\Delta(x,t)|\le c\,(1-t)^{-a}$ with $a>0$
  then $|e_t|\le\frac{c}{a}\big((1-t)^{1-a}-(1-t)\big)$, which at $a=1$ reads
  $|e_t|\le c\,t\le c$. Hence $\lim_{t\uparrow1}|e_t|=0$ when $a<1$, and
  $\limsup_{t\uparrow1}|e_t|\le c$ when $a=1$. For $a>1$ the right-hand side diverges
  and \eqref{eq:survival} constrains $e_t$ not at all---a diverging upper bound is
  not a diverging quantity, and $e\equiv0$ satisfies it. That case is settled by
  (c), not by (b);
\item \emph{(the bound is attained, so the threshold is real)} fix a unit vector $u$
  and take the spatially constant error $\Delta(x,t)=c\,(1-t)^{-a}u$, for which
  $v=\vstarz+\Delta$ is locally Lipschitz with $\Delta$ having Lipschitz constant
  $L_\Delta=0$ in $x$. Then \eqref{eq:survival-id} evaluates in closed form:
  \begin{equation}
  e_t=\frac{c}{a}\big((1-t)^{1-a}-(1-t)\big)\,u\quad(a>0),
  \qquad e_t=-c\,(1-t)\log(1-t)\,u\quad(a=0),
  \label{eq:survival-sharp}
  \end{equation}
  so \eqref{eq:survival} holds with equality for every $t<1$. Therefore
  $\lim_{t\uparrow1}|e_t|$ is $0$ for $a<1$, is exactly $c$ for $a=1$, and is $+\infty$
  for $a>1$: the trichotomy is realised and not merely permitted. These fields have
  $\mathcal L_i=\infty$ for $a\ge\frac12$, so the trichotomy concerns pathwise errors and
  not models with finite loss;
\item \emph{(what a surviving deviation requires)} write
  $I(t):=\int_0^t\Delta(x^v_s,s)(1-s)^{-1}ds$ for the accumulated weighted error, so
  that \eqref{eq:survival-id} reads $e_t=(1-t)I(t)$. Then for any $\Delta$ and any
  $e_\star\in\R^d$,
  \begin{equation}
  \lim_{t\uparrow1}e_t=e_\star
  \iff
  I(t)=\frac{e_\star}{1-t}+o\big((1-t)^{-1}\big) .
  \label{eq:survival-conv}
  \end{equation}
  Since $e_t=(1-t)I(t)$ identically, the behaviour of $e_t$ at the endpoint is exactly
  the behaviour of $I$ at the scale $(1-t)^{-1}$, and the cases are exhaustive: if
  $|I(t)|=o((1-t)^{-1})$ then $e_t\to0$; if $(1-t)I(t)$ converges to some
  $e_\star\ne0$---equivalently, if $I$ sits at that scale \emph{with a limiting
  direction}---then $e_t\to e_\star$; if $(1-t)|I(t)|\to\infty$ then $|e_t|\to\infty$
  and there is no endpoint; and if $e_t=(1-t)I(t)$ has no limit in $\R^d\cup\{\infty\}$,
  bounded or not, there is no endpoint either. In particular a nonzero endpoint deviation \emph{requires} the
  accumulated weighted error to attain exactly the $(1-t)^{-1}$ scale, and to do so in a
  direction that does not cancel: growing more slowly than that scale is annihilated
  by the factor $(1-t)$, whatever the pointwise behaviour of $\Delta$ that produced it.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

The reading is that the collapsed field is \emph{self-enforcing}. Its linear part is
$-\tfrac{1}{1-t}I_d$, a contraction whose strength diverges as $t\to1$, and that
contraction annihilates any perturbation that does not itself diverge at rate
$1/(1-t)$. A model can therefore retain conditional variance only by carrying an
endpoint-scale $1/(1-t)$ accumulated error (by failing, in the accumulated sense
part~(d) makes precise, to reproduce the blow-up) and the deviation it keeps is, to
leading order, the coefficient of that failure. This closes a loop the paper already
had open: Remark~\ref{lem:norepr} observes that no finite network with globally
Lipschitz activations represents $1/(1-t)$ pointwise on $[0,1)$, and
\eqref{eq:survival-conv} states the converse as an exact asymptotic rather than as a
word. We are deliberate about its form. It constrains
$I(t)=\int_0^t\Delta(x_s^v,s)(1-s)^{-1}ds$, not $\Delta$ pointwise: an error can
oscillate, or be large only on a thin set of times, and what survives depends on the
accumulation alone---the fourth case of~(d) is realised by an error of critical
magnitude whose direction turns forever and never settles
(Appendix~\ref{app:survival}). What the proposition does \emph{not} give is a pointwise
converse of the form ``$e_1\ne0\Rightarrow\Delta=\Theta((1-t)^{-1})$'', and we do not
claim one. Read this way the observed plateau is the size of the weighted integral the
network's error accumulates rather than an unexplained residual, and the collapse we
measure over training (Table~\ref{tab:exp1}, Section~\ref{sec:cifarddpm}) is that
quantity shrinking.

The bound as stated is pathwise, integrating along the model's own trajectory, which is
not a quantity anyone measures. It converts into the training loss with one extra
hypothesis, and it is a strong one (see below).

\begin{assumption}[Lipschitz error]
\label{as:lipschitz}
In the setting of Proposition~\ref{prop:survival}, the error
$\Delta(\cdot,t)=v(\cdot,t,y^i)-\vstarz(\cdot,t,y^i)$ is $L_\Delta$-Lipschitz in $x$ for
a.e.\ $t\le T$.
\end{assumption}

\begin{corollary}[Loss form]
\label{cor:lossform}
In the setting of Proposition~\ref{prop:survival} and under
Assumption~\ref{as:lipschitz}, let
\[
\mathcal L_i(v)\;:=\;\E_{t\sim\mathrm{Unif}(0,1)}\,\E_{X_0\sim\pi_0}
\big|v(X_t,t,y^i)-U\big|^2,\qquad X_t=(1-t)X_0+tx^i,\ U=x^i-X_0,
\]
be the flow-matching loss at index $i$, and write
$\mathcal E(t):=\big(\E_{X_0}|x^v_t-x^\star_t|^2\big)^{1/2}$ for the root-mean-square
deviation over initial conditions. Then for $t\le T$, and for $\delta$ with $1-\delta\le T$,
\begin{equation}
\mathcal E(t)\;\le\;e^{L_\Delta t}\,\sqrt{\mathcal L_i(v)}\;\sqrt{t(1-t)},
\qquad\text{so}\qquad
\mathcal E(1-\delta)\;\le\;e^{L_\Delta}\sqrt{\mathcal L_i(v)\,\delta}.
\label{eq:lossform}
\end{equation}
Consequently the conditional second moment about the memorised atom obeys
$\big(\E|x^v_{1-\delta}-x^i|^2\big)^{1/2}\le e^{L_\Delta}\sqrt{\mathcal L_i(v)\delta}
+\delta\,\big(\E|X_0-x^i|^2\big)^{1/2}$.
\end{corollary}

Note which Lipschitz constant is assumed: that of the \emph{error} $\Delta$, not of the
field, whose constant $1/(1-t)$ diverges by construction. An error $c\,(1-t)^{-1}u$, the
example in part~(c), is constant in $x$ and has
$L_\Delta=0$. The hypothesis is nonetheless strong. It is equivalent to
$\|\nabla_xv+(1-t)^{-1}I\|\le L_\Delta$, so a model with $\operatorname{Lip}_xv\le K$
satisfies it on $[0,T]$ only if $L_\Delta\ge(1-T)^{-1}-K$. For a bounded-Lipschitz
network and $T=1-\delta$ the bound is of order $e^{1/\delta}$ and says nothing, and the
corollary is a statement about fields that reproduce the linear part of $(\star)$. For
such fields, \eqref{eq:lossform} bounds the root-mean-square deviation from the exact truncated flow by $e^{L_\Delta}\sqrt{\mathcal L_i\delta}$. The actual conditional standard deviation is at most $\delta\sqrt d+e^{L_\Delta}\sqrt{\mathcal L_i\delta}$; the first term is the spread of the exact flow at that cutoff. Appendix~\ref{app:survival} reports the bound's measured
tightness over sixteen configurations and reports the $\sqrt{\mathcal L_i}$ scaling on
EXP-1's own checkpoints, where the slope is $0.429$ ($R^2=0.99$) over six checkpoints
and $0.485$ ($R^2=0.998$) over the last three, against $\tfrac12$. That is an empirical
scaling and not a test of the bound, whose hypothesis these networks do not meet with a
moderate constant; the fit implies a prefactor of about $25$, which we do not use. One limit remains: the bound
constrains how much variance can survive, not what the surviving law looks like.

\subsection{What training loss can certify}
\label{app:teacher-risk}

Training evaluates velocity error under the interpolant law, whereas generation
evaluates it along the learned flow. Relating the two requires additional control,
provided for example by learned-field regularity \citep{benton2024error} or by a
direct risk-transfer condition. We use the latter as a sufficient condition here.

\begin{theorem}[A finite-time certificate with risk transfer]
\label{thm:risktransfer}
Fix an identifying label with atom $a=x^i$, let $X_0\sim\N(0,I_d)$, and put
$\eps_t=1-t$, $Y_t=ta+\eps_tX_0$, and $f(x,t)=(a-x)/\eps_t$.
Let $Z_t$ solve $\dot Z_t=f(Z_t,t)+\Delta(Z_t,t)$ from the same $X_0$.
Suppose the solution and its variation-of-constants formula exist through $T<1$.
Define $r(t)=\E|\Delta(Y_t,t)|^2$ and $m(t)=\E|\Delta(Z_t,t)|^2$.
If $m(t)\le K(t)r(t)$ almost everywhere and
\[
B_T:=\eps_T\int_0^T\frac{\sqrt{K(t)r(t)}}{\eps_t}\,dt<\infty,
\]
then, writing $\nu_T=\law(Z_T)$ and $S(\nu)=\sqrt{\tr\Cov(\nu)}$,
\begin{align}
W_2(\nu_T,\law(Y_T))&\le B_T,\label{eq:risktransfer}\\
|\E Z_T-Ta|&\le B_T,\qquad
|S(\nu_T)-\eps_T\sqrt d|\le B_T.\label{eq:riskmoments}
\end{align}
For any positive measurable weight $w$ for which the factors are finite,
\[
B_T\le\eps_T\left(\int_0^T w(t)r(t)\,dt\right)^{1/2}
\left(\int_0^T\frac{K(t)}{\eps_t^2w(t)}\,dt\right)^{1/2}.
\]
Without risk transfer, the corresponding learned-trajectory bound is
\[
W_2(\nu_T,\law(Y_T))\le
\sqrt{T(1-T)\int_0^T m(t)\,dt}.
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Under the shared initialization, variation of constants gives
$Z_T-Y_T=\eps_T\int_0^T\Delta(Z_t,t)/\eps_t\,dt$.
Minkowski's integral inequality bounds its $L^2$ norm by
$\eps_T\int_0^T\sqrt{m(t)}/\eps_t\,dt$, proving~\eqref{eq:risktransfer}.
Weighted Cauchy--Schwarz proves the next bound. Applied directly to $m$,
Cauchy--Schwarz uses
$\eps_T^2\int_0^T\eps_t^{-2}\,dt=T(1-T)$.
For any square-integrable coupling $(U,V)$, Jensen's inequality bounds the
mean difference by $\|U-V\|_{L^2}$, and
\[
|S(\law U)-S(\law V)|\le
\|(U-\E U)-(V-\E V)\|_{L^2}\le\|U-V\|_{L^2}.
\]
Infimizing over couplings gives~\eqref{eq:riskmoments}.
\end{proof}

In particular, $W_2(\nu_T,\delta_a)\le
(1-T)\sqrt{|a|^2+d}+B_T$. A bounded total learned-trajectory error energy
therefore implies $W_2$ collapse as $T\uparrow1$. A density domination
$\nu_t\ll\law(Y_t)$ with $\|d\nu_t/d\law(Y_t)\|_\infty\le K(t)$ is one
sufficient condition for risk transfer. It is restrictive: a Gaussian even slightly
wider than the teacher need not satisfy a finite uniform density-ratio bound.
The theorem does not infer $K$ from an observed training loss.

\begin{proposition}[Small teacher loss does not certify the endpoint]
\label{prop:loss-no-certificate}
There are fields affine in space and piecewise continuous in time, each with a
finite uniform spatial Lipschitz constant, whose hard-conditioned losses tend
to zero while their endpoint laws are all $\N(a,I_d)$. Thus no bound
$W_2(\nu_1,\delta_a)\le\Phi(L_0(v))$ with $\Phi(u)\to0$ as $u\downarrow0$
holds over this class, and neither does a vanishing bound for endpoint variance.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Let $0<\delta<1/2$, $A_\delta=\log(1/\delta)$, and
$v_\delta(x,t,y^j)=x^j+k_\delta(t)(x-tx^j)$ at every observed label $y^j$. At the selected label fix $a=x^i$, so $v_\delta(x,t,y^i)=a+k_\delta(t)(x-ta)$, where
\[
k_\delta(t)=
\begin{cases}
-(1-t)^{-1},&0\le t<1-\delta,\\
2A_\delta/\delta,&1-\delta\le t<1-\delta/2,\\
0,&1-\delta/2\le t\le1.
\end{cases}
\]
Use the Carath\'eodory solution at the two time jumps. Then $U_t=Z_t-ta$
satisfies $\dot U_t=k_\delta(t)U_t$. It contracts to $\delta X_0$, amplifies by
$e^{A_\delta}=1/\delta$, and remains $X_0$ thereafter. Hence $Z_1=a+X_0$.
At each observed label the teacher-law residual is
$\Delta(Y_t,t)=(1+\eps_tk_\delta(t))X_0$. Averaging these identical conditional losses over the empirical labels gives exactly
\[
L_0(v_\delta)=d\left[\int_{\delta/2}^{\delta}
\left(1+\frac{2A_\delta\eps}{\delta}\right)^2d\eps+\frac\delta2\right]
=d\delta\left(1+\tfrac32 A_\delta+\tfrac76 A_\delta^2\right)\longrightarrow0.
\]
Yet $W_2(\nu_1,\delta_a)=\sqrt d$ and $\tr\Cov(\nu_1)=d$.
The spatial Lipschitz constants are finite for each $\delta$ but are not bounded
uniformly as $\delta\downarrow0$.
\end{proof}

This example identifies the missing distributional transfer in a loss-only
argument. It is not evidence that every trained network realizes this field.
The finite-time moment bounds above provide the positive statement when that
transfer can actually be controlled.

% ==========================================================================
\section{Additional experimental results}
\label{app:exp}

\subsection{Effective bandwidth and calibration: details}
\label{app:calibdetails}

\paragraph{Fit quality.} On the linear-Gaussian runs, $h_{\mathrm{eff}}$ explains the
per-condition variances with median log-space $R^2$ $0.86$, against $0.79$ for the
two-parameter power law behind $\beta$; a constant multiple of $\Covh$, also one
parameter, reaches $0.66$. The six CIFAR-10 reruns reproduce the published runs
($h_{\mathrm{eff}}=4.79$ and $5.81$ at 60k for seed 0, against $4.80$ and $5.75$). A
second rerun of all six on another GPU, used for the image-scale calibration below,
gives $4.93$ and $5.65$, the same 31/36 agreement and the same $1.13$--$1.34h$
endpoints: $h_{\mathrm{eff}}$ is reproducible to a few percent across non-deterministic
GPU runs.

\paragraph{The two ends, $h=0$ and $h=6$.}
\label{app:heffends}
Six further runs on the same protocol (three instances each) extend the measurement. At
$h{=}6$, where the reference barely varies (Proposition~\ref{prop:heffid}), $h_{\mathrm{eff}}$
ends at $1.05$--$1.14h$ and agrees with $h_{\mathrm{mean}}$ at $8/18$ checkpoints from 5k on,
against $31/36$ at $h\in\{4,5\}$. At $h{=}0$ it falls monotonically in all three runs, to
$3.44$--$3.74$ at 60k, and agrees with $h_{\mathrm{mean}}$ at none. The means stay on the
nearest training atom: the generated mean identifies it at $48/48$ conditions, and
$h_{\mathrm{mean}}$ stays at or below $2.8$, often at the lower edge of the grid. The
variance, $\tr\Cov=0.41$--$0.50$, is what the reference reaches only by mixing atoms at
$h\approx3.6$. So the residual variance of hard-conditioned training at this scale is
spread around each atom, not weight on its neighbours, and no single bandwidth describes
it, unlike the synthetic $h{=}0$ runs, where the two estimates agree.

\paragraph{Which reference family.}
\label{app:families}
Agreement of $h_{\mathrm{eff}}$ and $h_{\mathrm{mean}}$ shows that the means and the
variances are explained by one member of the reference family. It does not show that
the family is the right one. We therefore refit the same samples (the 48 checkpoints of
Section~\ref{sec:heff}, $M{=}1000$ at 20 conditions) under five families, each with one
scale $s$ and the same estimator \eqref{eq:heff}. Four are mixtures over the training
atoms with weights $w_i\propto e^{-|y-y^i|^2/2s^2}$ (Gaussian, the reference of
Theorem~\ref{thm:endpoint}), $e^{-|y-y^i|/s}$ (Laplace), $(1+|y-y^i|^2/s^2)^{-1}$
(Cauchy), and uniform weights on the $s$ nearest labels (kNN). The fifth is not atomic:
the linear-Gaussian posterior with observation noise $s$, whose covariance does not
depend on $y$. The Gaussian family reproduces the published fit exactly (the same
$h_{\mathrm{eff}}$ at 120/120 checkpoints). A sixth family, also not atomic but with a
variance that depends on $y$, is the endpoint-smoothed law
$\sum_ip_i^{(s)}(y)\N(x^i,\rho^2I_d)$ that noise of scale $\rho$ on the training
endpoints produces at the optimum, with trace $\tr\mathrm{Cov}_s(y)+d\rho^2$; it nests
the Gaussian at $\rho=0$, so it is fitted over
$(s,\rho)$ and compared by leave-one-condition-out error, where the extra parameter
counts only if it predicts conditions it was not fitted to.

\begin{table}[htb]
\centering\small
\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
\toprule
reference family & intervals agree & median $R^2$ & best variance fit & best mean prediction\\
\midrule
Gaussian (Theorem~\ref{thm:endpoint}) & 48/48 & $\mathbf{0.86}$ & $\mathbf{36}$/48 & $\mathbf{41}$/48\\
Laplace & 45/48 & $0.82$ & 9/48 & 5/48\\
Cauchy & 43/48 & $0.65$ & 3/48 & 1/48\\
kNN & 48/48 & $0.36$ & 0/48 & 1/48\\
posterior (not atomic) & 42/48 & $-0.12$ & --- & ---\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}

\vspace{6pt}
\setlength{\tabcolsep}{4pt}
\begin{tabular}{lcccccc}
\toprule
& \multicolumn{3}{c}{atomic families} & \multicolumn{3}{c}{endpoint-smoothed (not atomic)}\\
\cmidrule(lr){2-4}\cmidrule(l){5-7}
$h$ & G best & L best & med.\ $R^2$ G / L & $0\in$ CI of $\hat\rho$ &
max $d\hat\rho^2/\tr$ & held-out vs G\\
\midrule
$0$ & 7/12 & 3/12 & 0.82 / 0.75 & 9/12 & 0.06 & 6 / 4 / 2\\
$0.01$ & 8/12 & 3/12 & 0.82 / 0.81 & 10/12 & 0.08 & 1 / 5 / 6\\
$0.05$ & 11/12 & 1/12 & 0.94 / 0.91 & 12/12 & 0.03 & 1 / 6 / 5\\
$0.1$ & 10/12 & 2/12 & 0.95 / 0.95 & 10/12 & 0.07 & 2 / 6 / 4\\

\caption{The 48 synthetic checkpoints of Section~\ref{sec:heff} refitted under six
reference families. Top: ``intervals agree'': the 95\% bootstrap intervals of the scales
fitted to the variances and to the means overlap; ``best variance fit'': lowest log-space
misfit among the four atomic families; ``best mean prediction'': the scale fitted to the
variances predicts the means best. The posterior family's trace is the same at every
condition, so its $R^2$ is at most $0$ by construction; its optimum lies at the edge of
the grid at 37/48 checkpoints. Bottom, by training bandwidth: how often the Gaussian (G)
and Laplace (L) references fit the variances best, and the endpoint-smoothed family
fitted over $(s,\rho)$; ``held-out vs G'': checkpoints at which its
leave-one-condition-out error is lower / equal / higher than the Gaussian's. Script:
\texttt{scripts/heff\_family\_test.py}.}
\label{tab:families}
\end{table}

Interval agreement does not separate the families (Table~\ref{tab:families}): kNN passes
it at all 48 checkpoints and Laplace at 45. Fit quality does. Paired at each checkpoint,
the Gaussian reference has the lower variance misfit than Laplace at 36/48 checkpoints
and in 9/12 runs (median log-RMSE difference $0.020$, 95\% bootstrap interval $[0.011,
0.024]$), than Cauchy at 41/48 and than kNN at 48/48. Its variance-fitted scale also
predicts the means best at 41/48. The posterior family's $R^2=-0.12$ shows only that
the trained variance varies across conditions, which a $y$-independent covariance cannot
follow. The test that could fail is the endpoint-smoothed family, and it does not
displace the atoms: $\hat\rho=0$ lies inside its bootstrap interval at 41/48 checkpoints,
$d\hat\rho^2$ is at most $8\%$ of the trace, and its held-out error is lower than the
Gaussian's at 10/48 checkpoints and higher at 17. Only at $h{=}0$ does it help at half
the checkpoints, by a median $0.0007$ in log-RMSE. The evidence is therefore for a
mixture over the training atoms whose weights are best described by the Gaussian
kernel, with the Laplace kernel a close second. The preference is weakest where the
smoothing is the network's own: the Gaussian fits best at 7/12 checkpoints at $h{=}0$,
against 11/12 and 10/12 at $h=0.05$ and $0.1$, where it is also the kernel of the
training noise. At $h{=}0.5$, where the reference barely
varies, the Gaussian family still fits the variances best (14/15) but its intervals agree
at only 9/15, as Proposition~\ref{prop:heffid} predicts.

\paragraph{Protocol.} At every synthetic checkpoint with at least $3000$ iterations (75
in all) we measured, at 100 held-out pairs $(x^\star,y^\star)$, the coverage of the 90\%
region of the generated samples, a posterior-free PIT error, and the extraction rate.
$h_{\mathrm{eff}}$ is read from the training conditions only.

\paragraph{Baselines and confounds.} $h_{\mathrm{eff}}$ ranks the 90\% coverage with
Spearman $0.92$, ahead of the training loss ($0.85$), the raw generated variance
($0.76$), $\beta$ ($0.34$) and the iteration ($-0.41$). It keeps $0.90$ after
partialling out the iteration, $0.69$ after partialling out the iteration and $h$ (loss:
$0.42$), and $0.82$ across runs at a fixed iteration, so it is not a proxy for training
time. On an eight-mode Gaussian-mixture posterior in $d{=}8$ (10 runs) the result
repeats ($0.91$ pooled, $0.91$ given the iteration, $0.90$ at fixed iteration; PIT error
$-0.67$), with $h_{\mathrm{eff}}$ and $h_{\mathrm{mean}}$ at log correlation $0.96$.

\paragraph{Ranking is not adding information.} A regression on the free quantities (log
iteration, log training loss, log raw variance, the share of samples on a training atom
and $h$, each continuous one with its square), fitted to the coverage of the other runs,
predicts a held-out run's 90\% coverage with cross-validated $R^2=0.88$ (leave one run
out). Adding $\log h_{\mathrm{eff}}$ raises it to $0.92$, $\Delta R^2=+0.04$ with $95\%$
interval $[-0.04,0.22]$ over resampled runs, and lowers it for the PIT error ($-0.13$) and
the median $W_2$ distance to the posterior ($-0.06$)
(\texttt{scripts/audit\_incremental\_r2.py}). On these problems, then, $h_{\mathrm{eff}}$
does not add predictive power to quantities one already has. What it adds is that
Theorem~\ref{thm:calib}(d) turns it into a coverage prediction without any coverage labels,
at $R^2=0.90$, the level the fitted regression reaches only after seeing the coverage of
14 other runs.

\paragraph{The window.} The nominal 90\% region covers $0.86$--$0.88$ for
$h_{\mathrm{eff}}\in[0.1,0.4)$, where $\Covh$ matches $\Sigmapost$, and $0.54$ and $0.34$
for $[0.02,0.05)$ and below. Theorem~\ref{thm:calib} accounts for both edges: part (c)
bounds the coverage from below by $\alpha$ at large bandwidth, part (d) sends it to zero
as $n_{\mathrm{eff}}\to1$. Applied with the actual kernel weights at $h_{\mathrm{eff}}$
(the local model of Section~\ref{sec:heff}), it predicts the measured 90\% coverage with
$R^2=0.90$, mean absolute error $0.051$ and Spearman $0.89$. Nothing in the prediction
is fitted to coverage, but the 75 checkpoints are nested in 15 runs: resampling runs
gives $R^2\in[0.85,0.93]$ (95\%), and the binomial noise of 100 held-out pairs alone
would cap it at $0.97$ (\texttt{scripts/t3\_coverage\_uncertainty.py}). The exact finite
reference law at $h_{\mathrm{eff}}$ does so with $R^2=0.93$ ($[0.89,0.96]$), and the
two predictions agree with
each other at $R^2=0.97$. Replacing the actual weights by their effective sample size
alone (a plug-in of (d) that ignores weight fluctuations) gives only $R^2=0.61$: at small
$n_{\mathrm{eff}}$ the dispersion of the weights, not just their number, sets the
coverage. At the 50\% level the corresponding values are $0.59$ (local model) and $0.68$
(reference law).

\paragraph{Stopping rules.} Locating the window used the posterior. Taking the target
$h_{\mathrm{eff}}$ from other seeds' validation pairs instead halves the PIT error of
stopping at the last checkpoint ($0.034$ against $0.076$; $0.017$ against $0.037$ on the
mixture), but is no better than the same rule on the iteration count. Two limits: on
the 2-D problem $h_{\mathrm{eff}}$ predicts coverage, i.e.\ collapse, far better than
the PIT error ($-0.28$), and the on-atom fraction, not $h_{\mathrm{eff}}$, predicts
extraction ($0.95$).

\paragraph{Image scale.} The same measurement needs no posterior on CIFAR-10: the
bottom half of a test-split image is a draw from the true conditional law given its top
half. At the 42 archived checkpoints of a second training of the six $h\in\{4,5\}$ runs
of Section~\ref{sec:heff}, with 64 test images and $M{=}64$ draws each, we record per
hidden pixel whether the true value falls in the 5--95\% quantile interval of the
draws, together with the PIT error and the extraction rate
(\texttt{scripts/calib\_image\_checkpoints.py}). With $M{=}64$ that interval covers a
fresh draw with probability about $0.873$ in a continuous Gaussian calibration
control, rather than $0.90$. Unlike the tie-free rank reference below, this is not
a distribution-free guarantee for interpolated sample quantiles. Coverage starts above it ($0.91$--$0.95$ at
2k), falls to $0.67$--$0.78$ by 20k and partly recovers, to $0.72$--$0.81$ at 60k,
while extraction rises to $0.67$--$0.76$. $h_{\mathrm{eff}}$, which falls nearly
monotonically, cannot follow the recovery: it ranks coverage with Spearman $0.63$
($0.59$ given the iteration; $h_{\mathrm{eff}}/h$: $0.36$), against $0.98$ ($0.97$) for
the generated per-pixel standard deviation, which needs neither the training set nor a
reference. Extraction follows the iteration ($0.98$). At image scale the bandwidth
therefore locates the model relative to its training set, in its mean and total
variance, but does not predict its per-pixel calibration. The main text reports the
rank-based measurement below, which has an exact reference at finite $M$.

\paragraph{Several levels, components and joint regions.} A per-pixel interval cannot see
correlations between pixels. For all twelve runs ($h\in\{0,4,5,6\}$, 84 checkpoints) we
therefore also rank the true image among the $M{=}64$ draws
(\texttt{scripts/calib\_image\_levels.py}): per pixel and per principal component of the
hidden region at ten levels, and for one joint region per image in the span of the top 8
or 32 components (leave-one-out Mahalanobis rank). Under exchangeability and
absence of score ties, midpoint ranks have an exact reference at finite $M$,
$0.908$ at the 90\% level for both. The implementation uses deterministic midranks
when ties occur, for which this uniform-rank reference need not hold. Randomly
ordering tied blocks restores the discrete uniform reference under exchangeability;
the archived values below have not been recomputed with randomized ties. At 60k the per-pixel and per-component
90\% coverage is $0.77$--$0.87$ for $h>0$ ($0.06$--$0.08$ at $h{=}0$), but the joint region
covers only $0.59$--$0.78$ ($k{=}8$) and $0.47$--$0.77$ ($k{=}32$), and $0$ at $h{=}0$; its
mean gap over the ten levels exceeds the per-pixel one at all 84 checkpoints. The draws
are too concentrated jointly even where each pixel looks nearly calibrated. The ranking
result holds for every statistic: over the 63 checkpoints with $h>0$, a larger per-pixel
spread of the draws goes with a smaller gap, Spearman $|\rho|=0.85$--$0.93$
($0.83$--$0.92$ given the iteration), against $0.49$--$0.65$ for $h_{\mathrm{eff}}$
(\texttt{scripts/summarize\_calib\_levels.py}).

\paragraph{Why the joint region is worse.} The simplest account is a common shortfall in
spread. Draws from $\N(0,s^2I_k)$ against a truth from $\N(0,I_k)$ reproduce the measured
per-component coverage at $s\approx0.7$--$0.9$, and a modest shortfall in each direction
compounds in a $k$-dimensional region. We fit $s$ to the measured coverage of the top $k$
components at each of the 63 checkpoints with $h>0$, simulate the same rank statistics at
$M{=}64$, and predict the joint coverage with nothing else fitted
(\texttt{scripts/joint\_from\_marginal.py}). For $k{=}8$ the prediction holds, with
$R^2=0.81$ at the 90\% level ($0.97$ over the ten levels, mean absolute error $0.05$),
$0.51$--$0.78$ predicted at 60k against $0.59$--$0.78$ measured. For $k{=}32$ it ranks the
checkpoints (correlation $0.92$) but overstates the loss by $0.19$ on average, and a
separate $s_j$ for each component reduces that only to $0.16$, so in 32 directions the
shortfalls are not independent. In the top components, then, the joint gap is mostly a
uniform under-dispersion that per-pixel coverage reads as near nominal.

\subsection{A spectral-bias model of the effective bandwidth}
\label{app:specbias}

Proposition~\ref{prop:heffid} and Theorem~\ref{thm:calib} say when $h_{\mathrm{eff}}$ is
identified and what it implies, not why training produces it. We test the simplest
mechanism: a learner that resolves the label only gradually. We model the label
dependence of the trained field through the conditional moments
$f^\star(y)=\E[\phi(X)\mid\widetilde Y=y]$, $\phi(x)\in\{x,|x|^2\}$, which at bandwidth $h$
are the Nadaraya--Watson moments of Theorem~\ref{thm:endpoint}. This is a reduction: the
population field, whose endpoint law is Theorem~\ref{thm:endpoint}, depends on the whole weight vector
$p^{(h)}(y)$, which two moments do not determine. Suppose they are learned by kernel gradient flow in
$L^2(\rho_h)$, $\rho_h$ the law of $\widetilde Y$, from the unconditional moments
$c=\E\phi(X)$:
\begin{equation}
\partial_tf_t=-T_k(f_t-f^\star),\quad f_0=c,
\qquad\text{so}\qquad
f_t=c+(I-e^{-tT_k})(f^\star-c),
\label{eq:kgf}
\end{equation}
with $(T_kg)(y)=\E_{\rho_h}[k(y,Z)g(Z)]$. This is
the linearised dynamics of a network whose dependence on $y$ is governed by a fixed kernel $k$~\citep{jacot2018neural}. It learns larger-eigenvalue modes first. A Fourier low-frequency interpretation requires additional spectral assumptions; the model below is motivated by spectral-bias observations~\citep{rahaman2019spectral}.

\begin{proposition}[Resolution under kernel gradient flow]
\label{prop:specbias}
(a) Let $h=0$ and $k$ be strictly positive definite on the distinct labels
$\{y^i\}$. Then at every condition $y^j$, $f_t(y^j)\to f^\star(y^j)$ exponentially fast,
so the model's mean tends to $x^j$ and its variance to $0$: it reaches the reference
at $h$, with no floor. (b) Let $\rho$ be Lebesgue measure on $\R$ (a uniform label
density), let $k$ be stationary with spectral density $\hat k(\omega)=a|\omega|^{-\zeta}$,
$a,\zeta>0$, so that $T_k$ is the positive self-adjoint Fourier multiplier
$a|\omega|^{-\zeta}$ on $L^2(\R)$ (unbounded for every $\zeta>0$; since
$\int|\omega|^{-\zeta}d\omega$ diverges for every $\zeta>0$, no kernel with finite $k(y,y)$
has this spectrum, and $T_k$ is defined as the multiplier), and let $f^\star-c\in L^2(\R)$. Then $f_t-c$
is $f^\star-c$ filtered by $\hat\varphi_t(\omega)=G\big(\omega\,(at)^{-1/\zeta}\big)$,
$G(u)=1-e^{-|u|^{-\zeta}}$: a low-pass filter of fixed shape whose resolution scale is
$s(t)=(at)^{-1/\zeta}$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
(a) $\rho_0$ is uniform on $N$ points, so $L^2(\rho_0)\cong\R^N$ and $T_k$ is the matrix
$K/N$, $K_{ij}=k(y^i,y^j)$, with $K$ positive definite and smallest eigenvalue
$\lambda_{\min}>0$, and self-adjoint for the uniform weights;
hence $\|f_t-f^\star\|\le e^{-t\lambda_{\min}/N}\|f^\star-c\|$ in $\R^N$, which bounds
every coordinate. At $y^j$ the hard-conditioning moments are $f^\star(y^j)=(x^j,|x^j|^2)$,
so the mean tends to $x^j$ and the variance $|x^j|^2-|x^j|^2=0$.
(b) By functional calculus for the multiplier $T_k$, $e^{-tT_k}$ is the multiplier
$e^{-ta|\omega|^{-\zeta}}$ on $L^2(\R)$, so the solution of \eqref{eq:kgf}, in the
semigroup sense ($f^\star-c$ need not lie in the domain of $T_k$, and the equation holds
for $t>0$; it is unique since $\frac{d}{dt}\|w\|^2=-2\langle T_kw,w\rangle\le0$ for the
difference $w$ of two solutions), is
$f_t-c=(I-e^{-tT_k})(f^\star-c)$, with $I-e^{-tT_k}$ the multiplier
$1-e^{-ta|\omega|^{-\zeta}}=G(\omega(at)^{-1/\zeta})$, since
$|\omega(at)^{-1/\zeta}|^{-\zeta}=at|\omega|^{-\zeta}$.
\end{proof}

The spectral resolution scale does not identify a positive averaging kernel.
\begin{lemma}[The spectral filter is not a probability kernel]
\label{lem:nonpositive-filter}
For $a,\zeta>0$, the continuous function $G(0)=1$ and
$G(\omega)=1-e^{-a|\omega|^{-\zeta}}$ for $\omega\ne0$ is not the characteristic
function of any probability measure on $\R$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
If $G(\omega)=\int e^{i\omega x}\,d\mu(x)$ for a probability measure, fix
$\omega_n\ne0$ tending to zero. Fatou's lemma gives
\[
\frac12\int x^2\,d\mu\le\liminf_n
\int\frac{1-\cos(\omega_nx)}{\omega_n^2}\,d\mu
=\lim_n\frac{e^{-a|\omega_n|^{-\zeta}}}{\omega_n^2}=0.
\]
Thus $\mu=\delta_0$, contradicting $G(\omega)<1$ for nonzero $\omega$.
No second-moment assumption is needed.
\end{proof}
Consequently the continuum multiplier cannot be convolution by a probability
measure for all targets. This excludes an exact positive-kernel interpretation,
while permitting signed filters and approximate moment fits on finite conditions.

Part (b) is an idealisation in three ways. The label law is not uniform. $\hat k$ is a
power law only at high frequency (Laplace kernel: $\zeta=2$; Mat\'ern-$3/2$: $\zeta=4$;
Gaussian: exponential decay, $\zeta=\infty$). And the Nadaraya--Watson moments of a finite
training set satisfy $f^\star-c\in L^2(\R)$ only if all $x^i$ coincide: for $h>0$ they tend
to $\phi(x^{i_+})$ as $y\to+\infty$, where $y^{i_+}$ is the largest label, and for
$\phi(x)=x$ and $\phi(x)=|x|^2$ together this limit equals $c$ only in that case. Part (b) gives
the shape and the scale of the filter for a localised target, not the learned moments
themselves. We therefore compute \eqref{eq:kgf} exactly on the
actual EXP-1 training sets ($\rho_h$ by 15-point Gauss--Hermite quadrature around each
label, three seeds, $\ell\in\{0.3,1\}\times\mathrm{sd}(y)$) and apply the estimator
\eqref{eq:heff} at the 20 conditions (\texttt{theory\_t2\_resolution.py},
Figure~\ref{fig:t2}). Three findings. \emph{The model's moments admit a good single-bandwidth fit}: under the Laplace kernel the median log-space $R^2$ of the
fit is $0.97$ and $h_{\mathrm{mean}}$ and $h_{\mathrm{eff}}$ differ by a median $11\%$
(Mat\'ern: $0.95$, $9\%$), as for the trained networks. \emph{$h_{\mathrm{eff}}$ falls to
$h$}, in 6/6 Laplace and 5/6 Mat\'ern configurations, as (a) predicts for $h=0$.
\emph{The rate is set by the spectrum}: $s(t)$ falls with log-log slope $-0.40$
(Laplace; $-1/\zeta=-0.5$) and $-0.29$ (Mat\'ern; $-0.25$), while under the Gaussian
kernel it barely moves ($-0.05$) and $h_{\mathrm{eff}}$ never reaches $h$ (0/6).

\paragraph{From moments to the field.} The model above acts on moments. At the level of
the field, a learner that resolves the label only to the law $\varphi$ of a perturbation
$u$ replaces $\vstar$ by its label average $v_\varphi$, and Proposition~\ref{prop:filtered}
(Section~\ref{sec:heff}) says that this keeps the atoms.

\begin{proof}[Proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:filtered}]
\label{apx:pf-filtered}
(a) By Proposition~\ref{prop:kernel-field}, $\vstar(x,t,y')=\sum_iw_i(x,t,y')(x^i-x)/(1-t)$
with $w_i=a_ib_i/\sum_ja_jb_j$, $a_i=\pi_0(\frac{x-tx^i}{1-t})$, $b_i=K_h(y'-y^i)$;
averaging over $y'=y-u$ gives $\tilde w_i=\int w_i(x,t,y-u)\varphi(du)$, which is
nonnegative and sums to one. At $t=0$ all $a_i$ equal $\pi_0(x)$, so $w_i=p_i^{(h)}(y')$.
(b) Write $a_j=x^j$, set $c_j(u)=K_h(y-u-y^j)>0$, and use
$\phi=\varphi$. The index weights are
$q_j(x,t,u)\propto c_j(u)\exp(-|x-ta_j|^2/(2(1-t)^2))$; their averaged mean is
$m(x,t)=\int\sum_jq_j(x,t,u)a_j\,d\phi(u)$, and the flow is
$\dot x=(m-x)/(1-t)$. Set $\eps=1-t$. Below the integration variable for these coefficients is
denoted by $y'$; it is distinct from the fixed query $y$.

The original field is locally Lipschitz on compact intervals $t<1$, including
$t=0$: its spatial softmax derivatives are bounded independently of the
coefficients there. Since $m$ is in the convex hull of the atoms,
variation of constants gives existence on all of $[0,1)$ and a bounded trajectory.
We now prove convergence of this trajectory.

\emph{Convergence.}
Normalize $p_j(y')=c_j(y')/\sum_\ell c_\ell(y')$. Start at any $t_0>0$ and set
\[
\tau=-\log(1-t),\quad \alpha=\frac{\eps^2}{t},\quad
b_j=x\cdot a_j-\frac t2\lVert a_j\rVert^2,\quad R=\max_j\lVert a_j\rVert.
\]
From now on dots denote differentiation with respect to $\tau$, with
$t=1-e^{-\tau}$. Then $q_j\propto p_je^{b_j/\alpha}$ and $\dot x=m-x$,
which also gives a bounded $\tau$-speed. Define
\[
F=\int\alpha\log\sum_jp_j(y')e^{b_j/\alpha}\,d\phi(y'),\qquad
W=\tfrac12\lVert x\rVert^2-F+\tfrac12\eps R^2.
\]
The integrand lies between $\min b_j$ and $\max b_j$. Treating $t$ and $\alpha$
as independent arguments, differentiation gives
\[
\nabla_xF=m,\qquad \partial_\alpha F=-\int D_{\rm KL}(q\Vert p)\,d\phi,
\qquad \partial_tF=-\tfrac12\int\sum_jq_j\lVert a_j\rVert^2\,d\phi.
\]
These derivatives pass under the integral on compact positive-time intervals:
the spatial and time derivatives are bounded, and
\[
0\le D_{\rm KL}(q\Vert p)
=\frac{\sum_jq_jb_j}{\alpha}-\log\sum_jp_je^{b_j/\alpha}
\le\frac{\max_jb_j-\min_jb_j}{\alpha}
\]
is uniform in $y'$. Since $\dot\alpha<0$ and $\dot t=\eps$,
\[
\dot W=-\lVert\dot x\rVert^2+\dot\alpha\int D_{\rm KL}(q\Vert p)\,d\phi
+\frac\eps2\left(\int\sum_jq_j\lVert a_j\rVert^2\,d\phi-R^2\right)
\le-\lVert\dot x\rVert^2.
\]
Moreover $F\le\max_jb_j$, so
\[
W\ge\min_j\left\{\tfrac12\lVert x-a_j\rVert^2
+\tfrac\eps2(R^2-\lVert a_j\rVert^2)\right\}\ge0.
\]
Thus $\int_{\tau_0}^\infty\lVert\dot x\rVert^2\,d\tau<\infty$.

\emph{A finite set of possible limits.}
If $x_n\to z$ and $\tau_n\to\infty$, every weight on an atom outside $N(z)$
tends to zero for each $y'$, by the positive distance gap and the fixed positive
coefficient ratios. Bounded convergence gives the same conclusion after
averaging over $y'$. Every limit of $m(x_n,t_n)$ therefore lies in
$\operatorname{conv}N(z)$.

Let $C=\{z:z\in\operatorname{conv}N(z)\}$. This set is finite. For each
nonempty subset $S$ of atoms, there is at most one point in
$\operatorname{aff}S$ equidistant from all atoms of $S$: the difference of two
such points lies both in $\operatorname{span}(S-S)$ and its orthogonal
complement. A point of $C$ is one of these finitely many candidates.
On any compact set disjoint from $C$, the speed is eventually bounded below
by a positive constant. Otherwise a sequence of points there with speed tending
to zero would, by compactness and the preceding concentration argument, have
a limit in $C$.

An omega-limit point outside $C$ admits a closed ball disjoint from $C$.
Infinitely many visits to its inner half force residence for a fixed positive
time in the ball, because the speed is bounded above. If the trajectory remains
in the ball forever, the eventual positive lower speed bound already contradicts
finite energy. Otherwise choose disjoint visits; each spends a fixed positive
amount of energy, again a contradiction. Hence the omega-limit set is contained
in $C$. It is nonempty, compact, and connected, as the intersection of the nested
closures of the bounded connected trajectory tails. Being contained in a finite
set, it is a singleton.

\emph{Atomic endpoints almost surely.}
We may assume the atoms distinct, summing coefficients at repeated locations.
Use the $\tau$ variables above and write $\beta=1/\alpha$.
For a point $r$ in the closed probability simplex, define
\[
Q_j(y',r)=\frac{p_j(y')r_j}{\sum_\ell p_\ell(y')r_\ell},\quad
M(r)=\int\sum_jQ_j(y',r)a_j\,d\phi,\quad
V(r)=\int\operatorname{tr}\Cov_{Q(y',r)}(a)\,d\phi.
\]
All denominators are positive for each $y'$ outside one fixed null set.
The integrands are continuous in $r$ and uniformly bounded. Dominated
convergence makes $M,V$ continuous on the closed simplex. Positivity of the
coefficients and distinctness of the atoms imply that $V(r)=0$ exactly at its
vertices. Compactness therefore gives, for each $\delta>0$, a constant
$\kappa_\delta>0$ such that
\[
\dist(M(r),A)\ge\delta\quad\Longrightarrow\quad V(r)\ge\kappa_\delta,
\qquad A=\{a_1,\ldots,a_N\},
\]
whenever the set on the left is nonempty.
For the actual unconditional softmax vector $r_j\propto e^{b_j/\alpha}$,
the averaged $\tau$-field $f=m-x$ has divergence
\[
\operatorname{div}f=\beta V(r)-d.
\]

Consider a trajectory converging to a non-atom $z$, with $\rho=\dist(z,A)>0$.
Eventually $\lVert x-z\rVert<\rho/4$. At good times
$\lVert\dot x\rVert\le\rho/4$, the identity $m=x+\dot x$ gives
$\dist(m,A)\ge\rho/2$, hence $V\ge\kappa_{\rho/2}$.
The complementary times have finite measure by the finite energy proved above.
Their measure in the last unit interval therefore tends to zero. Good times
thus occupy at least half of every sufficiently late unit interval. Since
$V\ge0$ everywhere and $\beta(\tau)=(1-e^{-\tau})e^{2\tau}$,
\[
\int_{\tau_0}^\tau\operatorname{div}f(x(s),s)\,ds
\ge\kappa_{\rho/2}\int_{\mathrm{good}\cap[\tau-1,\tau]}\beta(s)\,ds
-d(\tau-\tau_0)\longrightarrow+\infty.
\]

The finite-time flow maps $F_\tau=F_{\tau,\tau_0}$ from $\tau_0>0$ are $C^1$
diffeomorphisms: spatial derivatives are locally uniformly bounded, and linear
growth permits both forward and backward solutions on finite intervals.
Liouville's formula gives
\[
J_\tau(\xi):=\det DF_\tau(\xi)
=\exp\!\left(\int_{\tau_0}^\tau
\operatorname{div}f(F_s(\xi),s)\,ds\right)>0.
\]
Thus $J_\tau\to\infty$ at every non-atom-convergent initial point.
For a bounded initial ball $K$, boundedness of $m$ places all $F_\tau(K)$
in one fixed ball $B$. Let $E\subset K$ be the non-atom basin; it is measurable,
since the limit map is a pointwise limit of continuous flow maps. Injectivity
and change of variables give $\int_EJ_\tau\,dx\le\operatorname{Leb}(B)$.
Fatou's lemma along integer times forces $\operatorname{Leb}(E)=0$.
Exhaust the initial space by bounded balls and take the preimage of the null
set under the finite-time diffeomorphism from $t=0$ to $t(\tau_0)$. Its inverse
is locally Lipschitz and preserves Lebesgue-null sets. Absolute continuity
of the source proves the assertion.
The limit map is measurable, and bounded convergence for continuous bounded test
functions identifies the weak endpoint law as the stated finite mixture.
\end{proof}

So a learner whose only defect is label resolution reweights the training atoms and
has an atomic limiting law almost surely. The $38\%$ off-atom fraction measured on
CIFAR-10 at $h{=}6$ is a finite-time numerical observation; solver and truncation
convergence are needed before attributing it to learned spatial error. Integrating the flow of $v_\varphi$ exactly
on the EXP-1 training set (Gaussian $\varphi$ of scale $s$, five conditions, 300
trajectories each; \texttt{theory\_t2\_filtered.py}) gives trajectories approaching atoms at the evaluated cutoff (distance $\le0.0032$). The endpoint weights are \emph{not} simply the label-averaged
weights $q$: they agree within Monte Carlo error when $s\le h$ (total variation $0.11$
and $0.14$ against floors $0.09$ and $0.13$ at $s=h=0.05$ and $0.1$), but for $s\gg h$
the flow re-concentrates them, halving the endpoint variance ($0.46$ against $1.14$ at
$h=0.01$, $s=0.05$). A label-resolution limit is therefore not equivalent to extra label
noise at the level of the field, and the additive law has no reason to be exact.

The model leaves two things open. Its additive exponent is $p\approx2.2$ (Laplace; $2.8$
Mat\'ern; $2.8$ for the label-averaged weights $q$), against $p=1.3$ fitted to the networks, but the network data hardly
distinguish the two (log RMSE $0.352$ at $p=1.3$ against $0.366$ at $p=2$). And it
predicts no floor, whereas $h_{\mathrm{eff}}$ settles at $1.08h$ on the synthetic runs;
whether that gap is optimisation (Adam, a finite learning rate) or feature learning,
which kernel gradient flow excludes, is not settled here. Nor does kernel time map to
iterations outside the lazy regime, so we do not compare rates with training curves.

\paragraph{Three tests narrow this down.} On the synthetic runs:
(i) Training is not lazy. The empirical tangent kernel of the trained field in its label
input (256 labels, 64 draws of $(x,t)$) changes between 100 and $2\times10^5$ iterations by
$0.68$--$1.23$ in normalised Frobenius norm for $h\le0.1$ ($0.09$--$0.38$ at $h=0.5$), so a
fixed-kernel account of $h_{\mathrm{eff}}$ does not apply. Its eigenvalues do end with a
power-law decay, with exponent $1.10$--$1.29$ at $h\le0.01$ against $2.09$ for the Laplace
kernel (\texttt{scripts/ntk\_label\_spectrum.py}).
(ii) The tested regularisation and step-size changes did not remove the measured gap above $h$. At
$2\times10^5$ iterations, $h_{\mathrm{eff}}$ is $0.86$--$1.22$ times its baseline under AdamW
weight decay $10^{-4}$--$10^{-2}$, a learning rate of $3\times10^{-4}$ or a cosine schedule
($h\in\{0,0.05\}$, three seeds per arm, ranges overlapping in every arm), and at $h=0.05$ it
sits at $1.01$--$1.18h$ in all of them; a learning rate of $3\times10^{-3}$ diverged in 4 of
6 runs (\texttt{scripts/e2\_floor\_compare.py}).
(iii) A density-adaptive bandwidth is not established. Regressing the log per-condition
bandwidth on the log label density at $h=0$ gives a pooled slope of $-0.35$
($[-0.52,-0.16]$), but the per-run intervals contain $0$ at 5 of the 6 checkpoints from
$10^5$ iterations, and at $h\in\{0.01,0.05,0.1\}$ the slopes are zero or positive
(\texttt{scripts/heff\_local\_density.py}). The paper therefore fits one bandwidth for all
conditions.



\subsection{An audit protocol for conditional flows}
\label{app:audit}

The diagnostics used throughout Section~\ref{sec:experiments} are collected below as
one procedure. It needs the training set and samples from the trained model, and no
retraining; the reference costs $O(CN(k+d))$ for $C$ conditions.

\begin{center}
\fbox{\begin{minipage}{0.94\textwidth}\small
\textbf{Audit.} \emph{Input:} training set $\D=\{(x^i,y^i)\}_{i=1}^N$; the bandwidth $h$
used in training ($h=0$ for hard conditioning); a trained sampler; evaluation conditions
$y_1,\dots,y_C$; $M$ samples per condition.
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=1.6em,itemsep=1pt,topsep=3pt]
\item \emph{Reference, per condition.} Weights
  $p_i^{(h)}(y_c)=K_h(y_c-y^i)/\sum_jK_h(y_c-y^j)$, computed in log space
  (at $h=0$ and $y_c=y^i$, $p^{(h)}=e_i$); effective sample size
  $n_{\mathrm{eff}}=1/\sum_i(p_i^{(h)})^2$; reference mean $\bar x_h(y_c)$ and trace
  $\tr\Covh(y_c)$ (Proposition~\ref{prop:moments}).
\item \emph{Measurement, per condition.} Draw $M$ samples and compute the mean $m_c$
  and covariance trace $T_c$ as in Section~\ref{sec:heff}. Record the on-atom fraction:
  the share of samples $\hat x$ with
  $\|\hat x-x^{(1)}\|^2\le\frac19\|\hat x-x^{(2)}\|^2$, where $x^{(1)},x^{(2)}$ are the two
  nearest training points \citep{yoon2023memorization}.
\item \emph{Level.} Report the aggregate ratio $\sum_c\tr\Cov/\sum_c\tr\Covh$ and the
  median per-condition ratio with a bootstrap interval. Never average per-condition
  ratios: their denominators can be arbitrarily small (Table~\ref{tab:seed3}).
\item \emph{Range.} Report how many decades $\log_{10}\tr\Covh$ spans across conditions.
  With little range, agreement in level is uninformative: at $0.74$ decades (CIFAR-10,
  $h{=}6$) the median ratio is $1.15$ while the slope has the wrong sign and $R^2=0.135$.
\item \emph{Tracking.} Fit $\beta$, the OLS slope of $\log\tr\Cov$ on $\log\tr\Covh$
  across conditions, with its standard error and $R^2$; the population optimum gives
  $\beta=1$.
\item \emph{Bandwidth.} Fit $h_{\mathrm{eff}}$ and $h_{\mathrm{mean}}$ by~\eqref{eq:heff},
  with the condition bootstrap of Section~\ref{sec:heff}. The synthetic fits use 240
  log-spaced bandwidths from $0.003$ to $5$; the CIFAR-10 fits use 160 from $0.5$ to $40$.
  Both grids contain positive bandwidths even for models trained at $h=0$.
  CIFAR-10 traces are floored at $10^{-12}$ before taking logarithms.
\item \emph{Calibration.} Where held-out pairs exist, rank the true value among the $M$
  draws per coordinate and jointly in the top principal components
  (\texttt{src/metrics/calibration.py}); ranks have an exact reference at finite $M$ when ties are absent or broken at random.
  Report the joint coverage as well as the per-coordinate one.
\end{enumerate}
\emph{Sizes.} $M\ge128$ and $C\ge48$, the smallest settings behind the CIFAR-10 numbers
here: under a Gaussian sampling approximation, the covariance trace has relative
standard error at most $\sqrt{2/(M-1)}$ ($12.5\%$ at $M{=}128$).
This is not a distribution-free bound and depends on fourth moments beyond Gaussian laws; and the $M{=}16$, $C{=}8$ training-time estimator was off by up to
$15\%$.\\
\emph{Reading.} $\beta\approx1$ with aggregate ratio $\approx1$ indicates agreement
of trace levels and their variation across conditions; it does not certify proximity
of fields or laws. $h_{\mathrm{eff}}\approx h_{\mathrm{mean}}$ means that one reference
bandwidth fits the means and traces. $h_{\mathrm{eff}}/h>1$ means that the fitted
reference is broader than the training-label kernel, not that the network law is
exactly that mixture. An on-atom
fraction near $1$ means that samples meet the stated relative nearest-neighbour
criterion, not exact support membership. $\tr\Cov\approx\tr\Sigmapost$ on its
own certifies nothing, since for a suitable $h$ it holds for an atomic law
(Section~\ref{sec:p7}).
\end{minipage}}
\end{center}

\subsection{Material deferred from Section~\ref{sec:experiments}}
\label{app:exp4}

This subsection gives the full measurements behind the conclusions quoted in
Section~\ref{sec:experiments}. Sanity checks for EXP-1 pass before any
measurement is taken: the analytic posterior satisfies the normal equations to
$1.8\times10^{-15}$, closed-form field integration maps every $x_0\to x^i$, and the
unconditional model does not leak $y$.

\paragraph{The collapse, checkpoint by checkpoint.} Figure~\ref{fig:traintraj} is
Figure~\ref{fig:mechanism}(b) as it forms: the same condition, the same $160$ draws of
$x_0$, integrated through five checkpoints of one run and then through the exact
population field. The generated trace falls $1.19\to1.16\to0.92\to0.87\to0.13$ against
the posterior's $1.00$ while the number of distinct atoms reached falls $72\to3$. The final panel is an exact-reference comparison, not an observed training limit.
Figure~\ref{fig:mechanismreal} adds the view a state-space panel cannot give: the same
population laws projected onto the posterior's principal axis against its density,
where the variance-matched middle law is a forest of spikes spanning the right range.

\centering
